Exam focus
  • Define LAN, MAN and WAN with one example each, and draw the comparison table.
  • Know the two kinds of WAN: point-to-point and switched.
  • Be able to explain why connecting LANs and WANs produces an internetwork.
Common mistakes
  • Classifying by speed alone. The defining feature is the geographical area covered; speed and ownership follow from it.
  • Calling Wi-Fi a WAN because it is wireless. Wi-Fi is a wireless LAN; cellular 4G/5G is wide-area wireless access.
Quick revision
  • PAN (metres, Bluetooth) < LAN (building/campus, private, fast) < MAN (city) < WAN (country/world, leased, slower, more errors).
  • WANs are point-to-point or switched.
  • Connected networks form an internetwork; the Internet is the global one using TCP/IP.

I.6 · Foundations

Delay, loss and throughput

The four delays a packet suffers at every node, how they add up end to end, when packets are lost, and how fast a file can really be delivered. The most examined numerical topic in your papers.

Sources: Kurose §1.4 · Chapter_1_v8.2 slides 44–56 · Chapter 1 Problems

Why delay matters

Ideally the Internet would move any amount of data between any two hosts instantly and without loss. Physics makes that impossible. Every packet is delayed at every node, packets can be lost, and the rate at which data arrives is limited. To design networks, and to answer most numerical questions in this course, you must know exactly where the time goes.

The four sources of nodal delay

Follow one packet as it passes through router A on its way to router B. The packet suffers four delays at A.

1. ProcessingRouter A examines the header to decide where to send the packet and checks for bit errors.
2. QueuingThe packet waits in A's output buffer until the packets ahead of it have been sent.
3. TransmissionA pushes all the bits of the packet onto the link, one after another.
4. PropagationEach bit travels along the link from A to B at the speed of the medium.

Processing delay, \(d_{\text{proc}}\)

The time to examine the packet's header, look up the forwarding table, and check for bit errors. In high-speed routers this is typically microseconds or less.

Queuing delay, \(d_{\text{queue}}\)

The time the packet waits in the output queue. It is zero if the queue is empty and the link is idle, and large if many packets are already waiting. It depends on how much traffic has arrived earlier, so it varies from packet to packet: from microseconds to milliseconds in practice.

Transmission delay, \(d_{\text{trans}}\)

The time to push all the packet's bits onto the link. A packet of \(L\) bits on a link of transmission rate \(R\) bits/s takes

\[ d_{\text{trans}} = \frac{L}{R} \]

For a 1,500-byte packet on a 10 Mbps Ethernet: \(12{,}000 / 10^7 = 1.2\) ms. On a 1 Gbps link: 12 µs. It depends on the packet length and the link rate, and not on the distance.

Propagation delay, \(d_{\text{prop}}\)

Once a bit is pushed onto the link, it must travel to router B. The propagation speed \(s\) depends on the medium: about \(2 \times 10^8\) m/s in copper and fibre and \(3 \times 10^8\) m/s in free space. Over a link of length \(d\),

\[ d_{\text{prop}} = \frac{d}{s} \]

It depends on the distance and the medium, and not on the packet length or the link rate. In a campus LAN it is microseconds; across a continent it is tens of milliseconds.

Total nodal delay

\[ d_{\text{nodal}} = d_{\text{proc}} + d_{\text{queue}} + d_{\text{trans}} + d_{\text{prop}} \]

Transmission delay versus propagation delay

These two are the most commonly confused ideas in the subject.

Transmission delayPropagation delay
What it measuresTime to push the whole packet onto the linkTime for one bit to travel along the link
Formula\(L / R\)\(d / s\)
Depends onPacket length \(L\), link rate \(R\)Distance \(d\), propagation speed \(s\)
Independent ofDistancePacket length, link rate
Typical sizeµs to msµs (LAN) to hundreds of ms (satellite)
Reduce it byA faster link or a smaller packetA shorter path
The caravan of cars (from your slides)

A caravan of 10 cars drives along a highway with a tollbooth every 100 km. Cars travel at 100 km/h. Each tollbooth serves one car every 12 seconds. The caravan must stay together, so it waits at a tollbooth until all 10 cars have been served.

  • Serving all 10 cars at a tollbooth takes \(10 \times 12 = 120\) s = 2 min. This is the transmission delay: the cars are bits, the tollbooth is the router pushing them onto the link.
  • Driving 100 km to the next tollbooth takes 1 hour. This is the propagation delay.
  • So the caravan reaches the next tollbooth 62 minutes after it began being served at this one.

Now change the numbers: cars travel at 1,000 km/h and the tollbooth takes 1 minute per car. Serving 10 cars takes 10 minutes, but driving 100 km takes only 6 minutes. The first car reaches the next tollbooth while 3 cars are still queued at the first one. In networks, too, the first bits of a packet can arrive at the next router while the rest are still being transmitted.

Interactive visualizationDelay and store-and-forward in motionSee transmission and propagation as separate phases on each link, with your own packet size, rates and distances.
Open simulator →

Queuing delay and traffic intensity

Queuing delay is the most complicated of the four, because it changes from packet to packet. Let

  • \(a\) = the average rate at which packets arrive at the queue (packets/s),
  • \(L\) = the packet length (bits), and
  • \(R\) = the transmission rate (bits/s).

Bits arrive at an average rate of \(La\) bits/s and leave at \(R\) bits/s.

Traffic intensity

\[ I = \frac{L a}{R} \]

  • If \(I > 1\), bits arrive faster than they can be sent. The queue grows without limit and the queuing delay approaches infinity. Rule one of traffic engineering: design the system so that the traffic intensity is no greater than 1.
  • If \(I \le 1\), the delay depends on how the traffic arrives. If one packet arrives exactly every \(L/R\) seconds, there is never a queue. If packets arrive in bursts, the later packets in a burst wait.
  • As \(I\) approaches 1, the average queuing delay rises very steeply. A small increase in traffic near full load produces a large increase in delay.

Packet loss

A queue has finite capacity. When a packet arrives to find the queue full, the router drops it: the packet is lost. The fraction of lost packets rises as the traffic intensity rises. A lost packet may be retransmitted by the source (TCP does this, Unit II), or simply never arrive (UDP). Performance at a node is therefore measured both by delay and by the probability of packet loss.

End-to-end delay

Suppose there are \(N - 1\) routers between source and destination (so \(N\) links), the network is uncongested (no queuing), and every router and link is the same. Then

End-to-end delay across N identical links

\[ d_{\text{end-end}} = N\,(d_{\text{proc}} + d_{\text{trans}} + d_{\text{prop}}) \]

When links differ, add each link's \(L/R_i + d_i/s_i\), and add \(d_{\text{proc}}\) once for each packet switch.

Traceroute

Traceroute is a program that shows the end-to-end path and measures the delay to each router. For each of the \(N - 1\) routers on the path, the source sends three special packets marked for that router. Each router returns a short message to the source, which records the round-trip time. Because traceroute sends three packets per router, you see three delays per hop; they differ because queuing delay varies. Sometimes a later router shows a smaller delay than an earlier one, simply because its packets met less queuing.

Throughput

Instantaneous throughput is the rate (bits/s) at which a host is receiving a file at a given moment. If a file of \(F\) bits takes \(T\) seconds to arrive, the average throughput is \(F/T\) bits/s.

For a path from a server (link rate \(R_s\)) through one router to a client (link rate \(R_c\)), with no other traffic, the throughput is the rate of the slower link: the bottleneck link.

Throughput of a path

\[ \text{throughput} = \min(R_1, R_2, \dots, R_N) \qquad \text{transfer time} \approx \frac{F}{\min(R_1, \dots, R_N)} \]

If a link of rate \(R\) is shared equally by \(k\) flows, each flow sees \(R/k\) on it, so the throughput is \(\min(R_s, R_c, R/k)\).

In today's Internet the core is heavily over-provisioned, so the bottleneck is usually the access network at the edge (your Wi-Fi or your ISP connection).

Throughput is not bandwidth

Bandwidth (link rate) is what one link can carry. Throughput is what you actually get end to end, after the bottleneck and sharing. A 1 Gbps Ethernet port behind a 50 Mbps ISP connection gives about 50 Mbps of throughput from the Internet.

Throughput is not delay

Throughput is a rate (bits per second); delay is a time (seconds). A satellite link can have high throughput and huge delay. A LAN can have low delay and modest throughput.

Bandwidth-delay product

The number of bits that can be “in flight” on a link at once is

\[ \text{bits in the link} = R \times d_{\text{prop}} \]

On a 2 Mbps link 20,000 km long with \(s = 2.5 \times 10^8\) m/s, \(d_{\text{prop}} = 0.08\) s, so up to \(2 \times 10^6 \times 0.08 = 160{,}000\) bits are in the link at once. The length of one bit in metres is \(s/R\): here \(2.5 \times 10^8 / 2 \times 10^6 = 125\) m.

Worked numericals

Level 1One packet, one link (Kurose problem, in Chapter 1 Problems)
Given

\(L = 1{,}000\) bytes, link length \(d = 2{,}500\) km, \(s = 2.5 \times 10^8\) m/s, \(R = 2\) Mbps.

Required

The time for the packet to reach the other end, and whether it depends on \(L\) and \(R\).

Formula

\(d_{\text{trans}} = L/R\), \(d_{\text{prop}} = d/s\), total \(= d_{\text{trans}} + d_{\text{prop}}\).

Substitution

\(L = 1{,}000 \times 8 = 8{,}000\) bits. \(d_{\text{trans}} = 8{,}000 / (2 \times 10^6)\). \(d_{\text{prop}} = 2.5 \times 10^6 \text{ m} / 2.5 \times 10^8\).

Calculation

\(d_{\text{trans}} = 0.004\) s = 4 ms. \(d_{\text{prop}} = 0.01\) s = 10 ms. Total = 14 ms.

Final answer

14 ms in total. The propagation part (10 ms) does not depend on the packet length or the transmission rate; only the transmission part (4 ms) does.

From a paperA point-to-point link 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q1
Given

\(R = 10\) Mbps, \(d_{\text{prop}} = 1\) µs, \(L = 100\) bytes.

Required

The time from the first bit leaving the sender until the receiver has the whole packet, in µs.

Formula

\(t = L/R + d_{\text{prop}}\) (the last bit leaves at \(L/R\) and arrives \(d_{\text{prop}}\) later).

Substitution

\(L = 100 \times 8 = 800\) bits; \(L/R = 800 / 10^7\).

Calculation

\(L/R = 8 \times 10^{-5}\) s = 80 µs. \(t = 80 + 1 = 81\) µs.

Final answer

81 µs. The four delay components the question also asks you to list are processing, queuing, transmission and propagation.

From a paperThree links, two switches, and which parameter matters 23CSE302 End 2025 Q5

A packet of length \(L\) crosses three links joined by two store-and-forward switches; each switch adds processing delay \(d_{\text{proc}}\); queuing is ignored.

Given

\(L = 1{,}200\) bytes; \(s_1 = s_2 = s_3 = 2.0 \times 10^8\) m/s; \(R_1 = R_2 = R_3 = 5\) Mbps; \(d_{\text{proc}} = 2\) ms; \(d_1 = 4{,}000\) km, \(d_2 = 3{,}000\) km, \(d_3 = 1{,}000\) km.

Required

The end-to-end delay in ms, and which parameter has the largest effect.

Formula

\( d_{\text{end-end}} = \sum_{i=1}^{3}\left(\frac{L}{R_i} + \frac{d_i}{s_i}\right) + 2\,d_{\text{proc}} \)

Substitution

\(L = 1{,}200 \times 8 = 9{,}600\) bits. \(L/R_i = 9{,}600 / (5 \times 10^6)\). \(d_1/s = 4 \times 10^6 / 2 \times 10^8\), \(d_2/s = 3 \times 10^6 / 2 \times 10^8\), \(d_3/s = 1 \times 10^6 / 2 \times 10^8\).

Calculation

Transmission: \(L/R = 1.92\) ms per link, \(3 \times 1.92 = 5.76\) ms. Propagation: \(20 + 15 + 5 = 40\) ms. Processing: \(2 \times 2 = 4\) ms. Total \(= 5.76 + 40 + 4 = 49.76\) ms.

Final answer

About 49.76 ms. Propagation is 40 / 49.76 = 80.4% of the total, transmission 11.6%, processing 8.0%. Propagation dominates because the links are thousands of kilometres long while the packet is small. Doubling every link rate would save only 2.88 ms; halving the distances would save 20 ms. So the link lengths (and the medium's speed) have by far the largest effect here.

From a paperMessage segmentation 19CCE304 Mid 2023 Q2 19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q5

A message of \(8 \times 10^6\) bits goes from a source to a destination through two store-and-forward switches (three links), every link at 2 Mbps. Propagation, queuing and processing are ignored.

Given

\(F = 8 \times 10^6\) bits; \(N = 3\) links; \(R = 2\) Mbps. With segmentation: \(P = 800\) packets of \(L = 10{,}000\) bits.

Required

(a) total time without segmentation; (b) time for the first packet to reach the first switch, and when the second packet is fully at the first switch; (c) total time with segmentation.

Formula

(a) \(N \cdot F/R\). (b) \(L/R\) and \(2L/R\). (c) \((N + P - 1) \cdot L/R\).

Substitution

\(F/R = 8 \times 10^6 / 2 \times 10^6\); \(L/R = 10{,}000 / 2 \times 10^6\).

Calculation

(a) \(F/R = 4\) s per link, so \(3 \times 4 = 12\) s. (b) \(L/R = 5\) ms; the second packet is fully at the first switch at \(2 \times 5 = 10\) ms. (c) \((3 + 800 - 1) \times 5\text{ ms} = 802 \times 5 = 4{,}010\) ms.

Final answer

(a) 12 s. (b) 5 ms and 10 ms. (c) 4.01 s, about three times faster, because the three links work in parallel like a pipeline. Other reasons to segment: a bit error forces retransmission of one small packet instead of the whole message, and a huge message doesn't block other users' packets at a switch. Drawbacks: every packet carries a header (overhead), and packets must be numbered and reassembled at the destination.

From a paperThroughput and transfer time 19CCE304 Mid 2023 Q1 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q11(a)
Given

Three links with \(R_1 = 500\) kbps, \(R_2 = 2\) Mbps, \(R_3 = 1\) Mbps; file = 4 million bytes. In part (c), \(R_2 = 100\) kbps.

Required

The throughput and the approximate transfer time, before and after \(R_2\) drops.

Formula

Throughput \(= \min(R_1, R_2, R_3)\); time \(\approx F / \text{throughput}\).

Substitution

\(F = 4 \times 10^6 \times 8 = 32 \times 10^6\) bits.

Calculation

Original: \(\min(500\text{k}, 2\text{M}, 1\text{M}) = 500\) kbps; time \(= 32 \times 10^6 / 5 \times 10^5 = 64\) s. With \(R_2 = 100\) kbps: throughput = 100 kbps; time \(= 32 \times 10^6 / 10^5 = 320\) s.

Final answer

500 kbps and 64 s; then 100 kbps and 320 s (5 min 20 s). The slowest link always sets the pace. (The 19CSE301 paper wrote “500 KBps”; if that really means kilobytes per second, the bottleneck becomes 1 Mbps on link 3 and the time 32 s. State which reading you use.)

From a paperStop-and-wait over a long link 19CCE304 End 2023 Q1(A)

Hosts A and B are 20,000 km apart on a direct 2 Mbps link with \(s = 2.5 \times 10^8\) m/s. The file is 800,000 bits (the question splits it into 20 packets of 40,000 bits).

Given

\(d = 2 \times 10^7\) m, \(R = 2 \times 10^6\) bps, \(s = 2.5 \times 10^8\) m/s, \(F = 800{,}000\) bits; in (b) 20 packets of 40,000 bits, each acknowledged before the next is sent, ACK transmission time negligible.

Required

(a) time to send the file continuously; (b) time with stop-and-wait per packet; (c) comparison.

Formula

(a) \(F/R + d/s\). (b) per packet \(L/R + 2\,d/s\) (data out, ACK back), times 20.

Substitution

\(d/s = 2 \times 10^7 / 2.5 \times 10^8 = 0.08\) s. (a) \(F/R = 800{,}000 / 2 \times 10^6\). (b) \(L/R = 40{,}000 / 2 \times 10^6\).

Calculation

(a) \(0.4 + 0.08 = 0.48\) s. (b) \(L/R = 0.02\) s; per packet \(0.02 + 0.16 = 0.18\) s; \(20 \times 0.18 = 3.6\) s.

Final answer

(a) 0.48 s. (b) 3.6 s. (c) Waiting for an acknowledgement after every packet wastes 0.16 s of round trip each time, so stop-and-wait is 7.5 times slower on a long link. This is exactly why TCP sends many packets before waiting (pipelining, Unit II).

From a paperWhen does propagation equal transmission? 19CCE304 End 2024 Q1(A)
Given

\(s = 2.5 \times 10^8\) m/s, \(L = 120\) bits, \(R = 56\) kbps.

Required

The distance \(m\) at which \(d_{\text{prop}} = d_{\text{trans}}\). Also, where is the first bit at \(t = d_{\text{trans}}\)?

Formula

\(m/s = L/R \Rightarrow m = s \cdot L/R\).

Substitution

\(L/R = 120 / 56{,}000\).

Calculation

\(L/R = 2.143 \times 10^{-3}\) s. \(m = 2.5 \times 10^8 \times 2.143 \times 10^{-3} = 535{,}714\) m.

Final answer

About 536 km. At \(t = d_{\text{trans}}\) the last bit has just left host A. If \(d_{\text{prop}} > d_{\text{trans}}\), the first bit is still on the link, \(s \cdot d_{\text{trans}}\) metres from A. If \(d_{\text{prop}} < d_{\text{trans}}\), the first bit has already reached B.

Exam focus
  • Name and define all four delays, with what each depends on. This appears as a definition question in many papers (19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q1, 20CYS303 Mid 2023 Q1(e,f)).
  • End-to-end delay over several links with different lengths: 23CSE302 End 2025 Q5 gave exactly this and asked for analysis. Always show the percentage split.
  • Message segmentation and \((N + P - 1) L/R\) appeared in four papers.
  • Throughput = bottleneck link. Divide a shared link's rate by the number of flows.
  • 20CYS303 End 2025 Q7 asked how bandwidth, latency and throughput relate. Bandwidth is capacity, latency is delay, throughput is the achieved rate; high bandwidth doesn't guarantee high throughput if there is a bottleneck or loss, and doesn't reduce propagation latency.
Common mistakes
  • Forgetting to convert bytes to bits (× 8). Then every transmission delay is 8 times too small.
  • Mixing km and m in \(d/s\). Convert distance to metres when \(s\) is in m/s.
  • Counting processing delay at the hosts. In these questions \(d_{\text{proc}}\) is added once per packet switch.
  • Adding transmission delay once for the whole path. With store-and-forward each link adds its own \(L/R\).
  • Writing throughput as the sum or the average of link rates. It is the minimum.
Quick revision
  • \(d_{\text{nodal}} = d_{\text{proc}} + d_{\text{queue}} + d_{\text{trans}} + d_{\text{prop}}\); \(d_{\text{trans}} = L/R\); \(d_{\text{prop}} = d/s\).
  • Traffic intensity \(La/R\); keep it ≤ 1; delay explodes near 1; loss when buffers fill.
  • End-to-end, N identical links: \(N(d_{\text{proc}} + L/R + d/s)\). P packets: \((N + P - 1)L/R\) plus propagation.
  • Throughput = \(\min(R_i)\); a link shared by \(k\) flows gives \(R/k\) each.
  • Bits in flight = \(R \times d_{\text{prop}}\); width of a bit = \(s/R\).

I.7 · Foundations

Protocol layers and service models

Why the Internet's work is split into five layers, what each layer does, how a message is wrapped in headers on the way down (encapsulation), and how TCP/IP compares with OSI.

Sources: Kurose §1.5 · Chapter_1_v8.2 slides 62–72, 80–82

Why organise protocols into layers?

The Internet is extremely complicated: many applications, protocols, end systems, packet switches and kinds of link. To make sense of it, designers organise protocols into layers. Each layer:

  1. provides a service to the layer above it (its service model), by
  2. performing certain actions within the layer, and
  3. using the services of the layer directly below it.
Air travel

Buying a ticket, checking baggage, going to the gate, take-off, flying the route, landing, leaving the gate, collecting baggage and complaining about the ticket. Each step is a layer: the baggage layer delivers bags from check-in to the carousel, using the gate layer, which uses the runway layer, which uses the aeroplane routing layer. A change at one layer (a new boarding procedure) doesn't affect the others. Networks are layered for the same reason.

Benefits of layeringDrawbacks of layering
Modularity: a complex system is broken into parts that can be designed and discussed separatelyLayers may duplicate work (for example, error recovery both per link and end to end)
A layer's implementation can change without affecting the rest, as long as its service stays the sameA layer sometimes needs information that only another layer has (for example, a timestamp), which breaks the separation
Each layer has a clear job, so protocols are easier to standardiseExtra headers at every layer add overhead

The five-layer Internet protocol stack

Taken together, the protocols of the layers are called the protocol stack. The Internet's stack has five layers.

ApplicationSupports network applications. Protocols: HTTP, SMTP, IMAP, DNS, FTP. A packet of information here is a message.
TransportCarries application messages between the two end points (processes). Protocols: TCP (reliable, connection-oriented, flow and congestion control) and UDP (connectionless, no frills). Its packet is a segment.
NetworkMoves datagrams from source host to destination host across routers. Protocols: IP (the one protocol every Internet device runs) and many routing protocols. Its packet is a datagram.
LinkMoves a frame from one node to the next node over a single link. Protocols: Ethernet, Wi-Fi (802.11), DOCSIS, PPP. Its packet is a frame.
PhysicalMoves the individual bits of a frame from one node to the next over the actual medium (twisted pair, fibre, radio).
  • Application layer: an application protocol is distributed over many end systems, which exchange messages. DNS (translating names like www.amrita.edu into addresses) is also an application-layer protocol.
  • Transport layer: TCP provides guaranteed delivery, flow control and congestion control; UDP provides none of these.
  • Network layer: the transport layer hands it a segment and a destination address; it delivers the segment to the destination host's transport layer. It is often simply called the IP layer.
  • Link layer: at each node, the network layer passes the datagram down to the link layer, which delivers it to the next node. A datagram may cross Ethernet on one link and Wi-Fi on the next.
  • Physical layer: its protocols depend on both the link and the medium (Ethernet has different physical-layer protocols for twisted pair, coax and fibre).

The OSI reference model

In the late 1970s the International Organisation for Standardisation (ISO) proposed a seven-layer model called OSI (Open Systems Interconnection). It adds two layers between application and transport:

  • Presentation layer: lets communicating applications interpret the meaning of exchanged data: data compression, data encryption and data description (so applications needn't worry about how data is stored internally).
  • Session layer: delimiting and synchronisation of data exchange, including checkpointing and recovery.

The Internet has no separate presentation or session layer; if an application needs these services, the application developer builds them in.

OSI (7 layers)TCP/IP Internet (5 layers)
ApplicationApplication
Presentation(part of the application)
Session(part of the application)
TransportTransport
NetworkNetwork
Data linkLink
PhysicalPhysical
FeatureOSI modelTCP/IP model
Developed byISO, as a reference model before protocolsGrew from working protocols (ARPAnet)
Layers75 (often described as 4, merging link and physical)
Presentation and sessionSeparate layersDone by applications
UseTeaching and referenceThe protocols actually running on the Internet
TransportConnection-oriented and connectionlessTCP (connection-oriented) and UDP (connectionless)

Encapsulation: how headers are added and removed

Not every device implements every layer.

  • Hosts implement all five layers.
  • Routers implement layers 1 to 3 (physical, link, network).
  • Link-layer switches implement layers 1 and 2 (physical, link). They don't understand IP addresses.

At each layer, a packet has two kinds of field: header fields, added by that layer, and a payload, which is the packet from the layer above.

Step by step, for an application message \(M\):

1. ApplicationCreates the message M and passes it to the transport layer.
2. TransportAdds header Ht (for example port numbers, error-detection bits) → segment Ht M.
3. NetworkAdds header Hn (source and destination IP addresses) → datagram Hn Ht M.
4. LinkAdds header Hl (source and destination MAC addresses) and a trailer → frame Hl Hn Ht M.
5. PhysicalSends the frame as bits on the medium.

At the receiving side the process runs in reverse: each layer removes its own header, uses it, and passes the payload up. This is decapsulation. At each layer, the packet is like an envelope: a memo (the payload) inside an envelope with an address (the header), placed inside a larger envelope by the next department down.

LayerData unit (PDU)Address usedDevice that works at this layer
ApplicationMessageHost name (e.g. www.amrita.edu)Hosts
TransportSegmentPort number (e.g. 80, 443)Hosts
NetworkDatagramIP address (e.g. 172.17.4.25)Routers, hosts
LinkFrameMAC address (e.g. 1A:2F:BB:76:09:AD)Switches, routers, hosts
PhysicalBitsNoneHubs, repeaters, cables
From a paperAn email's journey down the stack 20CYS303 End 2025 Q3

The question asked you to describe an email as it passes through the transport, network and data link layers, naming the data at each layer, the layer's job and the addressing or control it adds.

Given

An email (an SMTP message) handed down by the mail client.

Required

Name, function and added addressing at each of the three layers.

Formula

Encapsulation: each layer adds its header to the unit it receives.

Substitution

Transport: TCP, since SMTP needs reliability. Network: IP. Link: Ethernet or Wi-Fi on the first hop.

Calculation

Transport: the data becomes a segment; TCP adds source and destination port numbers (the client's ephemeral port and server port 25 or 587), sequence and acknowledgement numbers, and a checksum; it provides reliable, in-order, flow- and congestion-controlled delivery between processes. Network: the segment becomes a datagram; IP adds source and destination IP addresses, TTL and a protocol field (6 for TCP); it routes the datagram host to host across routers. Link: the datagram becomes a frame; Ethernet adds source and destination MAC addresses (the destination is the next hop, usually the default gateway router) and a CRC trailer for error detection; it delivers the frame across one link.

Final answer

Segment (ports, reliability) → datagram (IP addresses, routing) → frame (MAC addresses, CRC, one hop). At every router the frame header is replaced; the datagram's IP addresses stay the same end to end (unless NAT rewrites them).

Exam focus
  • Draw the five-layer stack with the PDU name at each layer. 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q4 asked for the TCP/IP stack from sender to receiver through a router, showing encapsulation and decapsulation and the packet name at each layer (5 marks). Draw the figure above with a router (3 layers).
  • Explain why layering helps, and give one drawback.
  • OSI's extra layers: presentation (compression, encryption, description) and session (synchronisation, checkpointing, recovery).
  • Which devices implement which layers: router 1–3, switch 1–2, host 1–5.
  • “Encapsulation” is a common one-line definition (20CYS303 Mid 2023 Q1(i)).
Common mistakes
  • Mixing up PDU names: message, segment, datagram, frame. (A UDP segment is sometimes called a datagram in older books; in Kurose, “datagram” is the network-layer unit.)
  • Drawing a router with all five layers. Routers do not run TCP or applications for forwarded packets.
  • Saying the Internet has a session layer. It doesn't; applications handle it.
Quick revision
  • A layer gives a service to the layer above, using the layer below.
  • Internet stack: application (message), transport (segment), network (datagram), link (frame), physical (bits).
  • OSI adds presentation and session.
  • Encapsulation adds Ht, Hn, Hl on the way down; decapsulation removes them on the way up.
  • Hosts 5 layers, routers 3, switches 2.

I.8 · Foundations

Networks under attack, and a short history

The main ways attackers abuse the Internet (malware, botnets, denial of service, sniffing, spoofing), the lines of defence, and the milestones that produced today's Internet. Prescribed because Chapter 1 is in the syllabus in full.

Sources: Kurose §1.6–1.7 · Chapter_1_v8.2 slides 57–61, 73–79

Why security belongs in Chapter 1

The Internet was designed for a group of mutually trusting users on a transparent network. That assumption no longer holds: the same openness that lets anyone build an application lets anyone attack one. Knowing the attacks tells you why later protocols include authentication, encryption and firewalls.

Malware

Malware is malicious software that enters a host over the Internet. It can delete files, install spyware that collects passwords and keystrokes, or turn the host into part of a botnet: a network of thousands of compromised devices that an attacker controls, used for spam or for denial-of-service attacks.

  • A virus needs some user interaction to infect a device, for example opening an email attachment that runs malicious code.
  • A worm enters a device without any explicit user interaction, for example by exploiting a vulnerable network application that accepts the worm's packets.

Most malware is self-replicating: once it infects one host, it seeks out more hosts to infect, so it spreads exponentially.

Denial-of-service (DoS) attacks

A DoS attack makes a network, host or other piece of infrastructure unusable by legitimate users. Three categories:

  1. Vulnerability attack: send a few well-crafted messages to a vulnerable application or operating system, making the service stop or the host crash.
  2. Bandwidth flooding: send a deluge of packets to the target so its access link becomes clogged and legitimate packets can't reach the server.
  3. Connection flooding: open a large number of half-open or fully open TCP connections at the target, so it stops accepting legitimate connections.

A single attacking host can rarely flood a large server, because the attacker's own access link is too slow. In a distributed DoS (DDoS) attack the attacker controls a botnet of many sources that all send traffic at the target. DDoS attacks using botnets with thousands of hosts are common and much harder to detect and block, because the traffic comes from everywhere.

From a paperBotnets and DDoS 19CCE304 End 2023 Q1(B)
Given

The question asks how a botnet is created and how it is used for a DDoS attack.

Required

The steps of creation and of the attack.

Formula

Botnet = many compromised hosts under one controller.

Substitution

Creation: (1) the attacker finds a vulnerability (an unpatched service, a weak password, a phishing attachment); (2) malware exploits it and installs a bot program; (3) the bot contacts the attacker's command-and-control server and waits; (4) the bot scans for and infects other vulnerable hosts, so the botnet grows.

Calculation

Attack: the attacker sends one command to the command-and-control server; every bot then floods the victim with traffic (bandwidth flooding) or with connection requests (connection flooding). The combined traffic far exceeds the victim's access-link capacity.

Final answer

A botnet is built by self-propagating malware that turns vulnerable hosts into remotely controlled bots; a DDoS attack instructs all bots to send traffic at one target simultaneously, so legitimate users can't get service and the attack is hard to filter because it has thousands of sources.

Packet sniffing

A packet sniffer is a passive receiver that records a copy of every packet passing by. On broadcast media (Wi-Fi, a cable network, an old Ethernet hub), a sniffer near the sender can read every packet, including passwords and private messages. Wireshark (the tool in your lab) is a packet sniffer. Because a sniffer is passive (it never injects packets), it is very hard to detect. The best defence is cryptography: encrypt what you send.

IP spoofing

Creating a packet with an arbitrary (false) source address and putting it on the Internet is surprisingly easy. Injecting packets with a false source address is called IP spoofing, one way a user can pretend to be another. The defence is end-point authentication: a way to be sure a message really comes from where we think it does.

Lines of defence

DefenceWhat it does
AuthenticationProves who you are (passwords, certificates)
AuthorisationChecks what you are allowed to do
Encryption (confidentiality)Makes sniffed packets unreadable
FirewallsFilter traffic entering or leaving a network by address, port and protocol
Intrusion detection systemsInspect traffic deeply for known attack patterns and alert

A short history of the Internet

PeriodMilestones
1961–1972: packet switchingKleinrock's queuing theory showed packet switching suits bursty traffic; Baran (RAND) and Davies (NPL) developed packet switching independently. ARPAnet's first four nodes were connected in 1969; by 1972 it had 15 nodes and the first host-to-host protocol, NCP. The first email program was written in 1972.
1972–1980: internetworkingALOHAnet (a radio network in Hawaii), Ethernet (Metcalfe, 1976) and other networks appeared. Cerf and Kahn designed an architecture for interconnecting networks: TCP, later split into TCP and IP, plus UDP.
1980–1990: proliferationOn 1 January 1983 every ARPAnet host switched to TCP/IP. DNS was defined to map host names to IP addresses. The FTP protocol was defined (1985). TCP congestion control (Jacobson, 1988). NSFNET linked universities.
1990s: the Web and commercialisationARPAnet ended; NSFNET's restrictions on commercial use were lifted in 1991 and it was decommissioned in 1995, leaving the backbone to commercial ISPs. Tim Berners-Lee invented the World Wide Web (HTML, HTTP, a web server and a browser) at CERN in 1989–1991; Mosaic and Netscape browsers followed.
2000s onwardBroadband home access, Wi-Fi, smartphones (2007), social networks, online video, cloud services and huge private networks run by Google and others, and 4G/5G cellular access.
Exam focus
  • Botnet + DDoS is a 4-mark explain question (19CCE304 End 2023 Q1(B)).
  • Know the three kinds of DoS attack, and the virus vs worm difference.
  • Sniffing (passive, defended by encryption) vs spoofing (false source address, defended by authentication).
Quick revision
  • Malware: virus (needs user action), worm (no user action); botnets of compromised hosts.
  • DoS: vulnerability attack, bandwidth flooding, connection flooding; DDoS uses many sources.
  • Sniffing reads packets passively; spoofing fakes the source address.
  • History: packet switching (1960s), ARPAnet (1969), TCP/IP (1983), DNS (1983), Web (1989–91), commercial Internet (1995).

I.9 · Application layer

Principles of network applications

How applications are organised (client-server or peer-to-peer), how their processes talk through sockets, how a process is addressed, and how to choose between TCP and UDP.

Sources: Kurose §2.1 · Chapter_2_v8.2 slides 2–16

Where applications live

A network application is a set of programs running on different end systems that communicate over the network: a browser and a web server; WhatsApp on two phones. You write software only for the end systems, never for the routers. Network-core devices don't run applications; they work only at the network layer and below. This is what lets new applications spread so fast.

Two application architectures

Client-server

An always-on host, the server, serves requests from many other hosts, the clients. Clients don't talk directly to each other. The server has a fixed, well-known IP address, so a client can always reach it. Popular services use data centres with many servers, because one server can't keep up.

Examples: the web, email, FTP, AUMS, online banking.

Peer-to-peer (P2P)

There is little or no reliance on always-on servers. Pairs of intermittently connected hosts, called peers, communicate directly. Peers are owned by users (laptops, desktops), change IP addresses, and come and go.

Its key feature is self-scalability: each new peer adds service demand, but also adds service capacity by uploading to others.

Examples: BitTorrent file sharing, some video-calling systems.

FeatureClient-serverPeer-to-peer
Who serves dataA dedicated always-on serverThe peers themselves
Address of the providerFixed, well knownPeers change addresses
ScalabilityNeeds more servers or data centres as clients growSelf-scaling: every peer adds upload capacity
CostExpensive infrastructure and bandwidth for the providerCheap for the provider
Management and securityCentralised, easier to secure and controlHard: peers are untrusted, distributed, intermittent
ReliabilityThe server is a single point of failureNo single point of failure, but peers leave
Typical applicationWeb, email, FTP, bankingBitTorrent, file sharing
From a paper 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q2

“Compare client-server with P2P and give a suitable application for each” (5 marks). Use the table above, and name the web (client-server) and BitTorrent (P2P).

Processes communicate by exchanging messages

It is not hosts but processes (programs being executed) that communicate. Processes on the same host use inter-process communication from the operating system; processes on different hosts exchange messages across the network.

In every pair of communicating processes, one is labelled the client and the other the server: the process that initiates the communication is the client; the process that waits to be contacted is the server. In P2P file sharing a peer is a client when downloading and a server when uploading.

The socket: the door between the process and the network

A process sends and receives messages through a software interface called a socket. A socket is the interface between the application layer and the transport layer within a host; it is also called the API (application programming interface) between the application and the network.

A house and its door

A process is like a house and its socket is like the door. To send a message, the process pushes it out of the door. It relies on the transport infrastructure on the other side of the door to carry the message to the door of the destination process. The application developer controls everything inside the house, but outside the door they can only choose the transport protocol (TCP or UDP) and set a few parameters, such as the maximum buffer size.

Addressing a process

To deliver a message to a process on another host, the sender needs two pieces of information:

  1. the IP address of the destination host (a 32-bit number in IPv4) to find the host, and
  2. a port number that identifies the receiving process (socket) on that host.

Popular applications have well-known port numbers: a web server listens on port 80 (443 for HTTPS), a mail server on port 25, a DNS server on port 53, FTP control on port 21.

What transport services does an application need?

Applications can be classified by four requirements.

RequirementMeaningWho needs it
Reliable data transferEvery byte arrives, correct and in orderEmail, file transfer, web documents, banking. Loss-tolerant apps (audio, video) can live with some loss
ThroughputA guaranteed minimum rateBandwidth-sensitive apps such as video at a fixed bit rate. Elastic apps (email, file transfer, web) use whatever they get
TimingA bound on delayInternet telephony, video conferencing, multiplayer games (under a few hundred ms)
SecurityConfidentiality, integrity, end-point authenticationBanking, login, private messaging
ApplicationData lossThroughputTime-sensitive
File transfer / downloadNo lossElasticNo
EmailNo lossElasticNo
Web documentsNo lossElastic (few kbps)No
Internet telephony / video conferencingLoss-tolerantAudio: few kbps–1 Mbps; video: 10 kbps–5 MbpsYes, 100s of ms
Streaming stored audio/videoLoss-tolerantSame as aboveYes, a few seconds
Interactive gamesLoss-tolerantFew kbps–10 kbpsYes, 100s of ms
Smartphone messagingNo lossElasticYes and no

The two Internet transport services

TCP

  • Connection-oriented: client and server exchange control information (a handshake) before data flows; then a full-duplex connection exists between their sockets.
  • Reliable: delivers all data without error and in the proper order.
  • Congestion control: throttles the sender when the network is congested.
  • Does not give throughput or timing guarantees.

UDP

  • Connectionless: no handshake.
  • Unreliable: no guarantee that a message arrives, or arrives in order.
  • No congestion control: the sender can push data at any rate it likes.
  • Lightweight and fast to start.

Neither TCP nor UDP provides encryption. TLS (Transport Layer Security) is a layer added on top of TCP, implemented in the application, that provides encryption, data integrity and end-point authentication. HTTPS is HTTP over TLS.

ApplicationApplication-layer protocolTransport
EmailSMTPTCP
Remote terminal accessTelnet, SSHTCP
WebHTTP 1.1 (HTTP/3 runs over QUIC/UDP)TCP
File transferFTPTCP
Streaming multimediaHTTP (e.g. YouTube, DASH)TCP
Internet telephonySIP, RTP or proprietary (e.g. Skype)UDP or TCP
Name translationDNSUsually UDP

What an application-layer protocol defines

  1. The types of messages exchanged (request and response messages).
  2. The syntax of each message type: its fields and how they are delimited.
  3. The semantics of the fields: what the information means.
  4. The rules for when and how a process sends messages and responds.

Some protocols are open and defined in RFCs (HTTP, SMTP), so anyone can build compatible software; others are proprietary (Skype, Zoom).

From a paperChoosing TCP or UDP 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q5(a) 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q3(a)
Given

Applications: Apple Mail, Microsoft Edge (web browsing), Skype (voice/video calls), name translation; and in 23CSE302: live video streaming, DNS queries, web browsing, email.

Required

Transport protocol, application protocol and loss tolerance for each; and which suit UDP.

Formula

Use TCP when every byte must arrive; UDP when timeliness matters more than completeness, or for one-message request/reply exchanges.

Substitution

Look up each application's requirement in the tables above.

Calculation

Apple Mail: TCP, SMTP to send and IMAP to read, not loss-tolerant. Edge: TCP, HTTP/HTTPS, not tolerant. Skype: UDP (falls back to TCP), proprietary, loss-tolerant. Name translation: UDP, DNS, not tolerant (it retries instead).

Final answer

UDP suits live video streaming (a late frame is useless, so retransmission doesn't help; a lost frame just causes a brief glitch) and DNS queries (a single short query and reply; no connection setup delay; the client simply resends if no reply arrives). Web browsing and email need TCP because a missing byte corrupts the page or message.

Common mistakes
  • Saying “the client is the smaller machine”. The client is the process that initiates contact.
  • Forgetting that a port identifies a process (socket), and the IP address a host.
  • Saying TCP guarantees throughput or delay. It guarantees only reliable, in-order delivery.
Quick revision
  • Architectures: client-server (always-on server, fixed address) vs P2P (peers, self-scalability).
  • Client process initiates; server process waits.
  • Socket = door between process and transport layer. Process address = IP address + port.
  • App needs: reliability, throughput, timing, security.
  • TCP: connection-oriented, reliable, congestion control. UDP: none of these. TLS adds security on top of TCP.

I.10 · Application layer

The Web and HTTP

How a browser fetches a page: objects and URLs, non-persistent and persistent connections and their timing, request and response messages, cookies, web caches, conditional GET, and HTTP/2 and HTTP/3.

Sources: Kurose §2.2 · Chapter_2_v8.2 slides 17–53 · quiz 1 · Chapter2-Problems

The Web in one paragraph

A web page consists of objects. An object is simply a file (an HTML file, a JPEG image, a JavaScript file, a video clip) addressable by a single URL. Most pages consist of a base HTML file and several referenced objects: a page with HTML text and five images has six objects. Each URL has two parts, the host name of the server and the object's path name:

http://www.amrita.edu/campus/images/logo.png → host www.amrita.edu, path /campus/images/logo.png.

HTTP overview

HTTP (HyperText Transfer Protocol) is the Web's application-layer protocol. It is implemented in two programs, a client (the browser) and a server (a web server such as Apache or Nginx), which talk by exchanging HTTP messages.

  • HTTP uses TCP. The client opens a TCP connection to the server's port 80. Once connected, browser and server access TCP through their sockets. Because TCP is reliable, HTTP doesn't worry about lost data.
  • HTTP is stateless: the server keeps no information about past client requests. If the same client asks for the same object twice in a few seconds, the server sends it again. (Cookies, below, add state on top.)

Non-persistent and persistent connections

An application must decide whether each request/response pair goes over its own TCP connection or all requests go over the same one.

  • Non-persistent HTTP: at most one object is sent over a TCP connection, and the connection is then closed. Downloading several objects needs several connections. (HTTP/1.0.)
  • Persistent HTTP: the server leaves the connection open after responding, and later requests and responses between the same client and server use the same connection. (HTTP/1.1 default.) With pipelining, the client sends requests back to back without waiting for each response.

Round-trip time and the response time of one object

The round-trip time (RTT) is the time for a small packet to travel from client to server and back. It includes propagation, queuing and processing delays.

With non-persistent HTTP, fetching one object takes:

  1. The client initiates a TCP connection (sends a small segment); the server acknowledges. One RTT has passed.
  2. The client sends the HTTP request together with the third part of the handshake; the server sends the file. One more RTT, plus the time the server needs to transmit the file.
Response time per object

Non-persistent: \( 2\,\text{RTT} + \text{file transmission time} \) for every object.

Persistent: \( 2\,\text{RTT} \) for the first object (connection + request), then about 1 RTT per further object (or 1 RTT for all of them with pipelining).

Mode (base page + n small objects, RTT dominating)Total time
Non-persistent, no parallel connections\( 2 + 2n \) RTT
Non-persistent, \(k\) parallel connections\( 2 + 2\lceil n/k \rceil \) RTT
Persistent, without pipelining\( 2 + n \) RTT
Persistent, with pipelining\( 2 + 1 \) = 3 RTT

Add the DNS lookup time before the first TCP connection if the host name isn't cached.

Non-persistent HTTPPersistent HTTP
New TCP connection per objectOne TCP connection for many objects
2 RTT per object1 RTT per object after the first (or less with pipelining)
Server must allocate buffers and variables per connection, a heavy loadLess overhead at client and server
Often uses parallel connections to compensateDefault in HTTP/1.1
Connection closed after each responseServer closes an idle connection after a timeout
Interactive visualizationHTTP request–response timingAnimate DNS lookup, the TCP handshake and every GET for non-persistent, parallel, persistent and pipelined modes, with the RTTs added up as you go.

HTTP message format

Request message

GET /somedir/page.html HTTP/1.1
Host: www.someschool.edu
Connection: close
User-agent: Mozilla/5.0
Accept-language: fr
  • The first line is the request line: a method field, a URL field and the HTTP version.
  • The following lines are header lines. Host names the server (needed by web caches); Connection: close asks for non-persistent behaviour; User-agent names the browser; Accept-language says the user prefers French.
  • A blank line (carriage return and line feed) ends the headers, followed by an optional entity body.
MethodPurpose
GETRequest an object (the most common). Form input can go in the URL: /search?q=networks
POSTSend form data in the entity body (e.g. a login form)
HEADLike GET, but the response carries no object; used for debugging
PUTUpload an object to a given path on the server
DELETEDelete an object on the server

Response message

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Connection: close
Date: Tue, 18 Aug 2015 15:44:04 GMT
Server: Apache/2.2.3 (CentOS)
Last-Modified: Tue, 18 Aug 2015 15:11:03 GMT
Content-Length: 6821
Content-Type: text/html

(data data data ...)

The status line has the protocol version, a status code and a phrase. Then header lines, a blank line and the entity body (the object).

CodeMeaning
200 OKRequest succeeded; the object is in the body
301 Moved PermanentlyThe object moved; the new URL is in the Location: header
304 Not ModifiedThe cached copy is up to date (conditional GET); no object sent
400 Bad RequestThe server could not understand the request
404 Not FoundThe object does not exist on the server
505 HTTP Version Not SupportedThe server doesn't support the HTTP version
From a paperReading a server's response 19CCE304 End 2023 Q2

The paper showed a response beginning HTTP/1.1 200 OK, with Date: Tue, 07 Mar 2008 12:39:45GMT, Last-Modified: Sat, 10 Dec2005 18:27:46 GMT, Content-Length: 3874, Connection: Keep-Alive, followed by a body starting <!doctype html ....

Given

The status line and header lines of the response.

Required

(A) Was the document found, and when was the reply sent? (B) When was it last modified? (C) How many bytes? (D) The first 5 bytes, and was a persistent connection agreed?

Formula

Read the status code and the matching header lines.

Substitution

Status code 200; Date; Last-Modified; Content-Length; Connection and Keep-Alive.

Calculation

200 OK means found. Date gives the reply time. Content-Length gives the size. The body begins <!doc. Connection: Keep-Alive (with Keep-Alive: timeout=...,max=100) means the server will keep the connection open.

Final answer

(A) Yes, 200 OK; sent Tue 07 Mar 2008 at 12:39:45 GMT. (B) Sat 10 Dec 2005, 18:27:46 GMT. (C) 3,874 bytes. (D) <!doc; yes, the connection is persistent (Keep-Alive).

Cookies: adding state to a stateless protocol

HTTP is stateless, but websites want to identify users (to remember a shopping cart, a login, preferences). Cookies do this with four components:

  1. a Set-cookie: header line in the HTTP response;
  2. a Cookie: header line in later HTTP requests;
  3. a cookie file kept on the user's host and managed by the browser;
  4. a back-end database at the website.
From a paperA purchase record with cookies 19CCE304 Jan 2023 Q2(A)
Given

An e-commerce site wants a purchase record for each customer.

Required

How cookies achieve this.

Formula

The four cookie components.

Substitution

First visit: no cookie in the request.

Calculation

The server creates a unique ID (say 1678), stores a database entry keyed by it, and replies with Set-cookie: 1678. The browser saves it. Each later request carries Cookie: 1678; whenever the customer buys something, the server adds the item to the database entry for 1678. Weeks later, the same cookie lets the site show the purchase history and recommendations.

Final answer

The cookie ID links every visit to one database record, giving a stateful “session” on top of stateless HTTP. The trade-off is privacy: the site, and third parties whose cookies are embedded in pages, can track browsing behaviour.

Web caching (proxy servers)

A web cache, also called a proxy server, is a network entity that satisfies HTTP requests on behalf of an origin server. It has its own disk storage holding copies of recently requested objects. The browser is configured to send all requests to the cache first.

1. RequestThe browser opens a TCP connection to the cache and sends the HTTP request.
2. CheckThe cache checks whether it has a copy. If yes (a hit), it returns the object straight away.
3. MissIf not, the cache opens a TCP connection to the origin server and requests the object.
4. StoreThe origin server replies; the cache stores a copy and forwards the object to the browser.

A cache is both a server and a client: a server to the browser, a client to the origin server. Caches are usually installed by ISPs or universities.

Why use one? It cuts the response time (the cache is close to the client), reduces traffic on the institution's access link (so it needn't be upgraded as soon), and reduces Internet traffic as a whole.

Worked exampleDo we buy a faster access link or a cache? (Kurose example in your slides)

An institutional network has a 1 Gbps LAN and a 1.54 Mbps access link to the Internet. Browsers request 15 objects per second, each 100 kbits. The RTT from the institutional router to any origin server is 2 s.

Given

\(R_{LAN} = 1\) Gbps, \(R_{access} = 1.54\) Mbps, request rate \(a = 15\) /s, object size \(L = 100\) kbits, Internet delay = 2 s.

Required

Access-link utilisation and average delay (a) as is, (b) with a 154 Mbps access link, (c) with a cache of hit rate 0.4.

Formula

Utilisation \(= aL/R\). Average delay \(= h \times d_{cache} + (1-h) \times d_{origin}\).

Substitution

Data rate to browsers \(= 15 \times 100{,}000 = 1.5\) Mbps.

Calculation

(a) Access link: \(1.5 / 1.54 = 0.97\); LAN: \(1.5 / 1000 = 0.0015\). At 0.97 the queuing delay is minutes. (b) \(1.5 / 154 = 0.0097\): delay ≈ 2 s + milliseconds, but the link is expensive. (c) Only 60% of requests cross the access link: \(0.6 \times 1.5 = 0.9\) Mbps, utilisation \(0.9 / 1.54 = 0.58\), queuing delay of milliseconds. Average delay \(= 0.6 \times 2.01 + 0.4 \times (\text{a few ms}) \approx 1.2\) s.

Final answer

The cache gives about 1.2 s average delay, lower than the 154 Mbps upgrade (about 2 s), and it is much cheaper.

From a paperCache savings for a university website 19CSE301 End 2024 Q1(b)
Given

60,000 requests per day; hit rate 75%; average object 600 kB; cache latency 40 ms; origin latency 250 ms; cache–client bandwidth 500 Mbps.

Required

Requests served by cache and origin; bandwidth saved per day; average response time before and after.

Formula

Cache requests \(= h \times N\). Saved data \(= \text{cache requests} \times \text{object size}\). Average time \(= h\,t_{cache} + (1-h)\,t_{origin}\).

Substitution

\(h = 0.75\), \(N = 60{,}000\), object = 600 kB.

Calculation

Cache: \(0.75 \times 60{,}000 = 45{,}000\); origin: 15,000. Saved: \(45{,}000 \times 600\text{ kB} = 27{,}000{,}000\) kB = 27 GB (= 216 Gbits) of origin traffic per day. Before: every request goes to the origin, 250 ms. After: \(0.75 \times 40 + 0.25 \times 250 = 30 + 62.5 = 92.5\) ms.

Final answer

45,000 from the cache and 15,000 from the origin; 27 GB saved per day; average response time falls from 250 ms to 92.5 ms. (If you add the object's transmission time to the client, \(4.8\text{ Mbit} / 500\text{ Mbps} = 9.6\) ms, the figures become 259.6 ms and 102.1 ms.)

The conditional GET

A cached copy may be stale. The conditional GET lets a cache (or browser) check freshness without downloading the object again. A request is a conditional GET if it uses the GET method and includes an If-Modified-Since: header line.

The first response came with a Last-Modified: date, which the cache stored. Next time, it sends that date in If-Modified-Since:. If the object hasn't changed, the server replies 304 Not Modified with no body, saving bandwidth and transmission time. This answers 19CSE301 End 2024 Q1(a): a news site where users must always see the latest article without re-downloading unchanged resources uses conditional GET.

HTTP/2 and HTTP/3

HTTP/1.1 allowed pipelined GETs over one connection, but the server answers them in order (first come, first served). A small object can be stuck behind a large one: head-of-line (HOL) blocking. A lost TCP segment also stalls everything behind it.

HTTP/2 (RFC 7540, 2015) keeps the methods, status codes and most header fields, but:

  • breaks each message into small frames and interleaves the frames of different responses on one TCP connection, so a small object isn't stuck behind a large one;
  • lets the client set priorities for responses;
  • allows server push: the server sends objects the client will need (images in the page) before they are requested;
  • compresses headers.

HTTP/3 runs HTTP over QUIC, a transport protocol built on UDP, which adds reliability, congestion control and security per stream, so a loss on one stream doesn't stall the others. 20CYS303 End 2025 Q6 asked for the HTTP communication process and the role of HTTP/3.

Worked timing numericals

Level 1Counting RTTs 19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q8

A base HTML file references 10 objects. RTT dominates all other times.

Given

1 base object + \(n = 10\) objects; ignore transmission time.

Required

RTTs in four modes.

Formula

Use the mode table above.

Substitution

\(n = 10\); \(k = 10\) parallel connections in (ii).

Calculation

(i) Non-persistent, serial: \(2 + 2 \times 10 = 22\) RTT. (ii) Non-persistent, 10 parallel: \(2 + 2 = 4\) RTT. (iii) Persistent without pipelining: \(2 + 10 = 12\) RTT. (iv) Persistent with pipelining: \(2 + 1 = 3\) RTT.

Final answer

22, 4, 12 and 3 RTTs.

Level 2DNS plus HTTP (Kurose problem in Chapter2-Problems)

Three DNS servers are visited with RTTs of 4, 44 and 8 ms. The web page's base object references 3 very small objects; \(\text{RTT}_{HTTP} = 20\) ms.

Given

DNS = 4 + 44 + 8 = 56 ms; \(\text{RTT}_{HTTP} = 20\) ms; 3 extra objects; transmission times zero.

Required

Time until everything is received for (1) the base object only, (2) non-persistent serial, (3) non-persistent with up to 5 parallel connections, (4) persistent.

Formula

Total = DNS + base (2 RTT) + objects.

Substitution

2 RTT = 40 ms.

Calculation

(1) \(56 + 40 = 96\) ms. (2) \(96 + 3 \times 40 = 216\) ms. (3) \(96 + 40 = 136\) ms (all 3 in parallel). (4) \(96 + 20 = 116\) ms (all 3 pipelined on the open connection).

Final answer

96 ms, 216 ms, 136 ms and 116 ms.

From a paperFour parallel connections 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q3
Given

Local DNS cache visited once, \(\text{RTT}_0 = 3\) ms; \(\text{RTT}_{HTTP} = 34\) ms; base object + 10 tiny objects on the same server.

Required

Time in (a) base only, (b) non-persistent serial, (c) non-persistent with at most 4 parallel connections, (d) persistent with at most 4 pipelined requests at a time.

Formula

DNS + 2 RTT for the base, then per mode.

Substitution

\(2\,\text{RTT} = 68\) ms; \(\lceil 10/4 \rceil = 3\) rounds.

Calculation

(a) \(3 + 68 = 71\) ms. (b) \(71 + 10 \times 68 = 751\) ms. (c) \(71 + 3 \times 68 = 275\) ms. (d) \(71 + 3 \times 34 = 173\) ms.

Final answer

71 ms, 751 ms, 275 ms, 173 ms. (If (d) allowed all 10 requests to be pipelined at once, it would be \(71 + 34 = 105\) ms. State your interpretation.)

From a paperMedilab: DNS, non-persistent HTTP, then a cache 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q7(B,C)

The site www.medilab.org has a base page and 5 images. RTT client–local DNS = 6 ms; local DNS to the authoritative server through the .org TLD = 20 ms; client–web server RTT = 40 ms. Then a web cache is added at the ISP (client–cache RTT 10 ms), persistent HTTP is used, and 60% of requests are answered by the cache.

Given

Objects = 1 + 5 = 6. DNS = 6 + 20 = 26 ms. \(\text{RTT}_{server} = 40\) ms, \(\text{RTT}_{cache} = 10\) ms, hit share 60%.

Required

(B) total time, non-persistent without parallel connections; (C) total time with the cache and persistent HTTP.

Formula

(B) DNS + 6 × 2 RTT. (C) DNS + [1 RTT setup + requests] to the cache + [1 RTT setup + requests] to the origin (the same counting as your quiz 1 key).

Substitution

(C) 60% of 6 requests = 3.6 to the cache, 2.4 to the origin (expected values).

Calculation

(B) \(26 + 6 \times 2 \times 40 = 26 + 480 = 506\) ms. (C) Cache: \(10 + 3.6 \times 10 = 46\) ms. Origin: \(40 + 2.4 \times 40 = 136\) ms. Total \(= 26 + 46 + 136 = 208\) ms.

Final answer

(B) 506 ms. (C) About 208 ms. (If you round to whole objects, 4 from the cache and 2 from the origin, you get \(26 + (10 + 40) + (40 + 80) = 196\) ms; write down whichever assumption you use.)

From a paperFive DNS servers and a slow link 23CSE302 End 2025 Q3

Clicking https://my.amrita.edu/index/login visits 5 DNS servers with round-trip times RTT₁ … RTT₅. The page is a base HTML file plus 10 objects, each 200 kbits. The host–server link is 100 kbps, the one-way propagation time is \(T_p\), and TCP control and GET messages are negligible in size.

Given

DNS time \(= \sum_{i=1}^{5} \text{RTT}_i\). 11 objects of \(L = 200\) kbits, \(R = 100\) kbps. One RTT to the server \(= 2T_p\).

Required

(a) total time with 11 serial (non-persistent) connections; (b) the time if the link is clicked again 30 minutes later.

Formula

Per object: connection (1 RTT) + request and response (1 RTT) + \(L/R\).

Substitution

\(L/R = 200{,}000 / 100{,}000 = 2\) s. Per object \(= 2T_p + 2T_p + 2 = 4T_p + 2\) s.

Calculation

(a) \( \sum \text{RTT}_i + 11(4T_p + 2) = \sum_{i=1}^{5}\text{RTT}_i + 44\,T_p + 22 \) s. (b) After 30 minutes the name-to-address mapping is still in the DNS cache (its TTL hasn't expired), so the DNS term disappears: \(44\,T_p + 22\) s.

Final answer

(a) \(\text{RTT}_1 + \dots + \text{RTT}_5 + 44T_p + 22\) s. (b) \(44T_p + 22\) s. If the browser has also cached the objects and checks them with conditional GETs that return 304, the 22 s of transmission also disappears, leaving about \(44T_p\).

From a paperWhen parallel connections don't help 19CCE304 End 2024 Q2(A)

A 10 m link runs at 150 bits/s in each direction. Data packets are 100,000 bits; control packets (ACK, handshake) are 200 bits. N parallel connections each get 1/N of the bandwidth. The base object references 10 objects of 100 kbits each.

Given

\(R = 150\) bps; data 100,000 bits; control 200 bits; propagation \(\approx 10/3\times10^8\) s ≈ 0 (negligible).

Required

Does parallel non-persistent HTTP make sense? Is persistent HTTP much better?

Formula

Each object over a new connection: 3 control packets + 1 data packet. Persistent: 1 control (request) + 1 data per object after the first.

Substitution

Base object at full rate: \(3 \times 200/150 + 100{,}000/150 = 4 + 666.7 = 670.7\) s. Ten parallel connections at 15 bps each: \(3 \times 200/15 + 100{,}000/15 = 40 + 6{,}666.7 = 6{,}706.7\) s.

Calculation

Parallel non-persistent total \(= 670.7 + 6{,}706.7 = 7{,}377\) s. Persistent: \(670.7 + 10 \times (200/150 + 100{,}000/150) = 670.7 + 10 \times 668 = 7{,}351\) s.

Final answer

Parallel downloads give no real benefit, because the 10 connections just split the same 150 bps. Persistent HTTP is less than 1% faster (7,351 s vs 7,377 s). When transmission time dominates, saving RTTs hardly matters.

Exam focus
  • HTTP timing appeared in 8 papers. Always draw the timing diagram: SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK+GET, response, and count RTTs.
  • Persistent vs non-persistent, with and without parallel connections and pipelining.
  • Conditional GET (If-Modified-Since, 304 Not Modified) and web caching calculations.
  • Cookie components, status codes, and reading header lines.
  • 19CSE301 End 2023 Q2(d): a page of text and five small images in non-persistent mode needs a minimum of 6 TCP connections (one per object).
Common mistakes
  • Using 1 RTT per object for non-persistent HTTP. It's 2: one to set up the connection, one for the request.
  • Forgetting the DNS time, or adding it once per object. DNS happens once, before the first connection.
  • Forgetting the initial 2 RTT for the base page in persistent mode.
  • Thinking a 304 response carries the object. It carries no body.
Quick revision
  • Page = base HTML + referenced objects; URL = host + path. HTTP over TCP port 80, stateless.
  • Non-persistent: 2 RTT + transmission per object. Persistent: one connection; 1 RTT per object; pipelining.
  • Request line: method URL version. Status line: version code phrase. Codes 200, 301, 304, 400, 404, 505.
  • Cookies: Set-cookie, Cookie, browser file, back-end database.
  • Web cache = proxy, both client and server; average delay \(= h\,d_{cache} + (1-h)\,d_{origin}\).
  • Conditional GET: If-Modified-Since → 304 Not Modified.
  • HTTP/2: frames, interleaving, priorities, server push. HTTP/3: over QUIC/UDP.

I.11 · Application layer

File transfer: FTP

Why FTP uses two TCP connections, what travels on each, its commands and replies, active and passive modes, and why firewalls break file downloads.

Sources: Forouzan §26.3 Kurose 8e no longer covers FTP

Source note

Kurose 8th edition dropped FTP; your Chapter 2 slides only list it as an example protocol. FTP is prescribed through Forouzan Chapter 26 (Section 26.3, File transfer), which this topic follows.

What FTP is for

FTP (File Transfer Protocol) is the standard TCP/IP mechanism for copying a file from one host to another. Copying a file sounds simple, but two systems may use different file-name conventions, different ways of representing text and data, and different directory structures. FTP solves these problems in a very simple and elegant way.

A user sits at the local host and wants to transfer files to or from a remote host. To access the remote account, the user must give a user name and password. After that, the user can list directories, change directories, download (get) and upload (put) files.

The key idea: two separate connections

FTP differs from other client-server applications in that it establishes two connections between the hosts:

  • The control connection uses TCP port 21 on the server. It carries commands and responses: user name, password, “change directory”, “list files”, “send me this file”. It stays open for the entire user session (it is persistent).
  • The data connection uses TCP port 20 on the server (in active mode). It carries the file contents and directory listings. It is opened for each file transfer and closed when that transfer finishes (it is non-persistent).

Because the control information travels on a separate connection from the data, FTP is said to send its control information out-of-band. (HTTP sends its header lines in-band, in the same connection as the file.)

Why separate them?

  • Commands are short text lines exchanged interactively, while files are long and are sent in bulk. Separating them lets each be handled appropriately: the control connection needs only minimal delay; the data connection needs maximal throughput.
  • The user can still send commands (for example, abort) while a file transfer is in progress.
  • A new data connection per file makes the end of the file obvious: the sender closes the connection.

How a download works, step by step

Interactive visualizationFTP: control and data connectionsWatch login on port 21, then a separate data connection opened and closed for each file, in active and passive mode, including what a firewall blocks.
Open simulator →
  1. The client opens the control connection to server port 21 and logs in with USER and PASS.
  2. The client chooses an ephemeral port for data and tells the server with the PORT command (active mode), then sends RETR filename (retrieve).
  3. The server opens the data connection from its port 20 to the client's announced port, sends the file and closes the connection.
  4. The control connection remains open for further commands, until QUIT.

Commands and responses

Communication on the control connection uses the NVT ASCII character set: commands are plain-text lines; each response is a three-digit code followed by text.

CommandMeaning
USER, PASSSend user name and password
LISTSend a directory listing (over a data connection)
CWD, PWDChange / print working directory
RETR filenameRetrieve (download) a file
STOR filenameStore (upload) a file
PORTClient tells the server which client port to use for data (active mode)
PASVClient asks the server to choose a port and wait (passive mode)
TYPE A / TYPE IASCII or image (binary) transfer
QUITLog out
CodeMeaning
125 / 150Data connection already open / about to open
200Command OK
220Service ready
221Service closing
226Closing data connection, transfer complete
230User login OK
331User name OK, password required
425Can't open data connection
450 / 550File unavailable
500Syntax error, command unrecognised

The first digit tells the client the type of reply: 1 = positive preliminary, 2 = positive completion, 3 = positive intermediate (send more), 4 = transient error, 5 = permanent error.

Active and passive mode

Active mode (default in the original standard)Passive mode
Client sends PORT with its own data portClient sends PASV
Server opens the data connection, from port 20 to the client's portServer replies with a port number (227 Entering Passive Mode) and waits
An incoming connection to the client is neededClient opens the data connection to the server's announced port
Often blocked by the client's firewall or NATWorks through client firewalls and NAT, so browsers and most clients use it

Data handling: types, structures and modes

To overcome differences between systems, the client defines on the control connection:

  • File type: ASCII file (text, the default), EBCDIC file (for IBM systems), or image file (binary, sent as a stream of bits with no interpretation, used for compiled programs and images).
  • Data structure: file structure (a continuous stream of bytes, the default), record structure (text files divided into records) or page structure (pages with page numbers, accessible randomly).
  • Transmission mode: stream mode (the default; data delivered as a continuous stream of bytes), block mode (data in blocks, each with a header) or compressed mode (run-length compression).

Kinds of FTP access

KindDescription
Password-protected FTPThe normal case: a user name and password are required
Anonymous FTPPublic file archives: user name anonymous, password usually an email address; access is limited to a subset of commands and directories
Active and passive FTPThe two ways of opening the data connection, as above
Secure variantsFTPS (FTP over TLS) and SFTP (a separate protocol that runs over SSH) encrypt the password and data, which plain FTP sends in clear text
TFTP (Trivial FTP)A tiny file-transfer protocol over UDP with no authentication, used for booting devices

FTP compared with HTTP

FeatureFTPHTTP
ConnectionsTwo: control (21) + data (20)One (port 80)
Control informationOut-of-band, on its own connectionIn-band, header lines in the same connection
StateStateful: the server tracks the user's login and current directory for the whole sessionStateless (cookies add state)
Control connectionPersistent for the sessionPersistent or non-persistent
Data connectionNon-persistent: one per fileSame connection carries headers and body
AuthenticationBuilt in (USER, PASS)Optional (forms, tokens)
Message formatText commands and 3-digit repliesText request/response with headers
Typical useBulk upload and download of filesFetching web objects
From a paperThe blocked FTP port 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q5

An employee logs in to the company's FTP server successfully, but every download fails, and the firewall log shows that an FTP-related port was blocked.

Given

Login works; file transfer fails; a firewall blocked an FTP port.

Required

(a) Which connection is blocked and why that breaks the transfer. (b) The roles of the two connections, with a diagram.

Formula

Control on port 21 carries login and commands; data on port 20 (active mode) carries files.

Substitution

Login succeeded, so port 21 and the control connection work.

Calculation

The failing part must be the data connection. In active mode the server opens it from port 20 to a port on the client, and the client-side firewall blocks that incoming connection (or the firewall blocks port 20 itself).

Final answer

(a) The data connection (port 20 / the client's announced data port) is blocked, so no file contents or listings can flow even though commands and replies on port 21 still work. Switching the client to passive mode, where the client opens the data connection outward, usually fixes it. (b) Draw the two-connection figure: the control connection (port 21) is opened first, stays open for the whole session and carries USER, PASS, RETR, QUIT and the numeric replies; the data connection (port 20) is opened for each file or listing and closed when it ends.

Exam focus
  • 19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q2: “FTP uses separate connections for data and control. True or false? Justify with a diagram and list the types of FTP.” True: control on 21 (persistent), data on 20 (per file).
  • Out-of-band vs in-band control: the standard FTP vs HTTP comparison.
  • The firewall question from your own course paper. Expect another scenario question.
Common mistakes
  • Saying the data connection stays open for the whole session. It's the control connection that does.
  • Swapping the ports: 21 is control, 20 is data.
  • Calling FTP stateless. The server remembers the login and directory throughout the session.
Quick revision
  • FTP over TCP, two connections: control (21, persistent, commands and 3-digit replies), data (20, one per file).
  • Out-of-band control; stateful server.
  • Active mode: server connects to the client (PORT). Passive mode: client connects to the server (PASV), firewall-friendly.
  • File types ASCII/EBCDIC/image; structures file/record/page; modes stream/block/compressed.

I.12 · Application layer

Electronic mail in the Internet

The three parts of the email system, how SMTP pushes a message from Alice's server to Bob's, the message format and MIME, and how Bob pulls mail with POP3, IMAP or HTTP.

Sources: Kurose §2.3 · Forouzan §26.2 · Chapter_2_v8.2 slides 54–60 · Chapter2-Problems

The three components

User agents

Programs that let users read, reply to, forward, save and compose messages: Microsoft Outlook, Apple Mail, the Gmail app or the Gmail web page. When Alice finishes composing, her user agent sends the message to her mail server.

Mail servers

The core of the system. Each recipient has a mailbox on a mail server. Each server also has an outgoing message queue of mail waiting to be sent. Mail servers talk to each other with SMTP.

The third component is SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol), the principal application-layer protocol for email. It runs over TCP (reliable) on port 25. Every mail server runs both the client side of SMTP (when sending) and the server side (when receiving).

Sending a message, step by step

  1. Alice invokes her user agent, gives Bob's address (bob@someschool.edu), composes and asks the agent to send.
  2. Her user agent sends the message to her mail server, where it is placed in the message queue.
  3. The client side of SMTP on Alice's server sees the message and opens a TCP connection to an SMTP server on Bob's mail server.
  4. After an initial SMTP handshake, the client sends Alice's message into the connection.
  5. At Bob's mail server, the server side of SMTP receives it and places it in Bob's mailbox.
  6. Later, Bob invokes his user agent to read the message.

If Bob's server is down, Alice's server keeps the message in its queue and retries (typically every 30 minutes); after several days without success it gives up and notifies Alice. That is why Alice's agent doesn't send straight to Bob's server: Alice's PC may be switched off, but her mail server is always on and can keep retrying.

SMTP normally doesn't use intermediate servers: the TCP connection goes directly from Alice's server to Bob's, even if they are on opposite sides of the world.

Interactive visualizationEmail delivery from Gmail to YahooStep through the user agent, both mail servers, the DNS MX lookup, the SMTP dialogue, and Bob fetching the mail with IMAP or POP3.
Open simulator →

The SMTP dialogue

After the TCP connection is set up, the client (C, Alice's server crepes.fr) and server (S, Bob's server hamburger.edu) exchange text commands and three-digit replies:

S: 220 hamburger.edu
C: HELO crepes.fr
S: 250 Hello crepes.fr, pleased to meet you
C: MAIL FROM: <alice@crepes.fr>
S: 250 alice@crepes.fr ... Sender ok
C: RCPT TO: <bob@hamburger.edu>
S: 250 bob@hamburger.edu ... Recipient ok
C: DATA
S: 354 Enter mail, end with "." on a line by itself
C: Do you like ketchup?
C: How about pickles?
C: .
S: 250 Message accepted for delivery
C: QUIT
S: 221 hamburger.edu closing connection

The three phases are handshaking (HELO, sender and recipient), transfer of the message (DATA, ending with a line containing only a full stop) and closure (QUIT). SMTP uses persistent connections: if Alice's server has several messages for the same server, it sends them all on the same connection, repeating MAIL FROM onwards for each.

SMTP compared with HTTP

SMTPHTTP
DirectionPush: the sending server pushes the file to the receiving serverPull: the client pulls the object from the server
Who opens TCPThe machine that wants to sendThe machine that wants to receive
Body encodingMust be 7-bit ASCII; binary (images) must be encodedNo such restriction
Multiple objectsAll parts of a message (text + attachments) in one messageEach object in its own response message
Port2580

Mail message format and MIME

A message (RFC 5322) has a header and a body, separated by a blank line. The header must contain From: and To: lines and may contain Subject: and others:

From: alice@crepes.fr
To: bob@hamburger.edu
Subject: Searching for the meaning of life.

(body of the message in ASCII)

These header lines are part of the message itself; they are different from the SMTP commands (MAIL FROM, RCPT TO), which belong to the protocol dialogue.

Because SMTP carries only 7-bit ASCII, Forouzan describes MIME (Multipurpose Internet Mail Extensions), a supplementary protocol that lets non-ASCII data (other languages, images, audio, video, programs) be sent through SMTP. MIME transforms non-ASCII data at the sender into ASCII, and back at the receiver. It adds header lines:

MIME headerPurpose
MIME-Version: 1.0Says the message uses MIME
Content-Type: image/jpegThe type and subtype of the body (text/plain, image/jpeg, multipart/mixed, ...)
Content-Transfer-Encoding: base64How the body was encoded into ASCII (7bit, 8bit, binary, base64, quoted-printable)
Content-Id, Content-DescriptionIdentify and describe the body part

Mail access protocols: how Bob reads his mail

Bob's user agent can't use SMTP to fetch messages, because getting mail is a pull operation and SMTP is a push protocol. Other protocols do the retrieval.

POP3 (Post Office Protocol version 3), port 110 (Forouzan)

POP3 is simple and limited. The user agent opens a TCP connection to the mail server on port 110 and goes through three phases:

  1. Authorisation: the agent sends the user name and password (user, pass).
  2. Transaction: the agent retrieves messages (list, retr) and can mark them for deletion (dele).
  3. Update: after the client sends quit, the server deletes the marked messages and the session ends.

POP3 has two modes. In download-and-delete mode, messages are deleted from the server after they are retrieved, so a user who reads mail on a laptop can't read it again on another device. In download-and-keep mode, the messages stay on the server after download.

IMAP (Internet Message Access Protocol), port 143 (Kurose and Forouzan)

IMAP keeps messages on the server and lets the user manipulate them there: create folders on the server, move messages between folders, search them, and fetch only part of a message (for example just the headers, useful on a slow link). IMAP keeps user state across sessions (folder names and which message is in which folder), so the view is the same on every device. Most mail clients today use IMAP.

HTTP (webmail)

With Gmail or Yahoo in a browser, the user agent is the web browser, and it talks to the remote mailbox via HTTP. The browser uses HTTP to send messages to the mail server too. The mail servers still use SMTP between themselves.
