FeaturePOP3IMAP
Where mail livesDownloaded to the device (and usually deleted from the server)Stays on the server
Folders on serverNoYes
Multiple devicesPoor: each device downloads separatelyGood: all devices see the same, synchronised mailbox
Partial downloadNo, whole messagesYes, headers or parts
Server storage and complexityLowHigher
Works offlineYes, after downloadNeeds a connection (clients cache copies)
Port110143
ParadigmDownload and (optionally) deleteOnline, server-side mailbox management
From a paperFrom Gmail to Yahoo 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q6
Given

You send mail from your Gmail account to a friend's Yahoo account.

Required

(a) The protocol at each step: you → Gmail server → Yahoo server → your friend. (b) The role of each.

Formula

Push with SMTP (or HTTP for webmail), relay with SMTP, pull with IMAP/POP3/HTTP.

Substitution

Step 1 depends on the user agent: the Gmail web page uses HTTP; a mail app uses SMTP. Step 2 is always SMTP. Step 3 is IMAP or POP3 for an app, or HTTP for the Yahoo web page.

Calculation

Step 2 also needs DNS: Gmail's server queries DNS for the MX record of yahoo.com to find the Yahoo mail server's name and address, then opens TCP to its port 25.

Final answer

(a) You → Gmail: HTTP (webmail) or SMTP (mail app). Gmail → Yahoo: SMTP over TCP port 25 (after a DNS MX lookup). Yahoo → friend: IMAP (143) or POP3 (110), or HTTP for webmail. (b) HTTP/SMTP from the agent submits (pushes) the message to the sender's server; SMTP pushes it between servers and handles retries; IMAP/POP3/HTTP let the recipient pull and manage the mail in the mailbox.

From a paperSame server or not? 19CSE301 End 2024 Q7

Mail from alice@cb.amrita.edu to bob@am.amrita.edu. Is the message sent and received on the same server?

Given

Two different domains: cb.amrita.edu and am.amrita.edu.

Required

Whether one server handles both, and the SMTP messages exchanged.

Formula

The receiving server is whatever host the recipient domain's MX record names.

Substitution

Alice's server looks up the MX record for am.amrita.edu.

Calculation

If both domains' MX records point to the same physical mail server, the server simply moves the message from its queue to Bob's mailbox (no SMTP between servers is needed). If they point to different servers (the normal case for different domains), Alice's server opens TCP port 25 to Bob's server and runs: 220 greeting, HELO, 250, MAIL FROM, 250, RCPT TO, 250, DATA, 354, message lines, ".", 250, QUIT, 221.

Final answer

Generally no: different domains have their own mail servers, found through DNS MX records, and SMTP carries the message between them. It is the same server only if the organisation configures both domains on one server. Sending always ends in Bob's mailbox; receiving (reading) uses IMAP, POP3 or HTTP.

Exam focus
  • Email protocols appeared in 6 papers. Draw the Alice→Bob figure and label each hop's protocol.
  • SMTP is push; IMAP and POP3 are pull. SMTP: port 25, TCP; IMAP 143; POP3 110 (from Chapter2-Problems).
  • POP3 vs IMAP comparison (20CYS303 End 2025 Q1), and POP3's download-and-delete vs download-and-keep (19CCE304 End 2024 Q2(B)).
  • The SMTP command sequence (HELO, MAIL FROM, RCPT TO, DATA, QUIT) and reply codes 220, 250, 354, 221.
  • 20CYS303 Mid 2023 Q3: the end-to-end delivery of a mail with all protocols and their roles.
Common mistakes
  • Saying the recipient uses SMTP to read mail. SMTP only pushes; retrieval uses IMAP, POP3 or HTTP.
  • Forgetting the DNS MX lookup between the two mail servers.
  • Confusing the From: header line (part of the message) with the MAIL FROM command (part of SMTP).
Quick revision
  • Components: user agents, mail servers (mailboxes, queues), SMTP.
  • SMTP: TCP port 25, push, 7-bit ASCII, persistent, phases handshake/transfer/closure.
  • Message = header lines (From, To, Subject) + blank line + body; MIME for non-ASCII.
  • Access: POP3 (110, authorisation/transaction/update, download-and-delete or keep), IMAP (143, server folders, sync), HTTP (webmail).

I.13 · Application layer

DNS: the Internet's directory service

How names such as www.amrita.edu become IP addresses: why DNS is distributed and hierarchical, the root, TLD, authoritative and local servers, iterative and recursive queries, caching, resource records and how a new domain is registered.

Sources: Kurose §2.4 · Chapter_2_v8.2 slides 61–78 · Chapter2-Problems

The problem DNS solves

People prefer names (www.amrita.edu); routers need numbers (IP addresses such as 104.21.37.56, a 32-bit quantity in IPv4 with a hierarchical structure that routers can process). Something must translate between them. That is the Domain Name System (DNS), which is:

  1. a distributed database implemented in a hierarchy of DNS servers, and
  2. an application-layer protocol that lets hosts query that database.

DNS servers are often UNIX machines running BIND. DNS runs over UDP on port 53. Other application protocols (HTTP, SMTP, FTP) use DNS to translate user-supplied host names into IP addresses, so DNS adds delay, sometimes substantial, before the real communication can begin. Caching helps a lot.

The services DNS provides

ServiceWhat it meansExample
Host name to IP address translationThe main jobwww.amrita.edu → 104.21.37.56
Host aliasingA host with a complicated canonical name can have simpler alias names; DNS returns the canonical name and addresswww.enterprise.com is an alias for relay1.west-coast.enterprise.com
Mail server aliasingEmail addresses use simple names; DNS (MX records) finds the real mail serverbob@yahoo.com → relay1.west-coast.yahoo.com
Load distributionA busy site with many replicated servers has a set of IP addresses for one name; DNS returns the whole set but rotates the order, spreading trafficcnn.com has several addresses

Why not one central DNS server?

A single server holding every mapping would be simple, but it would fail badly:

  • Single point of failure: if it crashes, the whole Internet stops resolving names.
  • Traffic volume: one server would have to handle every DNS query from hundreds of millions of hosts.
  • Distant centralised database: it would be far from most clients, causing long delays.
  • Maintenance: one database would be huge and need updating for every new host.

In one phrase: a centralised database doesn't scale. So DNS is distributed by design.

A distributed, hierarchical database

  • Root DNS servers: more than 1,000 root server instances spread over the world, copies of 13 different root servers, managed by 12 organisations and coordinated by IANA. A root server returns the IP addresses of TLD servers.
  • Top-level domain (TLD) servers: one cluster for each top-level domain: com, org, net, edu, gov, and country domains such as in, uk, fr. Verisign runs the .com TLD; Educause runs .edu. A TLD server returns the IP addresses of authoritative servers.
  • Authoritative DNS servers: every organisation with publicly accessible hosts (web and mail servers) must provide publicly accessible DNS records mapping their names to addresses. An organisation runs its own authoritative server or pays a provider to.
  • Local DNS server: not strictly part of the hierarchy, but central to it. Each ISP (a university, a company, a residential ISP) has one, also called the default name server. When a host connects (via DHCP, Topic II.17), it learns the address of its local DNS server. When the host makes a DNS query, the query goes to the local DNS server, which acts as a proxy, forwarding the query into the hierarchy.

Iterative and recursive queries

Suppose the host cse.nyu.edu wants the IP address of gaia.cs.umass.edu. NYU's local DNS server is dns.nyu.edu and the authoritative server for gaia is dns.umass.edu.

Iterative query

Recursive query

  • In an iterative query, the contacted server replies with the address of the next server to ask (“I don't know, but ask this one”), and the local DNS server does the asking. Messages 2–7 above are iterative.
  • In a recursive query, the contacted server takes on the job: it asks the next server itself and returns the final answer. The query from host to local DNS server (message 1) is recursive.
  • Both examples need 8 messages. In practice, the query from the requesting host to the local DNS server is recursive and the rest are iterative.
IterativeRecursive
A server replies with a referral to the next serverA server obtains the full answer on the client's behalf
The local DNS server does the work of asking each levelEach level asks the next, so the burden moves up the hierarchy
Root and TLD servers stay lightly loaded, so this is the norm for themPuts heavy load on root and TLD servers, so they usually refuse it
Typical between the local DNS server and the hierarchyTypical between a host and its local DNS server
Interactive visualizationDNS resolution, step by stepAnimate a lookup in iterative or recursive mode, then repeat it to see the local DNS cache answer instantly. Change the domain and the RTTs and watch the delay add up.
Open simulator →

DNS caching

DNS makes heavy use of caching to cut delay and the number of DNS messages. When a DNS server receives a reply (a mapping of a host name to an address), it stores the mapping in its local memory. If another query for the same name arrives, it answers from the cache, even if it is not authoritative for that name. Cached entries are discarded after a period, usually two days, set by the record's TTL.

A local DNS server can also cache the addresses of TLD servers, so it can bypass the root servers for most queries; root servers are rarely visited.

Resource records

The DNS servers store resource records (RRs). A resource record is a four-tuple: (Name, Value, Type, TTL). TTL is the time to live (when the record should be removed from caches). The meaning of Name and Value depends on Type.

TypeNameValueExample
Aa host nameits IP address(relay1.bar.foo.com, 145.37.93.126, A)
NSa domainthe host name of an authoritative DNS server for it(foo.com, dns.foo.com, NS)
CNAMEan alias host namethe canonical host name(foo.com, relay1.bar.foo.com, CNAME)
MXan alias namethe canonical name of a mail server(foo.com, mail.bar.foo.com, MX)
  • An authoritative server for a host holds that host's type A record.
  • A server that is not authoritative for a host holds a type NS record for the domain, and a type A record giving the address of the DNS server named in the NS record. For example, an edu TLD server holds (umass.edu, dns.umass.edu, NS) and (dns.umass.edu, 128.119.40.111, A).
  • MX records let a company use the same alias for its mail server and for another server (such as a web server): a query for the mail server asks for the MX record; a query for the other server asks for the CNAME record.

DNS messages

Queries and replies have the same format: a 12-byte header followed by four sections.

PartContents
HeaderA 16-bit identification number (copied into the reply so the client matches replies to queries) and flags: query or reply, authoritative answer, recursion desired, recursion available
QuestionThe name being queried and the type (A, MX, ...)
AnswerThe resource records for the name (possibly several A records for a replicated server)
AuthorityRecords of other authoritative servers
AdditionalOther helpful records, such as the type A record for a mail server named in an MX answer

Registering a domain

Suppose you start a company, Network Utopia. You register networkutopia.com with a registrar (a commercial organisation accredited by ICANN that checks the name is unique and enters it into the DNS database). You give the registrar the names and IP addresses of your primary and secondary authoritative servers, say dns1.networkutopia.com (212.212.212.1). The registrar makes sure a type NS and a type A record are entered into the .com TLD servers:

  • (networkutopia.com, dns1.networkutopia.com, NS)
  • (dns1.networkutopia.com, 212.212.212.1, A)

You also put into your own authoritative server a type A record for your web server www.networkutopia.com and a type MX record for your mail server.

From a paperRecords for a startup 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q8

Company foo has DNS servers dns1.foo.com (140.112.30.40) and dns2.foo.com (140.112.30.44); a web server foo.com at 140.112.30.55 and 140.112.30.56, also named www.foo.com; and a mail server galaxy.foo.com (140.112.30.60). Emails are username@foo.com.

Given

The servers and addresses above. Write records as (name, value, type).

Required

(a) RRs given to the .com registrar; (b) RRs in the company's own DNS server.

Formula

TLD holds NS + A (glue) for the domain's name servers. Authoritative server holds A, CNAME, MX for the company's hosts.

Substitution

Two name servers, two web addresses, one alias, one mail server.

Calculation

(a) (foo.com, dns1.foo.com, NS); (dns1.foo.com, 140.112.30.40, A); (foo.com, dns2.foo.com, NS); (dns2.foo.com, 140.112.30.44, A). (b) (foo.com, 140.112.30.55, A); (foo.com, 140.112.30.56, A); (www.foo.com, foo.com, CNAME); (foo.com, galaxy.foo.com, MX); (galaxy.foo.com, 140.112.30.60, A); plus the server's own (foo.com, dns1.foo.com, NS), (foo.com, dns2.foo.com, NS) and their A records.

Final answer

The registrar gets only the NS records and the name servers' A records. The company's server holds the two A records for the web server (load distribution), the CNAME for the www alias, and the MX plus A record for mail.

From a paperMedilab's TLD records 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q7(A)
Given

Website www.medilab.org on 140.25.10.20 and 140.25.10.22; authoritative server dns1.medilab.org at 140.25.10.5.

Required

The records the .org TLD server keeps for this scenario.

Formula

A TLD server keeps NS records for each domain and A (glue) records for those name servers. It does not keep the web server's A records.

Substitution

Domain medilab.org; name server dns1.medilab.org.

Calculation

(medilab.org, dns1.medilab.org, NS) and (dns1.medilab.org, 140.25.10.5, A).

Final answer

Exactly those two records. The A records for www.medilab.org (140.25.10.20 and 140.25.10.22, plus perhaps a CNAME) live on dns1.medilab.org, the authoritative server.

DNS over UDP, and lost queries

DNS uses UDP because a query and a reply are each a single short message: there is no connection setup delay and no connection state at busy servers. But UDP doesn't recover lost packets.

From a paper 19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q3

“DNS uses UDP; if a DNS packet is lost there is no automatic recovery. Is that a problem, and how is it solved?” It would be a problem if nothing were done, but the DNS client (resolver) itself sets a timer: if no reply arrives within a timeout, it retransmits the query, possibly to another (secondary) DNS server. Reliability is built into the application instead of the transport layer. Caching also reduces the number of queries that can be lost. (Large responses and zone transfers between servers use TCP.)

DNS security

DNS is a critical piece of infrastructure. Attacks include DDoS bandwidth-flooding of root servers (largely unsuccessful because of packet filtering and because local servers cache TLD addresses), DDoS on TLD servers (potentially more dangerous), and spoofing / cache poisoning, where an attacker sends bogus replies that a DNS server caches. DNSSEC adds authentication to DNS records to defend against spoofing.

Exam focus
  • 19CSE301 End 2023 Q2(a): iterative and recursive resolution of aums.amrita.edu with a neat flow diagram (7 marks). Draw both diagrams with 8 numbered messages and name each server.
  • 19CSE301 End 2023 Q2(b): NS and A records in a TLD for two authoritative servers (prithvi and varuna): (amritanet.edu, prithvi.amritanet.edu, NS), (prithvi.amritanet.edu, IP₁, A), (amritanet.edu, varuna.amritanet.edu, NS), (varuna.amritanet.edu, IP₂, A).
  • Know all four record types with an example each, and why DNS is not centralised.
  • DNS delay appears in every HTTP timing question (Topic I.10).
Common mistakes
  • Putting the web server's A record in the TLD server. The TLD holds only NS and glue A records.
  • Saying the root server returns the final IP address. It returns TLD server addresses.
  • Forgetting that the local DNS server is not part of the hierarchy, but every query goes through it.
Quick revision
  • DNS: distributed hierarchical database + protocol over UDP 53.
  • Services: name→address, host aliasing, mail aliasing, load distribution.
  • Hierarchy: root (13 servers, 1000+ instances) → TLD → authoritative; local DNS acts as proxy.
  • Host→local: recursive; local→hierarchy: iterative; 8 messages in the textbook example.
  • Caching with TTL (≈2 days); local servers cache TLD addresses.
  • RR = (Name, Value, Type, TTL): A, NS, CNAME, MX.

I.14 · Application layer

Peer-to-peer file distribution

Why distributing a big file from one server gets slower with every client, why P2P doesn't, the two minimum-distribution-time formulas, and how BitTorrent trades chunks.

Sources: Kurose §2.5 · Chapter_2_v8.2 slides 79–86 · Chapter2-Problems

The problem

A server must send a large file (a software update, a video) to a large number of hosts. In client-server distribution the server must send a copy to every client, so it carries an enormous burden and its upload rate becomes the limit. In P2P distribution each peer can redistribute any portion of the file it has already received to other peers, helping the server.

The model

  • \(F\) = file size in bits; \(N\) = number of peers that want a copy.
  • \(u_s\) = the server's upload rate; \(u_i\) = the upload rate of peer \(i\); \(d_i\) = the download rate of peer \(i\); \(d_{min} = \min_i d_i\).
  • The Internet core has abundant bandwidth, so all bottlenecks are in access networks.
  • The distribution time is the time for all \(N\) peers to get a complete copy.

Client-server distribution time

  1. The server must upload \(N\) copies, \(NF\) bits in total, at rate \(u_s\). So it needs at least \(NF/u_s\).
  2. The peer with the lowest download rate can't receive the file in less than \(F/d_{min}\).
Client-server

\[ D_{cs} \ge \max\left\{ \frac{NF}{u_s},\ \frac{F}{d_{min}} \right\} \]

For large \(N\), \(D_{cs}\) grows linearly with \(N\).

P2P distribution time

  1. At the start only the server has the file, so it must send every bit at least once: at least \(F/u_s\).
  2. The slowest peer still needs \(F/d_{min}\).
  3. In total \(NF\) bits must be delivered, and the total upload capacity of the system is the server's plus every peer's: \(u_s + \sum u_i\). So at least \(NF / (u_s + \sum u_i)\).
P2P

\[ D_{P2P} \ge \max\left\{ \frac{F}{u_s},\ \frac{F}{d_{min}},\ \frac{NF}{u_s + \sum_{i=1}^{N} u_i} \right\} \]

Each new peer adds both demand (\(F\) bits) and capacity (\(u_i\)), so \(D_{P2P}\) stays bounded as \(N\) grows: this is self-scalability.

Interactive visualizationClient-server vs P2P calculatorChange F, N, the server's upload rate and the peers' rates; the solver shows every bound, which one wins, and a graph of both times as N grows.
Open simulator →

Worked numericals

Level 1Practice
Given

\(F = 1\) Gbit, \(u_s = 100\) Mbps, \(N = 20\) peers, each with \(d_i = 50\) Mbps and \(u_i = 10\) Mbps.

Required

\(D_{cs}\) and \(D_{P2P}\).

Formula

As above.

Substitution

\(F = 1{,}000\) Mbit. \(NF = 20{,}000\) Mbit. \(\sum u_i = 200\) Mbps.

Calculation

\(D_{cs} = \max(20{,}000/100,\ 1{,}000/50) = \max(200, 20) = 200\) s. \(D_{P2P} = \max(1{,}000/100,\ 20,\ 20{,}000/300) = \max(10, 20, 66.7) = 66.7\) s.

Final answer

200 s client-server; 66.7 s P2P, three times faster.

From a paper1000 MB to 10 peers 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q2(A)
Given

\(u_s = 5\) Mbps; \(F = 1000\) MB; \(N = 10\); \(d_{min} = 2\) Mbps; each \(u_i = 1\) Mbps.

Required

The minimum P2P distribution time.

Formula

\( D_{P2P} = \max\{F/u_s,\ F/d_{min},\ NF/(u_s + \sum u_i)\} \)

Substitution

\(F = 1000 \times 10^6 \times 8 = 8 \times 10^9\) bits = 8,000 Mbit. \(\sum u_i = 10 \times 1 = 10\) Mbps.

Calculation

\(F/u_s = 8{,}000/5 = 1{,}600\) s. \(F/d_{min} = 8{,}000/2 = 4{,}000\) s. \(NF/(u_s + \sum u_i) = 80{,}000/15 = 5{,}333.3\) s.

Final answer

\(D_{P2P} = 5{,}333.3\) s ≈ 88.9 minutes, limited by the total upload capacity. (Client-server would take \(\max(80{,}000/5,\ 4{,}000) = 16{,}000\) s ≈ 4.4 hours.)

From a paperThe full comparison table 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q6
Given

\(F = 10\) Gbits; \(u_s = 1\) Gbps; \(d_i = 200\) Mbps; \(N = 10, 100, 1000\); \(u = 2\) Mbps and 10 Mbps.

Required

Minimum distribution times for both architectures for every combination.

Formula

\(D_{cs} = \max(NF/u_s, F/d)\); \(D_{P2P} = \max(F/u_s, F/d, NF/(u_s + Nu))\).

Substitution

\(F = 10{,}000\) Mbit, \(u_s = 1{,}000\) Mbps. \(F/u_s = 10\) s; \(F/d = 50\) s.

Calculation

Client-server (independent of u): N = 10: max(100, 50) = 100 s; N = 100: 1,000 s; N = 1,000: 10,000 s. P2P with u = 2 Mbps: N = 10: \(100{,}000/1{,}020 = 98.0\) s; N = 100: \(10^6/1{,}200 = 833.3\) s; N = 1,000: \(10^7/3{,}000 = 3{,}333.3\) s. P2P with u = 10 Mbps: N = 10: \(100{,}000/1{,}100 = 90.9\) s; N = 100: \(10^6/2{,}000 = 500\) s; N = 1,000: \(10^7/11{,}000 = 909.1\) s.

Final answer

See the table below. Client-server grows tenfold with each tenfold N; P2P grows far more slowly, and faster peer uploads help a lot.

NClient-server, u = 2 or 10 MbpsP2P, u = 2 MbpsP2P, u = 10 Mbps
10100 s98.0 s90.9 s
1001,000 s833.3 s500 s
1,00010,000 s3,333.3 s909.1 s

BitTorrent

BitTorrent is the most popular P2P file-distribution protocol.

  • The collection of all peers taking part in distributing a particular file is a torrent.
  • Peers download equal-sized chunks of the file from each other (typically 256 KB).
  • Each torrent has an infrastructure node called a tracker. A peer joining the torrent registers with the tracker and periodically tells it that it is still there. The tracker sends the new peer a random subset of the participating peers' addresses (say 50); the peer opens TCP connections to them (its neighbours).
  • Which chunks to request: rarest first. A peer asks each neighbour for its list of chunks, then requests the chunks that are rarest among its neighbours, so rare chunks get copied quickly and the copies of every chunk even out.
  • Which neighbours to send to: tit-for-tat. A peer gives priority to the neighbours that are currently supplying it data at the highest rate. Every 10 seconds it picks the top four; these are unchoked (it sends them chunks). Every 30 seconds it also picks one extra neighbour at random and sends it chunks: this one is optimistically unchoked. All other neighbours are choked (they receive nothing from this peer).
  • Optimistic unchoking lets new peers (with nothing to trade yet) get started, and lets a peer discover better trading partners. Over time, peers that upload at compatible rates find each other.
  • A peer may leave once it has the whole file, or stay on to upload (a seeder); some peers leave with only part of it.
From a paper 20CYS303 Mid 2023 Q2

“How are files shared in BitTorrent? What do choked, unchoked and optimistically unchoked mean?” Answer with the tracker → neighbours → rarest-first chunks → tit-for-tat sequence above, and define the three states: unchoked = among the top four uploaders to me, so I upload to them; optimistically unchoked = one random neighbour I upload to every 30 s regardless; choked = everyone else.

Common mistakes
  • Forgetting the \(F/u_s\) term in the P2P formula: the server must send at least one full copy.
  • Using \(N\) peers' upload in the client-server formula. Peers don't upload in client-server.
  • Mixing bytes and bits in \(F\).
Quick revision
  • \(D_{cs} \ge \max\{NF/u_s, F/d_{min}\}\), linear in N.
  • \(D_{P2P} \ge \max\{F/u_s, F/d_{min}, NF/(u_s + \sum u_i)\}\), bounded: self-scalability.
  • BitTorrent: torrent, tracker, 256 KB chunks, rarest first, tit-for-tat (top 4 every 10 s, 1 optimistic unchoke every 30 s).

I.15 · Application layer

Video streaming

What a compressed video stream is, why streaming over the Internet is hard, how playout buffering hides delay jitter, and how DASH adapts the quality to the bandwidth.

Sources: Kurose §2.6.1–2.6.2 · Chapter_2_v8.2 slides 87–96

Video is the Internet's biggest load

Streamed video (Netflix, YouTube, Hotstar) is by far the largest share of Internet traffic. To stream well, you need to understand three things: what video data looks like, why the network makes streaming hard, and how clients cope.

Properties of video

  • A video is a sequence of images, displayed at a constant rate, for example 24 or 30 images (frames) per second.
  • An uncompressed digital image is an array of pixels, each encoded with bits for luminance and colour.
  • Video can be compressed, trading quality for bit rate, by exploiting two kinds of redundancy:
    • spatial redundancy, within one image (a large area of blue sky can be sent as “N pixels of this colour” instead of N separate values);
    • temporal redundancy, from one image to the next (if frame i+1 is almost the same as frame i, send only the differences).
  • CBR (constant bit rate): the encoding rate is fixed. VBR (variable bit rate): the rate changes as the amount of spatial and temporal change varies.
  • Typical rates: around 100 kbps for low-quality video, over 3 Mbps for HD, and over 10 Mbps for 4K streaming. The same video is usually stored in several versions at different rates.

Higher bit rate gives better quality but needs more average end-to-end throughput; the main performance measure for streaming is average throughput, which must be at least the video's bit rate for continuous playout.

Why streaming is hard

  • Bandwidth varies over time as other traffic comes and goes, with congestion in the home network, the access network or the core.
  • Packet loss and delay (jitter): packets arrive with varying delays, and lost packets are retransmitted (by TCP) after a delay.
  • Heterogeneity: some users are on fast wired links, others on 4G with poor reception.

The basic requirement is the continuous playout constraint: once the client begins playing, playback must follow the original timing, so data must arrive in time to be played.

Playout buffering

The client doesn't start playing the moment the first bytes arrive. It buffers a few seconds of video first, then plays from the buffer while more data keeps arriving. The playout delay absorbs the variation in network delay: as long as the buffer doesn't run dry, the viewer sees smooth video even when the network briefly slows.

PracticeHow long must the client buffer?
Given

A 10-minute video encoded at a constant 4 Mbps. The connection delivers a steady 3 Mbps.

Required

The minimum initial buffering (playout) delay \(t_0\) that avoids any stall.

Formula

By playback time \(t\) after the start, the client has received \(3(t_0 + t)\) Mbit and needs \(4t\) Mbit. Require \(3(t_0 + t) \ge 4t\) for every \(t\) up to 600 s.

Substitution

\(3t_0 \ge t\) must hold at the worst point, \(t = 600\) s.

Calculation

\(t_0 \ge 600/3 = 200\) s.

Final answer

The client must wait 200 s before playing. That is unacceptable, which is why real players instead switch to a lower bit-rate version (adaptive streaming) when throughput is below the encoding rate.

HTTP streaming

In HTTP streaming the video is stored on an HTTP server as an ordinary file with a URL. The client opens a TCP connection, sends a GET, and the server sends the video file as fast as TCP allows. The client buffers the bytes; once the buffer passes a threshold, it starts playing, taking frames from the buffer and decompressing them.

The weakness: every client receives the same encoding, whatever its available bandwidth, even though bandwidth differs hugely between clients and changes over time for the same client.

DASH: Dynamic Adaptive Streaming over HTTP

In DASH, the video is encoded into several versions, each with a different bit rate and quality level, and each version is cut into chunks (a few seconds each), each with its own URL.

1. ManifestThe client first requests a manifest file, which lists the URL and bit rate of every version.
2. MeasureThe client measures the throughput it is receiving as it downloads.
3. ChooseFor each next chunk, a rate-determination algorithm picks the highest-quality version the current bandwidth can sustain.
4. RequestThe client sends an HTTP GET for that chunk; it can switch version at every chunk.

With DASH, “intelligence” sits at the client. The client decides:

  • when to request a chunk (so the buffer neither starves nor overflows),
  • what encoding rate to request (higher quality when more bandwidth is available), and
  • where to request it from (a nearby, uncongested server, which is where CDNs come in).
PracticeChoosing a DASH chunk
Given

The manifest lists versions at 0.5, 1, 2.5, 5 and 8 Mbps. The client measured 3.2 Mbps over the last chunks, and its buffer is healthy.

Required

Which version to request next, and what changes if throughput falls to 1.4 Mbps.

Formula

Choose the highest bit rate that does not exceed the measured throughput (real players add a safety margin).

Substitution

Versions ≤ 3.2 Mbps: 0.5, 1, 2.5.

Calculation

Highest is 2.5 Mbps. At 1.4 Mbps, versions ≤ 1.4 are 0.5 and 1.

Final answer

Request the 2.5 Mbps chunk now; switch down to the 1 Mbps version when throughput drops to 1.4 Mbps, so the buffer doesn't drain and playback never stalls.

Plain HTTP streamingDASH
One encoding for everyoneMany encodings, chosen per chunk
The whole file at one URLChunks, each with a URL, listed in a manifest
Stalls when bandwidth drops below the video rateSwitches to a lower rate instead of stalling
No client decisionsThe client decides when, what rate and where
Exam focus
  • 20CYS303 End 2023 Q8: “How does DASH help HTTP streaming?” (5 marks). Describe versions, chunks, the manifest, client throughput measurement and per-chunk rate selection, and contrast with plain HTTP streaming.
  • Define spatial and temporal redundancy, CBR and VBR.
  • Explain why a playout buffer is needed.
Quick revision
  • Video = images at a constant rate; compression uses spatial and temporal redundancy; CBR vs VBR.
  • Challenges: varying bandwidth, loss, jitter, heterogeneity; continuous playout constraint.
  • Playout buffer absorbs delay variation.
  • DASH: versions, chunks, manifest; the client picks rate per chunk (when, what, where).

I.16 · Application layer

Content distribution networks

Why one giant data centre can't serve the world's video, how CDNs place servers (enter deep vs bring home), how DNS redirects you to a nearby server, how a cluster is chosen, and how Netflix and YouTube do it.

Sources: Kurose §2.6.3–2.6.4 · Chapter_2_v8.2 slides 97–103, 122–123

The problem

How can one company stream content to millions of users at once? The simplest idea, one huge data centre, fails for three reasons:

  1. Distance: if the client is far from the data centre, packets cross many links and ISPs; if any of them has low throughput, the video stalls (throughput is the bottleneck link's rate).
  2. Waste: a popular video is sent many times over the same links, so the company pays its ISP repeatedly for the same bytes.
  3. Single point of failure: if the data centre or its links go down, nobody gets any video.

What a CDN is

A content distribution network (CDN) manages servers in many geographically distributed locations, stores copies of the videos (and other web content, such as documents and images) on them, and directs each user request to a CDN location that will give the best user experience.

  • A private CDN is owned by the content provider itself: Google's CDN distributes YouTube videos; Netflix has Open Connect.
  • A third-party CDN distributes content for many content providers: Akamai, Limelight and Level-3.
From a paper 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q2(B)

“Define the role of a CDN on the Internet” (2 marks). A CDN stores copies of content on servers distributed close to users and redirects each request to a nearby, lightly loaded server, which reduces delay and stalls for users, cuts repeated traffic over long-haul links and provider costs, and removes the single point of failure of one central data centre.

Two server-placement philosophies

Enter deep (Akamai)Bring home (Limelight and others)
Deploy server clusters in access ISPs, all over the worldBuild large clusters at a smaller number of sites, typically at IXPs
Gets close to end users: fewer links and routers between user and server, so lower delay and higher throughputFewer sites, so much lower maintenance and management overhead
Very many clusters are hard to maintain and manageSomewhat higher delay and lower throughput for users

Once clusters are in place, the CDN replicates content across them. It may not keep every video in every cluster: some videos are rarely viewed in some countries. Many CDNs pull content (like a web cache): if a client requests a video the cluster doesn't have, the cluster fetches it from a central repository or another cluster, stores a copy and streams it; when storage fills, it removes videos that aren't requested often. Others push content to clusters ahead of time.

How a CDN intercepts your request (DNS redirection)

Suppose the content provider NetCinema uses the third-party CDN KingCDN. A NetCinema web page contains a link to a video at http://video.netcinema.com/6Y7B23V.

  1. The user visits NetCinema's web page.
  2. When the user clicks the video link, the host sends a DNS query for video.netcinema.com.
  3. The user's local DNS server relays the query to an authoritative DNS server for NetCinema, which sees the string “video” and, instead of an IP address, returns a host name in KingCDN's domain, such as a1105.kingcdn.com.
  4. From this point the query enters KingCDN's private DNS infrastructure: the local DNS server sends a second query, now for a1105.kingcdn.com, and KingCDN's DNS system eventually returns the IP address of a KingCDN content server.
  5. The local DNS server passes that address to the user's host.
  6. The client opens a TCP connection directly to that server and sends an HTTP GET for the video (with DASH, first the manifest, then chunks of different versions).

Choosing a cluster

The core of a CDN is its cluster selection strategy: dynamically directing clients to a server cluster or data centre. The CDN learns the IP address of the client's local DNS server through the DNS lookup, then chooses a cluster based on that address.

  • Geographically closest: map the local DNS server's address to a location and pick the cluster with the fewest kilometres to it. Works reasonably for most clients, but can fail: the nearest cluster in kilometres may be far in network hops; some users use a local DNS server that is far away from them; and it ignores how delay and bandwidth change over time.
  • Real-time measurements: clusters periodically send probes (ping messages or DNS queries) to local DNS servers around the world to measure delay and loss, and choose the best-performing cluster.
  • IP anycast (an alternative): all clusters advertise the same IP address through BGP, and routing delivers each client to the nearest one.
From a paper 19CCE304 Mid 2023 Q3

Besides delay, loss and bandwidth, what else goes into a CDN's server-selection strategy? Load balancing (don't send clients to an overloaded cluster), diurnal effects (load follows the time of day in each region), variations across DNS servers within one network (clients sharing a far-away local DNS server), limited availability of rarely watched videos (the chosen cluster must actually hold the content, or be able to pull it quickly), the need to relieve hot spots, and the provider's delivery cost (traffic through some ISPs costs more than others).

Case studies

Netflix

Netflix runs its website, user accounts, recommendations and video encoding (creating many versions of each film for DASH) in Amazon's cloud. To deliver video it built its own private CDN, Open Connect: server racks installed in IXPs and inside residential ISPs, in many countries. Netflix uses push caching: during off-peak hours it pushes the videos each region is likely to watch into the racks there, rather than pulling them on demand. When a user picks a film, Netflix's software in the cloud tells the client which local CDN servers hold copies, and the client streams the film with DASH.

YouTube

YouTube uses Google's private CDN, with server clusters in many IXP and ISP locations. Google uses pull caching (like a web cache) and DNS redirection, and usually sends a client to the cluster with the lowest RTT, sometimes redirecting to another cluster for load balancing. YouTube uses HTTP streaming, offering a small number of versions of each video with different quality and letting the user (or a simple algorithm) choose.

NetflixYouTube
CDNPrivate (Open Connect)Private (Google)
Server sitesIXPs and inside residential ISPsIXPs and ISP locations
ReplicationPush during off-peak hoursPull on demand
StreamingDASHHTTP streaming, a few versions
Common mistakes
  • Saying the content provider's DNS returns the CDN server's IP address. It returns a CNAME in the CDN's domain; the CDN's own DNS returns the address.
  • Assuming geographic closeness means best performance. Network distance, load and the local DNS server's position matter.
Quick revision
  • One data centre fails on distance, repeated traffic and single point of failure.
  • CDN = many distributed servers holding copies; private (Google, Netflix) or third-party (Akamai).
  • Enter deep (many clusters in access ISPs) vs bring home (few big clusters at IXPs).
  • DNS redirection: provider's DNS returns a CNAME in the CDN domain; CDN DNS returns a nearby server.
  • Cluster selection: geographically closest, real-time measurements, IP anycast; plus load and availability.
  • Netflix: Open Connect, push caching, DASH. YouTube: Google CDN, pull caching.

Unit II

Transport Layer and Network Layer

How data is delivered reliably between processes over an unreliable network (UDP, reliable data transfer, TCP, congestion control), and how datagrams find their way across routers (router architecture, IP, addressing, routing).

This unit carries the heaviest exam weight. Every end-semester paper has a TCP congestion-control question and a routing-algorithm question, and RDT, Go-Back-N/Selective Repeat and subnetting appear in most papers. Read the RDT topics slowly and use the simulators: every later idea in TCP is built from them.

II.1 · Transport layer

Transport-layer services

What the transport layer adds on top of the network layer: logical communication between processes, and the services TCP and UDP offer.

Sources: Kurose §3.1 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 2–8

Logical communication between processes

A transport-layer protocol provides logical communication between application processes running on different hosts. “Logical” means that, from the application's point of view, it is as if the hosts were directly connected, even though they may be on opposite sides of the planet with many routers and links between them.

Transport-layer protocols run in the end systems, not in routers:

  • At the sender, the transport layer converts application messages into transport-layer packets called segments: it breaks a long message into smaller chunks, adds a transport header to each, and passes each segment to the network layer, which wraps it in a datagram.
  • At the receiver, the network layer extracts the segment from the datagram and passes it up; the transport layer processes it and delivers the data to the right application.

Routers act only on the network-layer fields of the datagram; they never look at the segment inside.

Transport layer vs network layer

The network layer provides logical communication between hosts; the transport layer provides logical communication between processes.

Two houses of cousins (Kurose)

Twelve children in Ann's house in Mumbai write letters to twelve cousins in Bill's house in Coimbatore every week. Ann collects the letters in her house and hands them to the postal service; Bill collects arriving letters in his house and distributes them to his siblings.

In the analogyIn the network
Letters in envelopesApplication messages
CousinsProcesses
HousesHosts
Ann and BillThe transport-layer protocol
The postal serviceThe network-layer protocol

Ann and Bill work only inside their houses: they don't sort letters in post offices. And if Ann and Bill are replaced by Susan and Harvey, who are younger and sometimes lose letters, the house gets a worse “transport service” even though the postal service hasn't changed. Likewise, the services a transport protocol can offer are limited by the network layer underneath: if the post can't promise a delivery time, Ann and Bill can't promise one either.

Some services can be built on top of a network layer that doesn't offer them. IP doesn't guarantee delivery, but TCP still delivers reliably by retransmitting. IP doesn't encrypt, but a transport layer could. What a transport layer cannot add on its own are delay guarantees and bandwidth guarantees, because those depend on what happens inside the routers.

Transport layerNetwork layer
Logical communication betweenProcessesHosts
Runs inEnd systems onlyEnd systems and every router
UnitSegmentDatagram
AddressingPort numbersIP addresses
Internet protocolsTCP, UDPIP (+ routing protocols)

The Internet's network layer: best effort

The Internet's network-layer protocol, IP (Internet Protocol), provides a best-effort delivery service: it makes its “best effort” to deliver segments between hosts, but makes no guarantees. It doesn't guarantee delivery, doesn't guarantee in-order delivery, and doesn't guarantee the integrity of the data. IP is therefore an unreliable service. Every host has at least one IP address.

What UDP and TCP add

The most basic job of the transport layer is to extend IP's host-to-host delivery to process-to-process delivery. This is called transport-layer multiplexing and demultiplexing (Topic II.2). Both UDP and TCP also provide integrity checking by including error-detection fields in their headers.

ServiceUDPTCP
Process-to-process delivery (mux/demux)YesYes
Error checking (checksum)YesYes
Reliable, in-order deliveryNoYes: flow control, sequence numbers, acknowledgements, timers
Congestion controlNoYes: prevents any one connection from swamping the links and routers
Connection setupNoneThree-way handshake
Delay or bandwidth guaranteesNoNo
Exam focus
  • “Transport provides logical communication between processes; network between hosts”: a standard two-mark distinction, best illustrated with the household analogy.
  • Know what IP's best-effort service does not guarantee, and which of those TCP fixes.
  • 20CYS303 Mid 2023 Q4: differentiate TCP and UDP. Use the table above plus header sizes (UDP 8 bytes, TCP 20 bytes) and example applications.
Quick revision
  • Transport = logical communication between processes; runs only in end systems; unit = segment.
  • IP = best effort: no guarantee of delivery, order or integrity.
  • Both TCP and UDP: mux/demux and checksums. TCP adds reliability, flow control, congestion control and connections.
  • No transport protocol can add delay or bandwidth guarantees on its own.

II.2 · Transport layer

Multiplexing and demultiplexing

How the transport layer delivers each arriving segment to the right socket using port numbers: two-tuples for UDP, four-tuples for TCP, and why a web server has many sockets on port 80.

Sources: Kurose §3.2 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 9–22

The problem

A host receives segments for many processes at once: a browser with several tabs, a mail client, a music app, an SSH session. Each process has one or more sockets. When a segment arrives, the transport layer must hand it to the correct socket.

  • Demultiplexing: at the receiver, delivering the data in a transport segment to the correct socket.
  • Multiplexing: at the sender, gathering data chunks from different sockets, encapsulating each with header information (to be used later in demultiplexing), and passing the segments to the network layer.
Bill sorting letters

Demultiplexing is Bill taking the letters the postman delivers and handing each to the right sibling by reading the name. Multiplexing is Ann collecting letters from all her siblings and handing them to the postman.

Port numbers

Each segment carries two special header fields: the source port number and the destination port number. Each is a 16-bit number from 0 to 65535. Ports 0 to 1023 are well-known port numbers, reserved for well-known application protocols: HTTP 80, HTTPS 443, FTP 21 and 20, SMTP 25, DNS 53, SSH 22, Telnet 23, POP3 110, IMAP 143.

To demultiplex, the host's operating system reads the destination port number in the arriving segment and directs it to the corresponding socket. The segment's data then passes through the socket to the attached process.

Connectionless (UDP) demultiplexing

A UDP socket is fully identified by a two-tuple: (destination IP address, destination port number).

  • If two UDP segments have different source IP addresses and/or source ports but the same destination IP address and destination port, they are delivered to the same destination process through the same socket.
  • The source port serves as part of a “return address”: when the receiver wants to reply, it takes the source port from the arriving segment and uses it as the destination port of the reply.
  • The client side usually lets the transport layer assign a port automatically from 1024–65535 (an ephemeral port); the server side uses a specific port.

Connection-oriented (TCP) demultiplexing

A TCP socket is identified by a four-tuple: (source IP address, source port number, destination IP address, destination port number). When a TCP segment arrives, the host uses all four values to direct it to the right socket.

  • Two arriving TCP segments with different source IP addresses or source port numbers are directed to two different sockets, even if they have the same destination port (with one exception: segments carrying the original connection-establishment request).
  • A TCP server application has a welcoming socket that waits for connection requests on a well-known port (say 6789, or 80 for a web server). When a client's connection-establishment segment arrives, the server creates a new connection socket identified by the four-tuple of that client. All later segments from that client, matching all four values, go to that socket.
  • So one server host can support many simultaneous TCP connection sockets, all with the same local port 80, each identified by its own four-tuple.

Notice that A and C both use source port 26145. That's fine: the source IP addresses differ, so the four-tuples differ and the server keeps them apart.

Web servers and persistent HTTP

Today's high-performance web servers often use one process and create a new thread with a new connection socket for each client connection. With persistent HTTP, the client and server exchange all messages over the same server socket for the connection's lifetime. With non-persistent HTTP, a new TCP connection, and therefore a new socket, is created and closed for every request/response, which can severely affect a busy server's performance.

UDP socketTCP socket
Identified by2-tuple: (dest IP, dest port)4-tuple: (source IP, source port, dest IP, dest port)
Segments from different senders to the same portGo to the same socketGo to different sockets
Server sideOne socket serves all clientsA welcoming socket + one connection socket per client
How the receiver learns who sent itFrom the source IP and port in the datagram (returned with the data)Implicit: each socket belongs to one connection
From a paperPort numbers for two clients 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q3

Client A and client B each start an HTTP session with server S at about the same time.

Given

HTTP over TCP; server port 80; clients choose ephemeral ports.

Required

Possible source and destination ports for A→S, B→S, S→A, S→B; whether A and B can use the same source port if they are different hosts, and if they are the same host.

Formula

TCP demultiplexes on the 4-tuple; replies swap source and destination.

Substitution

Pick A's port = 26145 and B's port = 7532.

Calculation

A→S: source 26145, destination 80. B→S: source 7532, destination 80. S→A: source 80, destination 26145. S→B: source 80, destination 7532.

Final answer

(v) If A and B are different hosts, yes, they may use the same source port, because their IP addresses differ, so the four-tuples differ. (vi) If they are the same host, no: two connections from the same host to the same server port must have different source ports, or the four-tuples (and sockets) would be identical.

From a paperOne UDP socket, two senders 19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q4

A process in Host A has a UDP socket with port 2893. Hosts B and C each send a UDP segment to Host A with destination port 2893.

Given

UDP demultiplexes on (dest IP, dest port) = (A, 2893) for both segments.

Required

Do both go to the same socket? How does the process tell them apart? When would a developer prefer UDP?

Formula

UDP socket = 2-tuple.

Substitution

Both segments have destination (A, 2893).

Calculation

Same 2-tuple → same socket.

Final answer

Yes, both are delivered to the same socket. The operating system passes the source IP address and source port of each segment up with the data (for example through recvfrom), so the process knows one came from B and the other from C, and can reply to each. UDP is preferred when the application wants finer control over timing (no congestion control throttling), no connection-setup delay, no connection state at a busy server, and small header overhead: DNS, live streaming, Internet telephony, online games, network management (SNMP).

From a paperSockets at a web server 19CCE304 Mid 2023 Q5 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q7
Given

A web server on port 80 at Host C uses persistent connections and is receiving requests from Hosts A and B. In the second question, a client at port 12000 connects to the Apache server at www.cnn.com, and then a second browser on the same client connects too.

Required

Are all requests sent through the same socket at C? Do the sockets have port 80? Give four-tuples.

Formula

TCP connection socket = 4-tuple.

Substitution

A's connection: (A, a, C, 80). B's: (B, b, C, 80). CNN: (client, 12000, cnn, 80) and (client, 12001, cnn, 80).

Calculation

Different source IPs (or source ports) give different four-tuples.

Final answer

No: the requests from A and from B pass through different sockets at C, but both sockets have local port 80. The two CNN browser instances get separate connections and sockets, (client IP, 12000, cnn IP, 80) and (client IP, 12001, cnn IP, 80); they do not share a connection. (If the same requests used UDP, the server would see one socket, (cnn IP, 80), for both.)

Port scanning

A program such as nmap sends a TCP connection request to a port (or a UDP segment) on a target host to find which ports have an application listening. Administrators use it to audit their machines; attackers use it to find servers with known vulnerabilities.

Exam focus
  • Port-number and socket questions appeared in four papers. Always write the four-tuple for TCP and the two-tuple for UDP.
  • Draw the welcoming socket and per-connection sockets for a TCP server.
  • Explain why a server with non-persistent HTTP creates many sockets.
Common mistakes
  • Saying a server's connection sockets get new port numbers. They all keep the server's port (80); the four-tuples differ.
  • Saying UDP segments from different senders go to different sockets. UDP uses only the destination IP and port.
  • Swapping source and destination ports in the reply direction.
Quick revision
  • Demultiplexing (receiver): segment → right socket. Multiplexing (sender): sockets' data → segments with headers.
  • Ports: 16 bits (0–65535); well-known 0–1023.
  • UDP socket = (dest IP, dest port). TCP socket = (source IP, source port, dest IP, dest port).
  • A TCP server has a welcoming socket and one connection socket per client, all on the same port.

II.3 · Transport layer

UDP and the Internet checksum

The no-frills transport protocol: why applications choose it, its 8-byte header, and how the 16-bit one's-complement checksum (with Forouzan's pseudoheader) detects errors.

Sources: Kurose §3.3 · Forouzan §23.2 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 23–33 · Chapter3-Problems

What UDP does, and doesn't do

UDP (User Datagram Protocol, RFC 768) does just about as little as a transport protocol can: multiplexing/demultiplexing and light error checking. It adds almost nothing to IP. An application that chooses UDP is talking almost directly with IP.

UDP takes messages from the application, attaches the source and destination port fields and two other small fields, and passes the segment to the network layer. There is no handshaking between sender and receiver before sending, which is why UDP is called connectionless. UDP segments may be lost or delivered out of order to the application.

Why would anyone choose UDP over TCP?

  1. Finer application control over what data is sent, and when. As soon as the application hands data to UDP, UDP packages it and passes it to the network layer. TCP, in contrast, has congestion control that may throttle the sender, and keeps resending a segment until it is acknowledged, however long that takes. Real-time applications often need a minimum sending rate, don't want excessive delay, and can tolerate some loss.
  2. No connection establishment. TCP's three-way handshake adds delay before data flows; UDP adds none. This is the main reason DNS runs over UDP. It is also why QUIC (used by HTTP/3) is built on UDP.
  3. No connection state. TCP keeps receive and send buffers, congestion-control parameters and sequence/acknowledgement numbers per connection. UDP keeps none, so a server can support many more active clients.
  4. Small packet header overhead. UDP's header is 8 bytes; TCP's is 20 bytes.
ApplicationApplication protocolTransport
Email, remote terminal, web, file transferSMTP, Telnet/SSH, HTTP, FTPTCP
Name translationDNSTypically UDP
Network managementSNMPTypically UDP
Streaming multimedia, Internet telephonyOften proprietaryUDP or TCP
HTTP/3QUICUDP

Running a multimedia app over UDP is controversial, because UDP has no congestion control: if everyone streamed high-rate video over UDP, routers would overflow and few UDP packets would get through, while the TCP sessions sharing those links would slow to a crawl. An application can still get reliable transfer over UDP by building reliability into the application itself (acknowledgements and retransmissions), as QUIC does.

UDP segment structure

  • Source port and destination port: for multiplexing and demultiplexing.
  • Length: the number of bytes in the UDP segment, header plus data. It is needed because the data field size differs from segment to segment.
  • Checksum: used by the receiver to check whether errors have been introduced into the segment.
From a paper 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q5(b,c)

Why does UDP's length field count header plus data, while TCP's header-length field counts only the header? Because a UDP datagram is a self-contained message and the receiver needs its total size, whereas TCP is a byte stream: TCP's header length is needed because TCP has variable-length options, and the amount of data is found from the IP datagram's length minus the headers. Fields common to TCP and UDP: source port, destination port and checksum.

The Internet checksum

Why does UDP check for errors when many link-layer protocols already do? Because there is no guarantee that every link between source and destination checks for errors, and because bit errors can be introduced while a segment is stored in a router's memory. Since neither link-by-link reliability nor in-memory error detection is guaranteed, UDP must provide error detection end to end, if the end-to-end service is to provide it. This is an example of the end-end principle: functions placed at low levels may be redundant or of little value compared with providing them at a higher level.

How to compute it (sender)

  1. Divide the data to be checked into 16-bit words.
  2. Add all the words using one's-complement arithmetic: add normally, and whenever the sum carries out of the 16th bit, wrap the carry around and add it to the least significant bit.
  3. Take the one's complement of the final sum (flip every bit). That is the checksum, placed in the checksum field.

How to check it (receiver)

Add all the 16-bit words including the checksum. If there are no errors, the result is 1111111111111111 (all ones). If any bit of the result is 0, an error has been detected.

Internet checksum

\[ \text{checksum} = \overline{\,w_1 \oplus_1 w_2 \oplus_1 \cdots \oplus_1 w_n\,} \]

where \( \oplus_1 \) is 16-bit addition with end-around carry and the bar is bitwise complement. At the receiver: \( w_1 \oplus_1 \cdots \oplus_1 w_n \oplus_1 \text{checksum} = \texttt{FFFF} \).

The pseudoheader (Forouzan §23.2)

The UDP checksum covers more than the segment. Before computing it, the sender prepends a 12-byte pseudoheader taken from the IP header: the 32-bit source IP address, the 32-bit destination IP address, 8 bits of zeros, the 8-bit protocol field (17 for UDP), and the 16-bit UDP length. The pseudoheader is used only in the calculation; it is not sent. It ensures that a datagram delivered to the wrong host (because of an error in the IP address) or to the wrong protocol is caught. If the sender chooses not to compute a checksum, the field is all zeros; so if a computed checksum happens to be all zeros, it is sent as all ones (both mean zero in one's complement).

Worked numericals

Level 1Two words (Chapter3-Problems)
Given

\(w_1 = 01000010\ 00010110\), \(w_2 = 11110101\ 10111000\).

Required

The Internet checksum.

Formula

One's-complement sum with wraparound, then complement.

Substitution

In hex: \(w_1 = \texttt{4216}\), \(w_2 = \texttt{F5B8}\).

Calculation

\(\texttt{4216} + \texttt{F5B8} = \texttt{1 37CE}\). The carry 1 wraps around: \(\texttt{37CE} + 1 = \texttt{37CF}\) = 00110111 11001111. Complement: 11001000 00110000.

Final answer

Checksum = 11001000 00110000 (0xC830).

From a paperThree words, sender and receiver 19CSE301 End 2023 Q3(b)(ii)
Given

\(w_1 = 0110011001100000\), \(w_2 = 0101010101010101\), \(w_3 = 1000111100001100\).

Required

The checksum at the sender, and the check at the receiver.

Formula

Sum with wraparound; complement; receiver sums all four words.

Substitution

\(w_1 + w_2\) first.

Calculation

0110011001100000 + 0101010101010101 = 1011101110110101 (no carry out). 1011101110110101 + 1000111100001100 = 1 0100101011000001 → wrap: 0100101011000001 + 1 = 0100101011000010. Complement: 1011010100111101. Receiver: 0100101011000010 + 1011010100111101 = 1111111111111111.

Final answer

Checksum = 1011010100111101. The receiver's sum of all four words is 1111111111111111, so no error is detected.

From a paperWeather balloon, six hex words 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q3(b)
Given

Six 16-bit words: 0x4500, 0x0073, 0x0000, 0x4000, 0x4011, 0xB861.

Required

The UDP checksum value.

Formula

Running one's-complement sum, then complement.

Substitution

Add word by word, wrapping any carry out of 16 bits.

Calculation

0x4500 + 0x0073 = 0x4573. + 0x0000 = 0x4573. + 0x4000 = 0x8573. + 0x4011 = 0xC584. + 0xB861 = 0x17DE5 → wrap: 0x7DE5 + 0x1 = 0x7DE6. Complement: 0xFFFF − 0x7DE6 = 0x8219.

Final answer

Checksum = 0x8219 (1000 0010 0001 1001). Check: 0x7DE6 + 0x8219 = 0xFFFF.

From a paperA sum that is already all ones 20CYS303 End 2025 Q5
Given

Word A = 1110 0011 1011 0100 (0xE3B4); Word B = 0001 1100 0100 1011 (0x1C4B).

Required

(a) The checksum, showing the sum, any wraparound and the complement. (b) Why the checksum is “weak protection”.

Formula

Sum, wrap, complement.

Substitution

0xE3B4 + 0x1C4B.

Calculation

1110001110110100 + 0001110001001011 = 1111111111111111 (0xFFFF); no carry out, so no wraparound. Complement: 0000000000000000.

Final answer

(a) Checksum = 0x0000 (UDP would transmit it as 0xFFFF, since an all-zero field means “no checksum”). (b) The one's-complement sum is commutative and only adds columns, so it misses errors that keep the sum unchanged: if a bit flips from 0 to 1 in one word and the same bit position flips from 1 to 0 in another word, the sum and checksum are identical and the error passes undetected; reordering the words also goes undetected. CRC (Unit III) is far stronger.

Interactive visualizationChecksum, parity and CRC workbenchType your own 16-bit words to see the running sum, every wraparound and the complement; then flip bits and see which errors the checksum misses.
Open simulator →
Exam focus
  • Internet checksum calculations appeared in three papers, including your own course's mid-term. Show every partial sum and every wraparound.
  • Know all four UDP header fields and what the length field counts.
  • Give the four reasons for choosing UDP, and DNS as the classic example.
  • Explain why UDP needs a checksum when links have one (end-end principle).
Common mistakes
  • Dropping the carry instead of wrapping it around.
  • Forgetting the final complement (giving the sum as the checksum).
  • At the receiver, forgetting to add the checksum itself.
  • Saying UDP corrects errors. It only detects them (and usually discards the segment, or passes it up with a warning).
Quick revision
  • UDP: connectionless, unreliable, no congestion control; 8-byte header (src port, dst port, length, checksum).
  • Choose UDP for timing control, no setup delay, no state, small header: DNS, SNMP, streaming, QUIC.
  • Checksum: 16-bit words, one's-complement sum with wraparound, complement. Receiver sum = all ones.
  • UDP checksum also covers a pseudoheader (IP addresses, protocol 17, length).
  • Weak: misses compensating bit errors in the same column, and reordering.

II.4 · Transport layer

Reliable data transfer: the problem and the toolkit

What “reliable” means, the interfaces a reliable protocol sits between, how finite-state machines describe a protocol, and the five tools every reliable protocol is built from.

Sources: Kurose §3.4 (intro) · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 34–40

The service and the problem

The service a reliable channel provides to the layer above is simple: no transferred bit is corrupted (flipped) or lost, and all bits are delivered in the order they were sent. This is exactly what TCP offers to Internet applications.

The difficulty is that the layer below the reliable protocol may be unreliable. TCP is a reliable protocol built on top of IP, an unreliable host-to-host service. More generally, the layer below might be a single unreliable physical link. The job of a reliable data transfer (rdt) protocol is to provide the reliable service on top of an unreliable channel.

The four interface calls

CallWhereMeaning
rdt_send(data)Sending side, called from aboveThe application hands data to the rdt protocol to be delivered
udt_send(packet)Sending side (and receiver, for feedback)rdt hands a packet to the unreliable channel below
rdt_rcv(packet)Receiving side, called from belowA packet has arrived from the channel
deliver_data(data)Receiving side, upwardrdt delivers data to the application

We study unidirectional data transfer: data flows from sender to receiver only. Control packets (ACKs, NAKs) flow both ways, which is why both sides call udt_send().

Finite-state machines (FSMs)

Each protocol version is described by two FSMs, one for the sender and one for the receiver.

  • A state is drawn as a circle. In each state, the next state is uniquely determined by the next event.
  • An arrow is a transition from one state to another, labelled event / actions: the event above a horizontal line causes the transition, and the actions below the line are taken when it happens.
  • When no action is taken, the action is written Λ (lambda). When no event is needed, the event is Λ.
  • A dashed arrow marks the initial state.

The toolkit

Reliable protocols are built up step by step, in Topic II.5, by adding one mechanism for each new problem the channel causes:

Problem in the channelMechanism that solves itFirst appears in
Bits may be flippedChecksum (error detection)rdt2.0
Sender doesn't know whether a packet arrived intactReceiver feedback: positive (ACK) and negative (NAK) acknowledgementsrdt2.0
A corrupted packet must be resentRetransmissionrdt2.0
A retransmission may be a duplicateSequence numbersrdt2.1
Packets (or ACKs) may be lost entirelyCountdown timer and timeout-driven retransmissionrdt3.0
Waiting for each ACK wastes the linkPipelining with a window of sequence numbersGBN, SR (II.6)

Protocols based on retransmission are called ARQ (Automatic Repeat reQuest) protocols. ARQ needs three capabilities: error detection, receiver feedback and retransmission.

Exam focus
  • 20CYS303 End 2023 Q9 and 20CYS303 End 2024 Q6: “What is RDT? Why is it required?” Answer: a protocol that gives a reliable channel (no corruption, no loss, in order) on top of an unreliable one, required because IP and physical links can corrupt and lose packets.
  • Name the four interface calls and the FSM notation (event / action, Λ, dashed initial arrow).
Quick revision
  • Reliable service: no corruption, no loss, in-order delivery, built over an unreliable channel.
  • Calls: rdt_send, udt_send, rdt_rcv, deliver_data.
  • FSM: states, event/actions transitions, Λ.
  • Toolkit: checksum, ACK/NAK, retransmission, sequence numbers, timer, pipelining. ARQ = detection + feedback + retransmission.

II.5 · Transport layer

RDT 1.0, 2.0, 2.1, 2.2 and 3.0

Building a reliable protocol one problem at a time: a perfect channel, bit errors, garbled acknowledgements, removing NAKs, and finally packet loss with a timer. Ends with why stop-and-wait performs so badly.

Sources: Kurose §3.4.1 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 41–62 · Chapter3-Problems

Interactive visualizationRDT simulator: all five versionsPick a version and a scenario (corrupted packet, corrupted ACK, lost packet, lost ACK, premature timeout, duplicates) or set your own channel faults. Step forward and back through every event with the sender and receiver FSM states highlighted.
Open simulator →

rdt1.0: a perfectly reliable channel

Assume the channel below is completely reliable: no bit errors, no loss. Then the protocol is trivial.

SideStateEventActionsNext state
SenderWait for call from aboverdt_send(data)packet = make_pkt(data); udt_send(packet)Same state
ReceiverWait for call from belowrdt_rcv(packet)extract(packet, data); deliver_data(data)Same state

Each FSM has just one state. There is no need for the receiver to give feedback, because nothing can go wrong, and the receiver is assumed to be able to receive data as fast as the sender sends it.

rdt2.0: a channel with bit errors

Now bits in a packet may be corrupted (as on real links and in router memory), but packets are still never lost and arrive in order.

Dictating a long message

When you dictate a message over a noisy phone line, the listener says “OK” after each sentence heard correctly (a positive acknowledgement), and “Please repeat that” when a sentence is garbled (a negative acknowledgement). You then repeat that sentence.

rdt2.0 adds the three ARQ capabilities:

  1. Error detection: a checksum field in each packet (as in UDP), so the receiver can detect bit errors.
  2. Receiver feedback: the receiver replies with an ACK (positive, 1 bit is enough) or a NAK (negative).
  3. Retransmission: a packet received in error is retransmitted by the sender.
SideStateEventActionsNext state
SenderWait for call from aboverdt_send(data)sndpkt = make_pkt(data, checksum); udt_send(sndpkt)Wait for ACK or NAK
SenderWait for ACK or NAKrdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && isNAK(rcvpkt)udt_send(sndpkt) (retransmit)Wait for ACK or NAK
SenderWait for ACK or NAKrdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && isACK(rcvpkt)ΛWait for call from above
ReceiverWait for call from belowrdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && corrupt(rcvpkt)sndpkt = make_pkt(NAK); udt_send(sndpkt)Same
ReceiverWait for call from belowrdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && notcorrupt(rcvpkt)extract; deliver_data(data); sndpkt = make_pkt(ACK); udt_send(sndpkt)Same

The sender has two states; the receiver has one. While the sender waits for an ACK or NAK it cannot accept more data from the application. Because the sender sends one packet and then waits, rdt2.0 is a stop-and-wait protocol.

rdt2.0's fatal flaw

rdt2.0 assumes the ACK or NAK itself always arrives intact. But ACK and NAK packets can be corrupted too. If the sender receives a garbled ACK/NAK, it cannot tell whether the receiver got the data correctly.

  • Asking “what did you say?” leads to an endless loop of garbled questions.
  • Adding enough checksum bits to correct errors works only for bit errors, not lost packets.
  • Simply resending the packet when the feedback is garbled works, but introduces duplicate packets: the receiver can't tell whether an arriving packet is new data or a retransmission.

The fix is to number the packets: put a sequence number in each data packet. The receiver then only needs to check the number to know whether a packet is a retransmission. For stop-and-wait, a 1-bit sequence number (0 or 1) is enough.

rdt2.1: handling garbled ACKs and NAKs

The sender now numbers packets 0, 1, 0, 1, …. The FSMs double in size, because each state must remember which sequence number is expected.

SideStateEventActionsNext state
SenderWait for call 0 from aboverdt_send(data)sndpkt = make_pkt(0, data, checksum); udt_send(sndpkt)Wait for ACK or NAK 0
SenderWait for ACK or NAK 0rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && (corrupt(rcvpkt) || isNAK(rcvpkt))udt_send(sndpkt)Same
SenderWait for ACK or NAK 0rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && notcorrupt(rcvpkt) && isACK(rcvpkt)ΛWait for call 1 from above
SenderWait for call 1 from aboverdt_send(data)sndpkt = make_pkt(1, data, checksum); udt_send(sndpkt)Wait for ACK or NAK 1
SenderWait for ACK or NAK 1corrupt or NAKudt_send(sndpkt)Same
SenderWait for ACK or NAK 1notcorrupt and ACKΛWait for call 0 from above
ReceiverWait for 0 from belownotcorrupt(rcvpkt) && has_seq0(rcvpkt)extract; deliver_data; send ACK (with checksum)Wait for 1 from below
ReceiverWait for 0 from belowcorrupt(rcvpkt)send NAKSame
ReceiverWait for 0 from belownotcorrupt(rcvpkt) && has_seq1(rcvpkt) (a duplicate)send ACK (do not deliver)Same
ReceiverWait for 1 from below(mirror image with 0 and 1 swapped)
  • Sender: 4 states. It must check whether the ACK/NAK is corrupted, and remember whether the current packet is 0 or 1.
  • Receiver: 2 states. The state tells it which number it expects. On a duplicate (the wrong number, uncorrupted) it sends an ACK again, because the sender evidently missed the previous ACK. The receiver cannot know whether its last ACK/NAK was received correctly.

rdt2.2: a NAK-free protocol

The same function can be achieved with ACKs only. Instead of a NAK, the receiver sends an ACK for the last correctly received packet, and the ACK must include the sequence number of the packet being acknowledged. A sender that receives two ACKs for the same packet (a duplicate ACK) knows the following packet was not received correctly, and retransmits it.

SideChange from rdt2.1
Receivermake_pkt(ACK, 0, checksum) / make_pkt(ACK, 1, checksum): every ACK carries the sequence number it acknowledges. On a corrupt packet or a duplicate, it resends the ACK for the last good packet (this is what replaces NAK)
SenderChecks isACK(rcvpkt, 0) or isACK(rcvpkt, 1). In “Wait for ACK 0”, if the packet is corrupt or isACK(rcvpkt, 1) (the ACK for the previous packet), it retransmits

TCP uses exactly this idea: it has no NAKs, and duplicate ACKs signal a missing segment (Topic II.9).

rdt3.0: a channel with errors and loss

Now the channel can also lose packets (data or ACKs), as the Internet does when router buffers overflow. Two new questions: how do we detect loss, and what do we do about it? Checksums, sequence numbers, ACKs and retransmission already handle what to do. To detect loss, the sender uses a timer.

The sender waits “long enough” to be fairly sure a packet has been lost (at least one round trip plus processing time), and then retransmits. If a packet is merely delayed, not lost, the sender retransmits anyway, which creates a duplicate data packet; but rdt2.2's sequence numbers already handle duplicates.

The sender therefore needs a countdown timer: start it each time a packet (first time or retransmission) is sent, respond to a timer interrupt (timeout), and stop it when the right ACK arrives.

StateEventActionsNext state
Wait for call 0 from aboverdt_send(data)sndpkt = make_pkt(0, data, checksum); udt_send(sndpkt); start_timerWait for ACK0
Wait for call 0 from aboverdt_rcv(rcvpkt) (a stray or late ACK)ΛSame
Wait for ACK0rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && (corrupt(rcvpkt) || isACK(rcvpkt, 1))Λ (just keep waiting for the timer)Same
Wait for ACK0timeoutudt_send(sndpkt); start_timerSame
Wait for ACK0rdt_rcv(rcvpkt) && notcorrupt(rcvpkt) && isACK(rcvpkt, 0)stop_timerWait for call 1 from above
Wait for call 1 / Wait for ACK1Mirror images with 0 and 1 swapped

The rdt3.0 receiver is the same as the rdt2.2 receiver (ACKs with sequence numbers, re-ACK on duplicate or corruption). Notice that on a corrupt packet or wrong ACK, the rdt3.0 sender does nothing and lets the timer expire; it no longer retransmits on duplicate ACKs. Because sequence numbers alternate between 0 and 1, rdt3.0 is also called the alternating-bit protocol.

rdt3.0 in action: the four cases

(a) No loss

(b) Packet loss

(c) ACK loss

(d) Premature timeout

  1. No loss: every packet is acknowledged before the timer expires.
  2. Packet loss: pkt1 never arrives, no ACK comes back, the timer expires and the sender retransmits pkt1.
  3. ACK loss: pkt1 arrives and is delivered, but ACK1 is lost. The timer expires, pkt1 is resent, and the receiver recognises it as a duplicate (it expects 0 now), discards it and sends ACK1 again.
  4. Premature timeout: ACK1 is only delayed, so the timer expires before it arrives. The sender resends pkt1; the receiver discards the duplicate and ACKs again. When the second ACK1 arrives the sender, now waiting for ACK0, ignores it.
Interactive visualizationRDT 3.0: ACK lostThe exact scenario above, step by step: pkt0 sent and received, ACK lost, timeout, retransmission, the receiver spotting the duplicate, and the correct ACK returned.
Open simulator →

Comparison of the five versions

VersionChannel assumptionNew mechanismSender statesReceiver statesFeedback
rdt1.0Perfectly reliableNone11None
rdt2.0Bit errorsChecksum, ACK/NAK, retransmission21ACK, NAK
rdt2.1Bit errors, incl. in ACK/NAKSequence numbers 0/142ACK, NAK
rdt2.2Same as 2.1ACKs carry sequence numbers; duplicate ACK replaces NAK42ACK only
rdt3.0Bit errors and lossCountdown timer42ACK only

Performance of stop-and-wait

rdt3.0 is correct, but its performance is very poor because it is stop-and-wait.

Consider two hosts on opposite coasts of the USA with \(\text{RTT} = 30\) ms, connected by a channel of rate \(R = 1\) Gbps, sending packets of \(L = 1{,}000\) bytes (8,000 bits).

Given

\(R = 10^9\) bps, \(L = 8{,}000\) bits, RTT = 30 ms.

Required

The fraction of time the sender is busy (utilisation) and the effective throughput.

Formula

\( d_{trans} = L/R \); \( U_{sender} = \dfrac{L/R}{\text{RTT} + L/R} \); throughput \(= L / (\text{RTT} + L/R)\).

Substitution

\( L/R = 8{,}000 / 10^9 \).

Calculation

\( L/R = 8\ \mu\text{s} = 0.008 \) ms. \( U = 0.008 / 30.008 = 0.00027 \). Throughput \( = 8{,}000 \text{ bits} / 30.008 \text{ ms} \approx 267 \) kbps.

Final answer

The sender is busy only 2.7 hundredths of 1% of the time. On a 1 Gbps link it achieves about 267 kbps, a striking example of a protocol limiting the hardware.

Stop-and-wait utilisation

\[ U_{sender} = \frac{L/R}{\text{RTT} + L/R} \]

The fix is to let the sender send several packets without waiting for acknowledgements: pipelining (Topic II.6).

Answers to past-paper questions on RDT

From a paperWhy checksums, sequence numbers and a timer 20CYS303 End 2025 Q12
Given

rdt3.0 uses checksums, 1-bit sequence numbers and a countdown timer.

Required

(a) Why checksums alone were not enough in rdt2.0. (b) A scenario where sequence numbers are essential. (c) Why rdt3.0 needs a timer, and why the timer forces sequence numbers even if only data packets could be lost.

Formula

Match each problem to its mechanism (Topic II.4 table).

Substitution

Use the timelines above.

Calculation

(a) A checksum only detects corruption; the receiver must also tell the sender (ACK/NAK) and the sender must retransmit. Even then, rdt2.0 breaks if the ACK/NAK itself is corrupted. (b) The rdt2.0 “fatal flaw” timeline: ACK garbled → sender resends pkt → receiver, with no numbers, delivers the same data twice. (c) Without a timer, a lost packet or ACK leaves the sender waiting forever. With a timer, a packet that is merely delayed causes a premature timeout and a retransmission, so the receiver can get two copies even when nothing is lost; it can only tell them apart by sequence number.

Final answer

Checksums detect errors but need feedback and retransmission; feedback can be garbled, so retransmissions can create duplicates, which only sequence numbers expose; loss requires a timer, and timers (premature timeouts) create duplicates too, so sequence numbers remain essential.

From a paper 19CCE304 Jan 2023 Q3(A)

If the round-trip delay is constant and known to the sender, is a timer still needed in rdt3.0? Yes. The sender still has to detect that “one RTT has passed without an ACK”, and that requires measuring time. Knowing the RTT exactly just means the timer can be set precisely, so there will be no premature timeouts: when it expires, the packet or its ACK really was lost.

From a paperA receiver that sends only duplicate ACKs 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q8(a)

In a stop-and-wait protocol the receiver is an attacker that sends duplicate ACKs for every packet, so the sender keeps timing out and retransmitting.

Given

Every ACK received is a duplicate (for the previous sequence number); timeouts and retransmissions repeat forever.

Required

An idea to protect the server (at most 3 lines) and the changes to the sender's FSM.

Formula

Bound the number of retransmissions of one packet.

Substitution

Add a counter variable retx and a limit MAX_RETX.

Calculation

Sender FSM changes: in “Wait for call 0/1”, on rdt_send(data) set retx = 0. In “Wait for ACK 0/1”, on timeout: retx = retx + 1; if retx ≤ MAX_RETX then udt_send(sndpkt); start_timer; else stop_timer, close the connection and report the peer (move to a new “Closed / blocked” state). Optionally also count duplicate ACKs and treat more than a threshold as an attack.

Final answer

Limit retransmissions per packet (and the rate of duplicate ACKs accepted from one peer); after the limit, abort the session and block the misbehaving receiver. FSM: a retransmission counter reset on each new packet, incremented on each timeout, and a new transition to a terminating state when it exceeds the limit.

Exam focus
  • RDT FSMs appeared in 7 papers. Practise drawing: rdt2.0 sender (19CSE301 End 2023 Q3(b)(i)), rdt3.0 sender and receiver (20CYS303 End 2023 Q9, 20CYS303 End 2024 Q6), and the differences among rdt2.0, 2.1 and 2.2 with both FSMs (20CYS303 Mid 2023 Q6).
  • Always state each version's channel assumption, and draw the four rdt3.0 cases.
  • Stop-and-wait utilisation formula and the 0.00027 example.
Common mistakes
  • Giving rdt2.0 sequence numbers. It has none; that's its flaw.
