  • Saying the rdt3.0 sender retransmits on a duplicate ACK. It ignores it and waits for the timeout.
  • Forgetting start_timer after a retransmission, or stop_timer on the correct ACK.
  • Writing the receiver's duplicate handling as “discard silently”. It must re-send the ACK.
Quick revision
  • 1.0: reliable channel, 1 state each. 2.0: checksum + ACK/NAK + retransmit; stop-and-wait; flaw with garbled ACKs.
  • 2.1: 0/1 sequence numbers; sender 4 states, receiver 2; duplicates re-ACKed.
  • 2.2: no NAK; ACK carries the sequence number; duplicate ACK = NAK.
  • 3.0: + countdown timer for loss; alternating-bit protocol; four cases: no loss, packet loss, ACK loss, premature timeout.
  • \(U = (L/R)/(\text{RTT} + L/R)\); 1 Gbps, 1 KB, 30 ms gives 0.00027.

II.6 · Transport layer

Pipelined protocols: Go-Back-N and Selective Repeat

Sending a window of packets before waiting: how pipelining multiplies utilisation, how GBN recovers with cumulative ACKs and one timer, how SR recovers with individual ACKs and buffering, and the window-size limit.

Sources: Kurose §3.4.2–3.4.4 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 63–76, 148–149

Pipelining

Instead of stop-and-wait, the sender sends multiple packets without waiting for acknowledgements. Because the many in-transit packets can be visualised as filling a pipeline, this is called pipelining.

If the sender sends 3 packets before waiting, utilisation triples:

Utilisation with a window of N packets (as long as N L/R < RTT + L/R)

\[ U_{sender} = \frac{N \cdot L/R}{\text{RTT} + L/R} \]

With N = 3 in the example of II.5: \( U = 0.024 / 30.008 = 0.00081 \).

Pipelining has consequences:

  • The range of sequence numbers must increase: each in-transit packet needs a unique number.
  • The sender and receiver may need to buffer more than one packet: the sender buffers packets sent but not yet acknowledged; the receiver may buffer packets received correctly but out of order.
  • How lost, corrupted and delayed packets are handled depends on the approach: Go-Back-N or Selective Repeat.

If the sequence-number field has \(k\) bits, the sequence numbers run from \(0\) to \(2^k - 1\), with arithmetic modulo \(2^k\).

Interactive visualizationGo-Back-N vs Selective RepeatChoose GBN or SR, the window size, the number of packets and which packets or ACKs are lost. Watch the sender and receiver windows slide, the timers, and every retransmission.
Open simulator →

Go-Back-N (GBN)

The sender may have at most N unacknowledged packets in the pipeline. N is the window size, and GBN is a sliding-window protocol.

  • base = sequence number of the oldest unacknowledged packet; nextseqnum = smallest unused sequence number.
  • Numbers in \([0, \text{base} - 1]\) are already acknowledged; \([\text{base}, \text{nextseqnum} - 1]\) are sent but not acknowledged; \([\text{nextseqnum}, \text{base} + N - 1]\) can be used immediately if data arrives from above; numbers \(\ge \text{base} + N\) can't be used until the window slides.

The GBN sender responds to three events

  1. Invocation from above: if the window isn't full, create and send a packet and update variables; if it is full, refuse the data (or buffer it).
  2. Receipt of an ACK: an ACK for packet \(n\) is a cumulative acknowledgement: all packets with numbers up to and including \(n\) have been received correctly. The base moves to \(n + 1\).
  3. A timeout event: GBN uses a single timer, for the oldest transmitted but unacknowledged packet. On timeout, the sender resends all packets that have been sent but not acknowledged. On an ACK, if packets are still outstanding, the timer restarts; if none are, it stops.

The GBN receiver is simple

  • If packet \(n\) arrives correctly and in order (the last packet delivered was \(n - 1\)), the receiver sends an ACK for \(n\) and delivers the data.
  • In every other case, it discards the packet and resends an ACK for the most recently received in-order packet.
  • The receiver keeps only one variable, expectedseqnum, and buffers nothing. Discarding a correctly received out-of-order packet seems wasteful, but it would be retransmitted anyway, and it keeps the receiver simple.

Selective Repeat (SR)

GBN can perform badly: when the window and the bandwidth-delay product are both large, a single packet error can cause GBN to retransmit a large number of packets, many unnecessarily. Selective Repeat avoids this by having the sender retransmit only those packets it suspects were lost or corrupted.

  • The receiver individually acknowledges every correctly received packet, whether or not it is in order.
  • Out-of-order packets are buffered until the missing packets arrive; then a batch of in-order packets is delivered to the layer above.
  • The sender keeps a window of size N, but packets inside it may already be individually acknowledged. Each packet has its own logical timer; on a timeout, only that packet is resent.
SR sender eventAction
Data from aboveIf the next sequence number is within the window, packetise and send; otherwise buffer or refuse
Timeout of packet nResend packet n only; restart its timer
ACK received for n in [send_base, send_base + N − 1]Mark n as received. If n = send_base, move the base forward to the smallest unacknowledged number, and send any untransmitted packets that now fall inside the window
SR receiver eventAction
Packet n in [rcv_base, rcv_base + N − 1] arrives correctlySend ACK(n). If it wasn't received before, buffer it. If n = rcv_base, deliver it and all consecutive buffered packets, and move rcv_base forward
Packet n in [rcv_base − N, rcv_base − 1] arrivesSend ACK(n) again, even though it was already acknowledged (the earlier ACK may have been lost, so the sender's window can't move)
OtherwiseIgnore

The SR dilemma: why the window must be small enough

With 4 sequence numbers (0–3) and a window of 3, the receiver cannot tell these two situations apart: (a) all three ACKs were lost, and the sender is retransmitting the old packet 0; (b) all three ACKs arrived, the sender sent new packets 3, 0, 1, and the new packet 3 was lost, so a new packet 0 arrives. In both, a packet numbered 0 arrives while the receiver's window is {3, 0, 1}; in (a) it would wrongly accept a duplicate as new data.

Window-size limits with k-bit sequence numbers

\[ \text{GBN: } N \le 2^k - 1 \qquad \text{SR: } N \le 2^{k-1} \;(\text{half the sequence space}) \]

GBN versus SR (and stop-and-wait)

FeatureStop-and-wait (rdt3.0)Go-Back-NSelective Repeat
Packets in flight1Up to NUp to N
AcknowledgementsPer packetCumulativeIndividual
Timers11 (oldest unACKed)One per packet
On loss, resendThat packetAll unACKed packets from the lost one onwardOnly the lost packet
Receiver buffers out-of-order?—No, discardsYes
Receiver window11N
Max window (k-bit seq)1\(2^k - 1\)\(2^{k-1}\)
ComplexityLowestSimple receiverComplex sender and receiver
Bandwidth use under errorsPoorWastes bandwidth on retransmitsEfficient

Worked numericals

From a paperGo-back-3, every 6th transmission lost 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q12(b)
Given

Go-Back-N with N = 3; 11 packets to deliver; every 6th transmission is lost (transmissions 6, 12, 18, …); ACKs are not lost.

Required

The number of transmissions needed, with the complete flow.

Formula

Simulate: send while the window has room; a loss makes the receiver discard everything after it until the timeout resends the window.

Substitution

Number transmissions t1, t2, ….

Calculation

t1–t3: packets 1, 2, 3. ACKs slide the window: t4 = 4, t5 = 5, t6 = 6 (lost), t7 = 7, t8 = 8 (both discarded, receiver re-ACKs 5). Timeout for 6 → t9 = 6, t10 = 7, t11 = 8 (all arrive). Window slides: t12 = 9 (lost), t13 = 10, t14 = 11 (discarded). Timeout for 9 → t15 = 9, t16 = 10, t17 = 11 (all arrive). Done.

Final answer

17 transmissions for 11 packets.

From a paperFrames 4 and 7 lost, window 5 19CSE301 End 2024 Q2

Ten frames (1–10) are sent with window size 5; frames 4 and 7 are lost on their first transmission only.

Given

N = 5; frames 1–10; first transmissions of 4 and 7 lost.

Required

The sequence of transmissions and ACKs for GBN and for SR, and the total transmissions.

Formula

GBN resends the whole outstanding window on timeout; SR resends only the lost frame.

Substitution

Initial window 1–5.

Calculation

GBN: send 1–5; 4 lost; ACK1, ACK2, ACK3 arrive; 5 is discarded (ACK3 again). The window slides to 4–8, so 6, 7, 8 are sent; 7 is lost, and 6 and 8 are discarded (ACK3, ACK3). Timeout for 4: resend 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, which arrive (ACK4…ACK8). Window 9–13: send 9, 10; ACK9, ACK10. Total = 5 + 3 + 5 + 2 = 15. SR: send 1–5; 4 lost; ACK1, 2, 3, 5 (5 buffered). Window slides to 4–8: send 6, 7, 8; 7 lost; ACK6, ACK8 (buffered). Timeout for 4: resend 4 → receiver delivers 4, 5, 6; ACK4; sender's base moves to 7, window 7–11: send 9, 10 (buffered, ACKed). Timeout for 7: resend 7 → deliver 7, 8, 9, 10; ACK7. Total = 5 + 3 + 1 + 2 + 1 = 12.

Final answer

GBN needs 15 transmissions, SR needs 12. SR resent only the two lost frames.

From a paperGBN sender with a stop-and-wait receiver 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q8(b)
Given

Sender uses GBN with window 4 and 3-bit sequence numbers (0–7); receiver uses stop-and-wait; 6 packets.

Required

Is the combination valid (are all packets accepted and consumed)? Justify with a timeline.

Formula

A GBN receiver has a receive window of 1: it accepts only the next in-order packet and ACKs it.

Substitution

A stop-and-wait receiver also accepts only the next expected packet and acknowledges it, re-ACKing anything else.

Calculation

Sender sends 0, 1, 2, 3. Receiver (expecting 0) accepts 0 → ACK0; then 1 → ACK1; 2 → ACK2; 3 → ACK3. Each ACK slides the sender's window, which sends 4, 5; the receiver accepts them in order → ACK4, ACK5. If a packet were lost, the receiver would discard the following ones and re-ACK the last good packet; the GBN sender's timeout would resend from the lost one, which the receiver then accepts.

Final answer

Yes, valid, provided the receiver tracks the expected number in the same 3-bit space (mod 8): a stop-and-wait receiver behaves exactly like a GBN receiver (window 1, in-order only, cumulative-style ACK of the last good packet), so all 6 packets are accepted and consumed in order. It would not work with a strict 1-bit (alternating 0/1) receiver, which would misread sequence number 2 onwards.

From a paperAn SR receiver's window and rwnd 20CYS303 End 2025 Q8
Given

SR receiver window N = 5, rcv_base = 10; packets 11, 12, 13 arrive correctly and are buffered; 10 is lost and later retransmitted; advertised rwnd = 5 segments; buffer capacity 20 segments.

Required

(a) how the window and buffer evolve; (b) the window before and after 10 arrives; (c) when data is delivered; (d) the new rwnd.

Formula

SR receiver rules (above).

Substitution

Window [10, 14].

Calculation

(a) 11, 12, 13 each arrive inside [10, 14]: each is ACKed individually and buffered; rcv_base stays at 10 because 10 is missing. Free slots in the window: 10 and 14. (b) Before: window [10 … 14] with 11–13 buffered. When 10 arrives, it is ACKed, and 10, 11, 12, 13 are delivered; rcv_base jumps to 14; window [14 … 18]. (c) Nothing is delivered to the application until 10 arrives; then 10–13 are delivered together, in order. (d) After delivery the buffer slots are freed, so the whole window [14 … 18] is free again.

Final answer

New rwnd = 5 segments (it had effectively shrunk to 2 free slots while 11–13 were held waiting for 10).

Exam focus
  • GBN/SR timelines appeared in 7 papers (19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q7, 20CYS303 Mid 2023 Q7, 19CCE304 End 2023 Q4 and those above). Practise drawing windows, cumulative vs individual ACKs, and timeouts.
  • Window-size limits: GBN \(2^k - 1\), SR \(2^{k-1}\), and the SR dilemma.
  • \(U = N(L/R)/(\text{RTT} + L/R)\).
Common mistakes
  • In GBN, making the receiver buffer out-of-order packets. It discards them.
  • In SR, making the sender resend the whole window. It resends only the packet whose timer expired.
  • Forgetting that the GBN receiver re-ACKs the last in-order packet when it discards.
  • Using \(2^k\) as the GBN window limit (it is \(2^k - 1\)).
Quick revision
  • Pipelining: N packets in flight; \(U = N L/R / (\text{RTT} + L/R)\).
  • GBN: window N, cumulative ACK, one timer, resend all unACKed on timeout, receiver discards out-of-order.
  • SR: individual ACKs, per-packet timers, receiver buffers, resend only the lost packet; re-ACK old packets.
  • Windows: GBN ≤ \(2^k - 1\); SR ≤ \(2^{k-1}\).

II.7 · Transport layer

TCP: the connection, the segment, sequence and acknowledgement numbers

What a TCP connection is, every field of the TCP segment, and how byte-stream sequence numbers and cumulative acknowledgement numbers work, with the numericals examiners set on them.

Sources: Kurose §3.5.1–3.5.2 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 77–85 · Chapter3-Problems

The TCP connection

TCP is connection-oriented: before one process can send data to another, the two must first “handshake”, sending preliminary segments to each other to set up the parameters of the transfer. Both sides then initialise many TCP state variables.

  • The connection is logical: its state lives entirely in the two end systems. Routers in between are unaware of it; they see only datagrams.
  • It is full-duplex: data can flow from A to B and from B to A at the same time.
  • It is point-to-point: between exactly one sender and one receiver. Multicasting (one sender to many receivers) is not possible with TCP.

The client sends a special segment, the server replies with a special segment, and the client replies with a third. The first two carry no application data; the third may. Because three segments are exchanged, this is the three-way handshake (Topic II.10).

Once the connection is set up, the client process passes a stream of data through its socket. TCP directs the data to the connection's send buffer, and from time to time takes chunks from it and passes them to the network layer. The maximum amount of application-layer data in a segment is the MSS (maximum segment size). The MSS is set by first finding the largest link-layer frame the local host can send (the MTU, maximum transmission unit), so that one TCP segment plus the TCP/IP headers (typically 40 bytes) fits in one frame. Ethernet's MTU is 1,500 bytes, so a typical MSS is 1,460 bytes. MSS counts only application data, not headers.

At the other end, each segment's data goes into the connection's receive buffer, from which the application reads the stream.

The TCP segment

FieldSizePurpose
Source and destination port numbers16 bits eachMultiplexing and demultiplexing, as in UDP
Sequence number32 bitsByte-stream number of the first data byte in the segment
Acknowledgement number32 bitsSequence number of the next byte the sender of this segment expects
Header length4 bitsLength of the TCP header in 32-bit words (variable because of options; normally 5 words = 20 bytes)
Flags6 (+2) bitsACK: the acknowledgement field is valid. RST, SYN, FIN: connection setup and teardown. CWR, ECE: explicit congestion notification. PSH: pass data to the upper layer immediately. URG: urgent data present
Receive window16 bitsFlow control: the number of bytes the receiver is willing to accept
Checksum16 bitsError detection, computed like UDP's
Urgent data pointer16 bitsLocation of the last byte of urgent data (rarely used)
OptionsVariableMSS negotiation, window scaling for high-speed links, timestamps

Sequence numbers and acknowledgement numbers

These two fields are the heart of TCP's reliable service.

TCP views data as an unstructured, but ordered, stream of bytes. TCP's sequence numbers count bytes, not segments. The sequence number for a segment is the byte-stream number of the first byte in the segment.

Worked exampleNumbering a file (Kurose)
Given

Host A sends a 500,000-byte file to Host B; MSS = 1,000 bytes; the first byte of the stream is numbered 0.

Required

The number of segments and their sequence numbers.

Formula

Segments \( = \lceil 500{,}000 / 1{,}000 \rceil \); segment \(i\) starts at byte \( 1{,}000(i-1) \).

Substitution

500 segments.

Calculation

Segment 1 → sequence number 0 (bytes 0–999); segment 2 → 1,000 (bytes 1,000–1,999); segment 3 → 2,000; … segment 500 → 499,000.

Final answer

500 segments with sequence numbers 0, 1000, 2000, …, 499000.

The acknowledgement number that Host A puts in its segment is the sequence number of the next byte Host A is expecting from Host B.

  • If A has received all bytes numbered 0 to 535 from B, and is about to send a segment to B, it puts 536 in the acknowledgement field.
  • If A has received bytes 0–535 and 900–1,000, but not 536–899, it still puts 536: it is still waiting for byte 536. Because TCP acknowledges bytes only up to the first missing byte, it provides cumulative acknowledgements.
  • What does the receiver do with the out-of-order bytes 900–1,000? The TCP standards leave it to the implementer. In practice receivers keep them and wait for the missing bytes.
  • In practice both sides choose a random initial sequence number, so a segment from an older connection that is still in the network is not mistaken for a valid segment of a new connection between the same ports.

Telnet: piggybacked acknowledgements

In Telnet (remote login), each character the user types is sent to the server, which echoes it back. Suppose the client's starting sequence number is 42 and the server's is 79, and the user types the single letter “C”.

  1. A sends seq 42 (first byte it sends), ack 79 (the first byte it expects from B), data “C”.
  2. B acknowledges with ack 43 (it has received byte 42) and echoes “C” with its own seq 79. The acknowledgement for A's data is carried in a data segment from B to A: it is piggybacked.
  3. A acknowledges B's echo with ack 80; this segment carries no data but still has a sequence number (43), as every segment must.
Interactive visualizationTCP connection simulatorThree-way handshake with real initial sequence numbers, data transfer with sequence and ACK numbers on every segment, the sliding send window and receive window (flow control), and the four-segment close.
Open simulator →

Worked numericals

From a paperTwo back-to-back segments 19CCE304 End 2023 Q3

Host B has received all bytes up through byte 126 from Host A. A then sends two segments back to back with 80 and 40 bytes of data; the first has sequence number 127, source port 302 and destination port 80. B acknowledges every segment it receives.

Given

Next expected byte at B = 127. Segment 1: seq 127, 80 bytes (bytes 127–206). Segment 2: 40 bytes.

Required

(A) the second segment's sequence number and ports; (B) the ACK for the first segment: ack number and ports.

Formula

Next seq = previous seq + previous length. ACK number = next byte expected. Replies swap ports.

Substitution

\(127 + 80\).

Calculation

Segment 2: seq = 207, source port 302, destination port 80. After segment 1 arrives, B expects byte 207.

Final answer

(A) Seq 207, source port 302, destination port 80. (B) ACK number 207, source port 80, destination port 302. (If the second segment arrived first, B's ACK would carry 127: it still expects byte 127.)

Level 2A window of five segments, some lost (Chapter3-Problems)
Given

Initial sequence number 61; the first five segments have sequence numbers 61, 841, 1621, 2401, 3181; segments 2, 3 and 4 are lost.

Required

The segment size, and the ACK numbers the receiver sends.

Formula

Segment size = difference between consecutive sequence numbers. ACK = next byte expected.

Substitution

\(841 - 61\).

Calculation

Each segment carries 780 bytes. Segment 1 arrives → ACK 841. Segments 2–4 never arrive (no ACK). Segment 5 (seq 3181) arrives out of order → the receiver still expects 841 → ACK 841 (a duplicate).

Final answer

780-byte segments; ACKs: 841, x, x, x, 841.

From a paperWhen do TCP sequence numbers run out? 19CCE304 Jan 2023 Q3(B)
Given

The sequence-number field has 4 bytes (32 bits); MSS = 536 bytes; 66 bytes of transport, network and link headers per segment; a 155 Mbps link; no flow or congestion control.

Required

(a) the largest file size L that doesn't exhaust the sequence numbers; (b) the time to send it.

Formula

(a) \(L_{max} = 2^{32}\) bytes. (b) Segments \(= \lceil L / 536 \rceil\); total bytes \(= L + 66 \times \text{segments}\); time \(= 8 \times \text{total} / R\).

Substitution

\(2^{32} = 4{,}294{,}967{,}296\) bytes; \(4{,}294{,}967{,}296 / 536 = 8{,}012{,}998.7\).

Calculation

Segments = 8,012,999. Header bytes \(= 66 \times 8{,}012{,}999 = 528{,}857{,}934\). Total \(= 4{,}294{,}967{,}296 + 528{,}857{,}934 = 4{,}823{,}825{,}230\) bytes \(= 38{,}590{,}601{,}840\) bits. Time \(= 38{,}590{,}601{,}840 / (155 \times 10^6) = 249\) s.

Final answer

(a) About 4.29 GB (\(2^{32}\) bytes). (b) About 249 seconds.

Exam focus
  • Know every header field and its size, and that the minimum header is 20 bytes (UDP: 8).
  • Sequence number = first byte's number; ACK number = next byte expected; cumulative.
  • Piggybacking (Telnet example).
  • Seq/ACK numericals: add the segment lengths; ACK the first missing byte.
Common mistakes
  • Numbering segments 0, 1, 2 instead of by bytes.
  • Setting the ACK number to the last byte received instead of the next byte expected.
  • Thinking a pure ACK segment has no sequence number. Every segment has one.
Quick revision
  • TCP: connection-oriented, full-duplex, point-to-point, state only in end systems; send and receive buffers; MSS ≈ 1,460 bytes.
  • Header ≥ 20 bytes: ports, 32-bit seq and ack, header length, flags (ACK, RST, SYN, FIN, PSH, URG, CWR, ECE), receive window, checksum, urgent pointer, options.
  • Seq = byte number of the first data byte; ACK = next expected byte (cumulative); random initial sequence numbers.

II.8 · Transport layer

RTT estimation and the timeout interval

How TCP measures round-trip times, smooths them with an exponentially weighted moving average, measures their variability, and sets the retransmission timeout, including why premature timeouts happen.

Sources: Kurose §3.5.3 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 86–88 · Chapter3-Problems

Why the timeout must be chosen carefully

TCP recovers from loss with a timeout-and-retransmit mechanism. The timeout must be longer than the connection's RTT (the time from sending a segment until it is acknowledged), or there will be unnecessary retransmissions. But how much longer? If it is far too long, TCP reacts slowly to a lost segment. And the RTT itself varies with router queues and the load on the end systems.

Timeout too shortTimeout too long
Premature timeout: the ACK was on its way, but the sender retransmits anywaySlow reaction to a real loss: the connection sits idle
Wasted bandwidth on duplicate segmentsLower throughput

Measuring SampleRTT

The SampleRTT of a segment is the time from when it is sent (passed to IP) until its acknowledgement is received. Most TCP implementations take one SampleRTT measurement at a time rather than one per segment: at any moment, SampleRTT is being measured for only one transmitted but currently unacknowledged segment, giving a new value about once per RTT.

TCP never computes a SampleRTT for a segment that has been retransmitted, only for segments sent once. (If an ACK arrives for a retransmitted segment, there is no way to tell whether it acknowledges the first or the second transmission.)

EstimatedRTT: an exponentially weighted moving average

SampleRTT values fluctuate from segment to segment, so a single sample is atypical. TCP keeps an average:

Estimated RTT (α = 0.125 is recommended, RFC 6298)

\[ \text{EstimatedRTT} = (1 - \alpha)\cdot \text{EstimatedRTT} + \alpha \cdot \text{SampleRTT} \]

With \(\alpha = 1/8\): \( \text{EstimatedRTT} = 0.875 \cdot \text{EstimatedRTT} + 0.125 \cdot \text{SampleRTT} \).

This is an exponential weighted moving average (EWMA): the weight given to a past sample decays exponentially as new samples arrive, so recent samples count more, reflecting the current congestion more closely.

DevRTT: how much the RTT varies

Deviation of the RTT (β = 0.25 is recommended)

\[ \text{DevRTT} = (1 - \beta)\cdot \text{DevRTT} + \beta \cdot \lvert \text{SampleRTT} - \text{EstimatedRTT} \rvert \]

DevRTT is an EWMA of how far SampleRTT strays from EstimatedRTT. If samples vary little, DevRTT is small; if they vary a lot, DevRTT is large.

The timeout interval

The timeout should be at least EstimatedRTT, plus a safety margin that is large when the RTT varies a lot and small when it is steady:

Retransmission timeout

\[ \text{TimeoutInterval} = \text{EstimatedRTT} + 4 \cdot \text{DevRTT} \]

  • An initial TimeoutInterval of 1 second is recommended.
  • When a timeout occurs, TimeoutInterval is doubled (to avoid a premature timeout for the next segment, which is likely to be acknowledged soon).
  • As soon as a segment is received and EstimatedRTT is updated, TimeoutInterval is computed again from the formula.
Interactive visualizationRTT and timeout visualizerWatch segments and ACKs measure SampleRTT, then type your own samples: the table and chart recompute EstimatedRTT, DevRTT and TimeoutInterval, show where a premature timeout would happen, and let you switch between the textbook and RFC update orders.
Open simulator →

Why premature timeouts happen

Suppose the RTT has been steady at 100 ms, so EstimatedRTT ≈ 100 ms and DevRTT is small (say 2 ms): the timeout is about 108 ms. Now a router queue suddenly grows and the next ACK takes 150 ms. The timer expires at 108 ms, before the ACK arrives: a premature timeout. TCP resends a segment that wasn't lost. The large SampleRTT will raise DevRTT (and EstimatedRTT a little), widening the margin for the next segment, and the doubling rule widens it immediately after a timeout. This is exactly why the margin uses DevRTT: a steady RTT allows a tight timeout; a jumpy RTT needs a loose one.

Worked numericals

From a paperFive samples 19CCE304 Mid 2023 Q6 19CSE301 End 2024 Q3(a)
Given

SampleRTTs 106, 120, 140, 90, 115 ms; EstimatedRTT = 100 ms and DevRTT = 5 ms before the first sample; \(\alpha = 0.125\), \(\beta = 0.25\).

Required

EstimatedRTT, DevRTT and TimeoutInterval after each sample.

Formula

\(E \leftarrow 0.875E + 0.125S\); then \(D \leftarrow 0.75D + 0.25\lvert S - E\rvert\) (using the new E, as in Kurose's solutions); \(T = E + 4D\).

Substitution

Sample 1: \(E = 0.875(100) + 0.125(106)\).

Calculation

S₁ = 106: E = 87.5 + 13.25 = 100.75; D = 0.75(5) + 0.25(5.25) = 3.75 + 1.3125 = 5.06; T = 100.75 + 20.25 = 121.00. S₂ = 120: E = 0.875(100.75) + 15 = 88.16 + 15 = 103.16; D = 0.75(5.06) + 0.25(16.84) = 3.80 + 4.21 = 8.01; T = 103.16 + 32.03 = 135.19. S₃ = 140: E = 0.875(103.16) + 17.5 = 107.76; D = 0.75(8.01) + 0.25(32.24) = 6.01 + 8.06 = 14.07; T = 107.76 + 56.26 = 164.02. S₄ = 90: E = 0.875(107.76) + 11.25 = 105.54; D = 0.75(14.07) + 0.25(15.54) = 10.55 + 3.89 = 14.43; T = 105.54 + 57.74 = 163.28. S₅ = 115: E = 0.875(105.54) + 14.375 = 106.72; D = 0.75(14.43) + 0.25(8.28) = 10.83 + 2.07 = 12.89; T = 106.72 + 51.58 = 158.30.

See the table. (Values carried at full precision and rounded to 2 decimals; rounding at every step can change the last digit.)

A contradiction between your materials, and how to handle it

Your uploaded Chapter3-Problems.docx solves its RTT problem (EstimatedRTT 390 ms, DevRTT 21 ms, samples 250, 200, 360 ms) by computing DevRTT with the old EstimatedRTT, which is the order in RFC 6298 (the standard implementations follow it). Kurose's own worked solutions, like the five-sample table above, update EstimatedRTT first and use the new value in DevRTT. Both follow the same formulas; only the order differs, and the results differ slightly. In the exam, state which order you use and apply it consistently. The RFC-order answer for the uploaded problem:

PracticeTimeout doubling
Given

TimeoutInterval = 160 ms. The segment sent at t = 0 is lost; so is its first retransmission.

Required

When are the retransmissions sent?

Formula

After each timeout, TimeoutInterval doubles (until a fresh ACK recomputes it).

Substitution

160 → 320 → 640 ms.

Calculation

First timeout at 160 ms → retransmit, timer 320 ms. Second timeout at 160 + 320 = 480 ms → retransmit, timer 640 ms.

Final answer

Retransmissions at 160 ms and 480 ms; the next timer is 640 ms. This exponential backoff is a mild form of congestion control.

Exam focus
  • The five-sample RTT table appeared in two papers with the same numbers. Learn the layout and the three formulas.
  • State α = 0.125 and β = 0.25, the initial timeout of 1 s, and doubling on timeout.
  • Explain why SampleRTT is not measured for retransmitted segments, and what a premature timeout is.
Common mistakes
  • Swapping the weights: the old EstimatedRTT gets 0.875 and the new sample 0.125.
  • Using \( (S - E) \) without the absolute value in DevRTT.
  • Writing \(E + 4\beta\) or \(E + 4S\). The margin is \(4 \times \text{DevRTT}\).
Quick revision
  • SampleRTT: one at a time, never for retransmitted segments.
  • \(E = 0.875E + 0.125S\); \(D = 0.75D + 0.25\lvert S - E\rvert\); \(T = E + 4D\).
  • Initial timeout 1 s; double on each timeout; recompute on a fresh ACK.
  • Premature timeout: the timer fires before a delayed ACK arrives.

II.9 · Transport layer

TCP reliable data transfer, fast retransmit and flow control

How one retransmission timer, cumulative ACKs and the receiver's ACK rules make TCP reliable; how three duplicate ACKs trigger fast retransmit; and how the receive window stops a fast sender from overflowing a slow receiver.

Sources: Kurose §3.5.4–3.5.5 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 89–100

TCP's reliable data transfer service

TCP creates a reliable service on top of IP's unreliable one: the data stream a process reads from its receive buffer is uncorrupted, without gaps, without duplication and in sequence. Kurose's simplified TCP sender uses only one retransmission timer, even when several segments are outstanding (managing one timer per segment is expensive), and it reacts to three events.

Event at the senderActions
Data received from the applicationCreate a segment with sequence number NextSeqNum; pass it to IP; if the timer isn't running, start it (the timer belongs to the oldest unacknowledged segment); NextSeqNum += length(data)
Timer timeoutRetransmit the not-yet-acknowledged segment with the smallest sequence number; restart the timer
ACK received with ACK field value yIf y > SendBase: SendBase = y (cumulative: all bytes before y are acknowledged); if there are still unacknowledged segments, restart the timer. Otherwise it is a duplicate ACK (see fast retransmit)

Three retransmission scenarios

A lost ACK

A premature timeout

A cumulative ACK covers a lost ACK

  1. Lost ACK: B received the segment but its ACK was lost. A times out and resends; B discards the duplicate bytes but sends the ACK again.
  2. Premature timeout: the ACKs are delayed past the timeout. A resends segment 92 only; when ACK 120 arrives, A knows both segments arrived and doesn't resend segment 100.
  3. Cumulative ACK: the ACK for the first segment is lost, but the ACK for the second (120) arrives before the timeout. Because ACKs are cumulative, A knows both segments arrived.

Doubling the timeout interval

Each time TCP retransmits after a timeout, it sets the next timeout to twice the previous value, instead of deriving it from EstimatedRTT and DevRTT. The intervals grow exponentially after each retransmission. When a new ACK arrives (or new data from above), the timeout is computed from the formula again. This is a limited form of congestion control: a timeout probably means the network is congested, and retransmitting repeatedly would make it worse.

The receiver's ACK policy (RFC 5681)

Event at the receiverTCP receiver action
In-order segment arrives with the expected sequence number; all data up to it is already acknowledgedDelayed ACK: wait up to 500 ms for another in-order segment; if none arrives, send the ACK
In-order segment arrives with the expected sequence number; one other in-order segment is waiting for its ACKImmediately send one cumulative ACK, acknowledging both segments
Out-of-order segment arrives with a higher-than-expected sequence number (a gap is detected)Immediately send a duplicate ACK, indicating the next expected byte (the lower end of the gap)
A segment arrives that partly or completely fills a gapImmediately send an ACK, provided the segment starts at the lower end of the gap

Fast retransmit

A timeout-triggered retransmission can take a long time, because the timeout is fairly long. But the sender can often detect loss well before the timeout from duplicate ACKs: an ACK that re-acknowledges a segment for which the sender has already received an ACK.

Because a TCP sender often sends many segments back to back, if one is lost there will likely be many back-to-back duplicate ACKs. If the TCP sender receives three duplicate ACKs for the same data, it takes this as a sign that the segment after the ACKed data was lost, and retransmits it before its timer expires. This is TCP fast retransmit.

Why three, not one? Segments can be reordered in the network; one or two duplicate ACKs may simply mean a segment arrived slightly out of order. Three duplicates make loss much more likely.

Is TCP Go-Back-N or Selective Repeat?

Like Go-Back-NLike Selective Repeat
Cumulative acknowledgementsReceivers usually buffer correctly received out-of-order segments
The sender keeps only SendBase and NextSeqNumOn a timeout, TCP retransmits only one segment (the oldest unacknowledged), not the whole window

TCP's error recovery is best described as a hybrid of GBN and SR. The selective acknowledgement (SACK) option lets a receiver acknowledge out-of-order segments selectively, making TCP even more like SR.

Flow control

When TCP receives bytes that are correct and in sequence, it places them in the receive buffer. The application reads from this buffer, but not necessarily the instant data arrives: it may be busy. If the application is slow to read, the sender can easily overflow the receive buffer by sending too much too fast.

TCP provides a flow-control service to eliminate this possibility. Flow control is a speed-matching service: it matches the rate at which the sender sends to the rate at which the receiving application reads.

Flow control is not congestion control

Both throttle the sender, but for different reasons. Flow control protects the receiver's buffer. Congestion control (II.11–II.12) protects the network (router buffers and links). They are entirely different mechanisms with different variables (rwnd versus cwnd).

How the receive window works

The receiver (Host B) keeps:

  • RcvBuffer = the size of the receive buffer;
  • LastByteRead = the number of the last byte read by the application from the buffer;
  • LastByteRcvd = the number of the last byte that has arrived and been placed in the buffer.

Because TCP may not overflow the buffer: \( \text{LastByteRcvd} - \text{LastByteRead} \le \text{RcvBuffer} \).

The receive window

\[ \text{rwnd} = \text{RcvBuffer} - (\text{LastByteRcvd} - \text{LastByteRead}) \]

Host B advertises rwnd in the receive window field of every segment it sends to A. Host A makes sure that \[ \text{LastByteSent} - \text{LastByteAcked} \le \text{rwnd} \]

The zero-window problem

If B's buffer fills, B advertises rwnd = 0. Suppose B then has nothing to send A. As B's application empties the buffer, TCP doesn't send new segments with new rwnd values to A (TCP sends a segment only when it has data or an acknowledgement to send). A would never learn that space has opened and would be blocked forever. To solve this, the TCP standard requires A to keep sending segments with one data byte while rwnd is zero. B acknowledges these, and eventually the buffer starts to empty and the ACKs carry a nonzero rwnd.

From a paperA 10 KB receive buffer 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q13(b)
Given

RcvBuffer = 10 KB; the receiver currently has 4 KB of data buffered (received, not yet read).

Required

The flow-control action of receiver and sender.

Formula

\( \text{rwnd} = \text{RcvBuffer} - (\text{LastByteRcvd} - \text{LastByteRead}) \).

Substitution

\( 10 - 4 \).

Calculation

rwnd = 6 KB.

Final answer

The receiver advertises rwnd = 6 KB in the receive-window field of its next segment. The sender keeps its unacknowledged data (LastByteSent − LastByteAcked) at or below 6 KB. If the application reads more, rwnd grows; if rwnd reaches 0, the sender sends 1-byte probe segments until the window reopens.

PracticeFollowing rwnd over time
Given

RcvBuffer = 8,000 bytes. 3,000 bytes arrive; the application reads 1,000; then 4,000 more bytes arrive; then the application reads 2,000.

Required

rwnd after each event.

Formula

rwnd = RcvBuffer − buffered.

Substitution

buffered = arrived − read.

Calculation

After 3,000 arrive: 8,000 − 3,000 = 5,000. After reading 1,000: 8,000 − 2,000 = 6,000. After 4,000 more: 8,000 − 6,000 = 2,000. After reading 2,000: 8,000 − 4,000 = 4,000.

Final answer

5,000 → 6,000 → 2,000 → 4,000 bytes.

Exam focus
  • “Fast retransmit” and “cumulative acknowledgement” are common definition questions (20CYS303 End 2023 Q1(c,d), 20CYS303 End 2024 Q7). Mention three duplicate ACKs and before the timer expires.
  • Draw the three retransmission scenarios.
  • Flow control: the rwnd formula, the sender's constraint, and the zero-window probe.
  • Flow control vs congestion control.
Common mistakes
  • Saying TCP retransmits the whole window on a timeout (that's GBN). TCP resends only the oldest unacknowledged segment.
  • Triggering fast retransmit on the first duplicate ACK. It needs three duplicates (four identical ACKs in total).
  • Confusing rwnd (receiver's buffer) with cwnd (the network).
Quick revision
  • One timer for the oldest unACKed segment; timeout → resend that segment, double the timeout.
  • Receiver: delayed ACK (500 ms), cumulative ACK, immediate duplicate ACK on a gap.
  • Fast retransmit: 3 duplicate ACKs → resend immediately.
  • TCP = hybrid of GBN and SR; SACK option.
  • rwnd = RcvBuffer − (LastByteRcvd − LastByteRead); LastByteSent − LastByteAcked ≤ rwnd; 1-byte probes when rwnd = 0.

II.10 · Transport layer

TCP connection management

Opening a connection with the three-way handshake, why two messages aren't enough, closing with FIN and ACK, the TCP state machines, and what RST and SYN floods are.

Sources: Kurose §3.5.6 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 101–110, 150

Establishing a connection: the three-way handshake

  1. Step 1, SYN: the client sends a special segment with no application data and the SYN bit set to 1 (a SYN segment). It chooses a random initial sequence number, client_isn, and puts it in the sequence-number field.
  2. Step 2, SYNACK: when the SYN arrives, the server allocates TCP buffers and variables for the connection and sends a connection-granted segment with SYN = 1, acknowledgement field = client_isn + 1, and its own random initial sequence number server_isn. It says, in effect, “I received your SYN to start a connection with your initial sequence number client_isn. I agree. My initial sequence number is server_isn.”
  3. Step 3, ACK: the client allocates its buffers and variables and sends a segment acknowledging the server's segment (ack = server_isn + 1), with SYN = 0, since the connection is now established. This third segment may carry application data.

Why not just two messages?

With a two-way handshake (request, accept), problems arise because messages can be delayed and retransmitted:

  • Half-open connection: the client's request is delayed, the client times out and retransmits, the connection completes and later closes; then the delayed original request arrives, and the server opens a connection that the client knows nothing about.
  • Duplicate data accepted: an old retransmitted data segment from a finished connection could be accepted on a new connection.

The third message, and random initial sequence numbers, let each side confirm that the other really is responding to this connection's sequence numbers.

A human three-way handshake

“Let's talk; I'll start at sentence 42.” → “OK, I heard you're at 42; I'll start at 79.” → “Great, I heard 79.” Only now are both sure the other is listening and knows where the conversation starts.

Closing a connection

Either side can end the connection. Suppose the client decides to close:

  1. The client sends a segment with the FIN bit set to 1.
  2. The server acknowledges it (ACK). The server may still send data in the other direction (a half-closed connection).
  3. The server sends its own FIN segment.
  4. The client acknowledges the server's FIN, and enters TIME_WAIT. TIME_WAIT lets the client resend the final ACK if it was lost (the server would retransmit its FIN). It typically lasts 30 seconds, 1 minute or 2 minutes (two maximum segment lifetimes). Then the connection formally closes and all resources (including port numbers) are released.

Because four segments are exchanged, this is often called the four-way handshake for closing.

Interactive visualizationOpening and closing in the TCP simulatorStep through SYN, SYNACK and ACK with the state names on both sides, then the FIN/ACK/FIN/ACK close and the TIME_WAIT timer.
Open simulator →

The TCP state machines

Client states (typical)Server states (typical)
CLOSED → (send SYN) → SYN_SENTCLOSED → (listen) → LISTEN
SYN_SENT → (receive SYNACK, send ACK) → ESTABLISHEDLISTEN → (receive SYN, send SYNACK) → SYN_RCVD
ESTABLISHED → (send FIN) → FIN_WAIT_1SYN_RCVD → (receive ACK) → ESTABLISHED
FIN_WAIT_1 → (receive ACK) → FIN_WAIT_2ESTABLISHED → (receive FIN, send ACK) → CLOSE_WAIT
FIN_WAIT_2 → (receive FIN, send ACK) → TIME_WAITCLOSE_WAIT → (send FIN) → LAST_ACK
TIME_WAIT → (wait 30 s–2 min) → CLOSEDLAST_ACK → (receive ACK) → CLOSED

When no one is listening: RST

If a host receives a TCP SYN for a port with no socket accepting connections (say port 80 with no web server running), it replies with a special reset segment with the RST flag set to 1: “I don't have a socket for that segment; please don't resend it.” (For UDP, the host instead sends a special ICMP datagram.)

A port scanner such as nmap uses this: it sends a SYN to a target port. SYNACK back → an application is listening (port open). RST back → no application, but no firewall in the way (port closed). Nothing back → a firewall probably blocked the SYN.

The SYN flood attack

In a SYN flood, the attacker sends a huge number of TCP SYN segments (often with spoofed source addresses) and never completes the third step. The server allocates and initialises connection state for each, so its resources run out and legitimate clients are denied service. The defence is SYN cookies: the server doesn't allocate state on receiving a SYN; it computes its initial sequence number as a hash of the source and destination IPs and ports and a secret number (a cookie), and allocates a connection only when a valid ACK returns whose acknowledgement number matches cookie + 1.

From a paperHandshake plus file 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q11(b)
Given

RTT = 10 ms; transferring the file from the server takes 25 ms.

Required

Total time from the start of the TCP connection request until the file is received.

Formula

SYN → SYNACK = 1 RTT; ACK + request → start of response = 1 RTT; then the file's transfer time.

Substitution

\( 10 + 10 + 25 \).

Calculation

45 ms.

Final answer

45 ms. (Draw the three-way handshake with the HTTP request piggybacked on the third segment, then the response.)

Exam focus
  • 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q12(a): the three-way handshake and the four-way close with neat diagrams (4 marks). Label SYN, ACK, FIN bits and sequence/acknowledgement numbers.
  • Explain why a two-way handshake fails.
  • Know the state names, TIME_WAIT's purpose, RST and SYN flood/SYN cookies.
Common mistakes
  • Setting the SYNACK's ack number to client_isn. It is client_isn + 1 (the SYN consumes one sequence number).
  • Forgetting that the third segment may carry data.
  • Thinking the server closes immediately after the client's FIN. It can keep sending until it sends its own FIN.
Quick revision
  • SYN (client_isn) → SYNACK (server_isn, ack client_isn + 1, buffers allocated) → ACK (ack server_isn + 1, may carry data).
  • Two-way handshakes fail on delayed and retransmitted messages.
  • Close: FIN → ACK → FIN → ACK; TIME_WAIT then CLOSED.
  • RST for a closed port; SYN flood defeated with SYN cookies.

II.11 · Transport layer

Principles of congestion control

What congestion costs (huge delays, wasted retransmissions, wasted upstream work), shown with Kurose's three scenarios, and the two broad ways to control it.

Sources: Kurose §3.6 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 111–121

What congestion is

Packet loss usually happens when router buffers overflow because the network is congested. Retransmission treats the symptom (lost packets) but not the cause: too many sources trying to send data at too high a rate. To treat the cause, senders must be throttled when the network is congested.

Congestion control vs flow control (again)

Flow control: one sender must not overwhelm one receiver. Congestion control: too many senders, sending too fast, must not overwhelm the network.

The causes and costs of congestion: three scenarios

Scenario 1: two senders, a router with infinite buffers

Hosts A and B each send at \(\lambda_{in}\) bytes/s through one router with an outgoing link of capacity \(R\) and an unlimited buffer. There is no loss and no retransmission.

  • Per-connection throughput rises with \(\lambda_{in}\) up to \(R/2\), and can never exceed \(R/2\) (the two connections share the link).
  • But as \(\lambda_{in}\) approaches \(R/2\), the average queuing delay grows without bound.

Cost 1: large queuing delays are experienced as the packet-arrival rate nears the link capacity.

Scenario 2: two senders, a router with finite buffers

Now the buffer is finite, so packets are dropped when it is full, and senders retransmit lost packets. Let \(\lambda_{in}\) be the rate the application sends original data, and \(\lambda'_{in}\) the offered load: original data plus retransmissions.

  • If the sender somehow knew when the buffer had space and sent only then (no loss), throughput would equal \(\lambda'_{in}\) up to \(R/2\).
  • If it retransmits only packets known to be lost, then at an offered load of \(R/2\), the rate of data actually delivered to the application (the goodput) is lower, say \(R/3\): a share of the transmitted data is retransmissions.
  • If it also suffers premature timeouts, it retransmits packets that were only delayed. Each such packet may be forwarded twice, so with every packet forwarded twice, goodput approaches \(R/4\).

Cost 2: the sender must retransmit to compensate for packets dropped because of buffer overflow. Cost 3: unneeded retransmissions (after a large delay) make routers use their link bandwidth to forward unnecessary copies.

Scenario 3: four senders, multihop paths

Four hosts each send over two-hop paths that overlap, so each router carries traffic that has already crossed another router. As the offered load grows very large, packets that crossed the first router are increasingly dropped at the second, where they compete with “fresh” traffic.

  • At high load, end-to-end throughput falls towards zero.
  • Cost 4: when a packet is dropped along a path, the transmission capacity used at each upstream link to forward it to the drop point is wasted.
ScenarioSetupCost of congestion it shows
12 senders, 1 router, infinite bufferLarge queuing delays as the rate approaches capacity
22 senders, finite buffer, retransmissionsRetransmissions reduce goodput; unneeded retransmissions waste link capacity
34 senders, multihop pathsUpstream capacity wasted on packets later dropped; throughput can collapse

Approaches to congestion control

End-to-end congestion control

The network layer gives no explicit support. End systems must infer congestion from what they observe: packet loss (a timeout or three duplicate ACKs) and rising delay. This is TCP's classic approach, because IP gives no congestion feedback. TCP decreases its window when it sees loss.

Network-assisted congestion control

Routers give explicit feedback to senders about congestion. The feedback can be a single bit (as in IBM SNA, DEC DECnet and ATM ABR), or an explicit rate the router can support. It can be sent directly as a choke packet from the router to the sender, or, more commonly, a router marks a field in a packet flowing to the receiver, and the receiver tells the sender. TCP/IP's ECN (explicit congestion notification) uses two bits in the IP header and the TCP ECE/CWR flags this way.

Exam focus
  • 19CCE304 End 2024 Q3(B): explain congestion control with an example. Use scenario 2 or 3 and the costs.
  • Know the four costs and the two approaches, with an example of each.
Quick revision
  • Congestion = too many senders too fast for the network; symptoms: loss, delay.
  • Costs: queuing delay near capacity; retransmissions cut goodput; unneeded retransmissions waste capacity; dropped packets waste upstream capacity.
  • End-to-end (TCP infers from loss/delay) vs network-assisted (routers mark or send choke packets; ECN, ATM ABR).

II.12 · Transport layer

TCP congestion control

The congestion window, slow start, congestion avoidance, fast retransmit and fast recovery; TCP Tahoe vs TCP Reno; AIMD and its sawtooth; CUBIC; and every kind of cwnd question set in your papers.

Sources: Kurose §3.7–3.7.1 · Chapter_3_v8.2 slides 122–134 · Chapter3-Problems

The approach

TCP uses end-to-end congestion control: each sender limits the rate at which it sends traffic as a function of the congestion it perceives. Three questions: how does a sender limit its rate, how does it perceive congestion, and what algorithm does it use to change the rate?

How the sender limits its rate: the congestion window

Each side of a connection keeps a variable called the congestion window, cwnd. The amount of unacknowledged data at the sender may not exceed the minimum of cwnd and rwnd:

Sending constraint and approximate rate

\[ \text{LastByteSent} - \text{LastByteAcked} \le \min\{\text{cwnd},\ \text{rwnd}\} \]

Ignoring rwnd (large receive buffer), the sender sends about cwnd bytes per RTT: \[ \text{rate} \approx \frac{\text{cwnd}}{\text{RTT}} \ \text{bytes/s} \]

How the sender perceives congestion

A loss event is either a timeout or the receipt of three duplicate ACKs. When there is excessive congestion, router buffers overflow and a datagram (containing a segment) is dropped, causing a loss event. When things are fine, acknowledgements arrive; TCP uses them to increase cwnd. Because ACKs pace the increase, TCP is said to be self-clocking: if ACKs arrive slowly (a congested path), cwnd grows slowly; if they arrive quickly, it grows quickly.

The guiding principles

  1. A lost segment implies congestion, so the sender's rate should be decreased when a segment is lost.
  2. An acknowledged segment indicates the network is delivering, so the rate can be increased when an ACK for a previously unacknowledged segment arrives.
  3. Bandwidth probing: TCP keeps increasing its rate on ACKs until a loss occurs, then backs off, then starts probing again to see whether the available rate has changed.

The TCP congestion-control algorithm (RFC 5681)

It has three components: slow start, congestion avoidance and fast recovery. Slow start and congestion avoidance are mandatory; fast recovery is recommended.

Interactive visualizationTCP congestion window simulatorSet the initial cwnd, ssthresh, the rounds where a timeout or triple duplicate ACK occurs, and Tahoe or Reno. The graph animates round by round with every phase coloured, and the table shows cwnd, ssthresh and the cumulative segments sent.
Open simulator →

Slow start

  • When a connection begins, cwnd is typically 1 MSS, so the initial rate is about MSS/RTT (for MSS = 500 bytes and RTT = 200 ms, only 20 kbps).
  • In slow start, cwnd increases by 1 MSS every time a transmitted segment is first acknowledged. TCP sends 1 segment; when its ACK arrives, cwnd = 2, so it sends 2; when both ACKs arrive, cwnd = 4, and so on.
  • So cwnd doubles every RTT: the rate starts slow but grows exponentially.

Slow start ends in one of three ways:

  1. A timeout: set ssthresh = cwnd / 2, set cwnd = 1 MSS, and start slow start again. (ssthresh is the slow-start threshold.)
  2. cwnd reaches or passes ssthresh: slow start ends and TCP moves to congestion avoidance, because continuing to double might be reckless near the point where congestion was last seen.
  3. Three duplicate ACKs: TCP performs a fast retransmit and enters fast recovery.

Congestion avoidance

Entering congestion avoidance, cwnd is about half its value when congestion was last encountered. Rather than doubling every RTT, TCP now increases cwnd by just 1 MSS every RTT: a linear increase. A common way to do this is to increase cwnd by \(\text{MSS} \cdot (\text{MSS}/\text{cwnd})\) bytes for each new ACK. (With 10 segments per window, each ACK adds 1/10 MSS, so after one RTT's worth of ACKs cwnd has grown by 1 MSS.)

Congestion avoidance's linear increase ends when:

  • a timeout occurs: ssthresh = cwnd / 2, cwnd = 1 MSS, back to slow start (the same as in slow start);
  • three duplicate ACKs arrive: the network is still delivering some segments, so TCP reacts less drastically: ssthresh = cwnd / 2, cwnd = ssthresh + 3 MSS (adding 3 MSS for the three duplicate ACKs), and it enters fast recovery.

Fast recovery

In fast recovery, cwnd is increased by 1 MSS for every duplicate ACK received for the missing segment that caused TCP to enter this state. When an ACK finally arrives for the missing segment, TCP sets cwnd = ssthresh and enters congestion avoidance. If a timeout occurs instead, it does the same as in the other states: ssthresh = cwnd / 2, cwnd = 1, slow start.

Summary of the algorithm

StateEventActionNext state
Slow startNew ACKcwnd += 1 MSS (doubles per RTT)Slow start, or congestion avoidance if cwnd ≥ ssthresh
Slow start3 duplicate ACKsFast retransmit; ssthresh = cwnd/2; cwnd = ssthresh + 3Fast recovery
Congestion avoidanceNew ACKcwnd += MSS·(MSS/cwnd) (≈ +1 MSS per RTT)Congestion avoidance
Congestion avoidance3 duplicate ACKsFast retransmit; ssthresh = cwnd/2; cwnd = ssthresh + 3Fast recovery
Fast recoveryDuplicate ACKcwnd += 1 MSSFast recovery
Fast recoveryNew ACKcwnd = ssthreshCongestion avoidance
Any stateTimeoutssthresh = cwnd/2; cwnd = 1 MSS; retransmitSlow start

TCP Tahoe versus TCP Reno

TCP Tahoe (early version)TCP Reno (newer version)
On timeoutssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = 1, slow startSame
On 3 duplicate ACKsTreated like a timeout: ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = 1 MSS, slow startFast recovery: ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = ssthresh + 3 (textbook graphs often simply show cwnd = ssthresh), then linear growth
Fast retransmitYesYes
Fast recoveryNoYes
EffectEvery loss collapses the window to 1Isolated losses only halve the window

In both cases ssthresh becomes 6 after the loss (half of 12). Reno continues linearly from 9 (= 6 + 3) in this plot; Tahoe restarts slow start from 1, doubling 1 → 2 → 4, is capped at ssthresh 6, then grows linearly.

AIMD and the sawtooth

Ignoring the initial slow start, and assuming losses are signalled by triple duplicate ACKs, TCP's congestion control is additive increase, multiplicative decrease (AIMD): cwnd grows by 1 MSS per RTT and is halved on each loss event. The result is the characteristic sawtooth pattern of cwnd over time. AIMD was shown (Jacobson, Chiu and Jain) to converge to an efficient and fair sharing of a bottleneck among competing connections.

Average throughput of a long-lived TCP connection (Reno)

If \(W\) is the window size (in bytes) when a loss occurs, cwnd oscillates between \(W/2\) and \(W\) in a sawtooth, so on average it is \(0.75W\):

Macroscopic TCP throughput

\[ \text{average throughput} = \frac{0.75\,W}{\text{RTT}} \]

For W = 20 segments of 1,500 bytes and RTT = 100 ms: \(0.75 \times 20 \times 1{,}500 \times 8 / 0.1 = 1.8\) Mbps.

TCP CUBIC

AIMD's linear increase is cautious. If the bottleneck's state probably hasn't changed much since the last loss, it would be better to climb back quickly towards the rate at which loss occurred and then probe cautiously. TCP CUBIC (the default in Linux, widely deployed) does this. Let \(W_{max}\) be cwnd when the last loss was detected. CUBIC grows cwnd as a cubic function of the time since the loss: fast when cwnd is far below \(W_{max}\), slowly as it approaches \(W_{max}\), and then increasingly fast again beyond \(W_{max}\), to probe for a new operating point. Increases depend on time rather than on ACK arrivals, so CUBIC is fairer to connections with different RTTs, and it tends to keep the connection near \(W_{max}\) for longer.

Worked numericals

From a paperTahoe: slow start to 16, then a loss at RTT 10 23CSE302 End 2025 Q4
Given

TCP Tahoe; initial cwnd = 1 MSS; ssthresh = 16 MSS; receiver window large; an ACK for every segment; constant RTT; triple duplicate ACK at the end of RTT 10.

Required

(a) the RTTs needed for cwnd to reach 16, and the packets sent in slow start; (b) cwnd at the start of each RTT up to RTT 14, segments sent and cumulative total; how Tahoe's recovery differs from Reno's.

Formula

Slow start doubles per RTT while cwnd < ssthresh; congestion avoidance adds 1 per RTT; Tahoe on any loss: ssthresh = ⌊cwnd/2⌋, cwnd = 1.

Substitution

Start: cwnd = 1, ssthresh = 16.

Calculation

(a) cwnd at the start of RTTs 1–5: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16. It takes 4 RTTs of slow start to reach 16; packets sent in slow start = 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15. (b) From RTT 5 congestion avoidance: 16, 17, 18, 19, 20, 21 (RTT 10). Loss at the end of RTT 10 with cwnd = 21: ssthresh = ⌊21/2⌋ = 10, cwnd = 1. RTTs 11–14 (slow start): 1, 2, 4, 8.

Final answer

(a) 4 RTTs; 15 packets. (b) See the table. Tahoe treats the triple duplicate ACK like a timeout and drops cwnd to 1, wasting several RTTs re-growing the window. Reno would perform fast recovery: ssthresh = 10 and cwnd = 10 + 3 = 13 (or 10 in simplified graphs), then grow linearly (13, 14, 15, …), keeping most of its throughput.
