From a paperAIMD with a timeout in the fifth transmission 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q13(a)
Given

Window at the start of slow start = 2 MSS; threshold at the first transmission = 8 MSS; a timeout during the 5th transmission.

Required

cwnd at the end of the 10th transmission.

Formula

Slow start doubles (not beyond ssthresh); congestion avoidance +1; on timeout ssthresh = cwnd/2 and cwnd returns to the initial window (2 MSS here).

Substitution

Rounds 1–3: 2, 4, 8 (8 = threshold).

Calculation

Round 4: 9; round 5: 10 → timeout: ssthresh = 10/2 = 5, cwnd = 2. Round 6: 2; round 7: 4; round 8: min(8, 5) = 5 (reaches ssthresh); round 9: 6; round 10: 7.

Final answer

7 MSS in the 10th transmission.

From a paperA timeout during the fifth transmission round, ssthresh 8 23CSE302 Mid 2025 Q4
Given

cwnd starts at 1 MSS; ssthresh = 8 MSS; a timeout occurs during the 5th transmission (round).

Required

cwnd after the acknowledgements of the 9th transmission arrive.

Formula

As above, with cwnd returning to 1 MSS on timeout and ssthresh = ⌊cwnd/2⌋.

Substitution

Rounds 1–4: 1, 2, 4, 8 (reaches ssthresh 8).

Calculation

Round 5: 9 → timeout: ssthresh = ⌊9/2⌋ = 4, cwnd = 1. Round 6: 1; round 7: 2; round 8: 4 (= ssthresh, so congestion avoidance from here); round 9: 5. When all of round 9's ACKs arrive, cwnd increases by 1 MSS.

Final answer

cwnd = 5 MSS during the 9th transmission, and 6 MSS once its acknowledgements have been received (the value the question asks for). State your interpretation: if you keep ssthresh = 4.5, round 8 is still slow start and the numbers shift slightly.

From a paperReading a Reno cwnd graph 19CSE301 End 2023 Q3(a) 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q5 20CYS303 End 2023 Q13

These papers reproduce Kurose's Problem P40 graph: cwnd rises 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32 (rounds 1–6), then linearly to 42 (round 16), drops to 24 (round 17) and rises linearly to 29 (round 22), then drops to 1 (round 23) and rises 1, 2, 4, 8 (rounds 23–26).

Given

The cwnd values above.

Required

(a) slow-start intervals; (b) congestion-avoidance intervals; (c) the loss after round 16; (d) the loss after round 22; (e) ssthresh values; (f) cwnd and ssthresh if a triple duplicate ACK is detected after round 26.

Formula

Exponential growth = slow start; linear = congestion avoidance; drop to about half = triple duplicate ACK; drop to 1 = timeout.

Substitution

Read the shape.

Calculation

(a) Rounds 1–6 and 23–26. (b) Rounds 6–16 and 17–22. (c) cwnd falls from 42 to 24 (= 21 + 3), not to 1, so it was a triple duplicate ACK. (d) cwnd falls to 1: a timeout. (e) Initial ssthresh = 32 (slow start stops at 32); after round 16, ssthresh = 42/2 = 21; after round 22, ssthresh = ⌊29/2⌋ = 14. (f) At round 26 cwnd = 8: ssthresh = 4, cwnd = 4 + 3 = 7 MSS.

Final answer

SS 1–6, 23–26; CA 6–16, 17–22; round 16: triple duplicate ACK; round 22: timeout; ssthresh 32 → 21 → 14; after round 26: ssthresh = 4, cwnd = 7.

From a paperShort Reno questions 20CYS303 End 2025 Q11
Given

(a) cwnd = 1, ssthresh = 32, two RTTs fully acknowledged. (b) Reno, triple duplicate ACK at cwnd = 50 MSS. (c) The switch from slow start to congestion avoidance.

Required

(a) cwnd at the start of RTT 3; (b) the new ssthresh and the cwnd on entering fast recovery; (c) the condition and the principle.

Formula

Slow start doubles per RTT; Reno: ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = ssthresh + 3.

Substitution

(a) 1 → 2 → 4. (b) 50/2.

Calculation

(a) 4 MSS. (b) ssthresh = 25 MSS; cwnd = 25 + 3 = 28 MSS. (c) When cwnd ≥ ssthresh.

Final answer

(a) 4 MSS. (b) 25 MSS and 28 MSS. (c) TCP leaves slow start when cwnd reaches ssthresh: it has come back to half the window at which congestion was last seen, so it stops doubling and probes cautiously with additive increase (the AI of AIMD).

Exam focus
  • TCP congestion control appears in every end-semester paper. Practise building the round-by-round table and reading graphs.
  • Always write the three rules: timeout → ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = 1; 3 dup ACKs → ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = ssthresh + 3 (Reno) or 1 (Tahoe); cwnd ≥ ssthresh → linear growth.
  • Explain Tahoe vs Reno (20CYS303 End 2024 Q11).
  • Throughput ≈ 0.75 W / RTT; rate ≈ cwnd / RTT.
Common mistakes
  • Letting slow start overshoot ssthresh (8 → 16 when ssthresh is 10). Cap it at ssthresh.
  • Resetting ssthresh to the old ssthresh/2 instead of the current cwnd/2.
  • Treating Reno's triple duplicate ACK like a timeout (that's Tahoe).
  • Confusing “transmission round” (one RTT, cwnd segments) with individual segments.
Quick revision
  • Sender limit: unACKed ≤ min(cwnd, rwnd); rate ≈ cwnd/RTT.
  • Loss event = timeout or 3 duplicate ACKs. ACKs clock the growth.
  • Slow start: cwnd = 1, +1 per ACK, doubles per RTT, until ssthresh.
  • Congestion avoidance: +1 MSS per RTT.
  • Timeout: ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = 1. Triple dup ACK (Reno): ssthresh = cwnd/2, cwnd = ssthresh + 3, fast recovery. Tahoe: cwnd = 1.
  • AIMD sawtooth; average throughput 0.75 W/RTT; CUBIC grows as a cubic of time since loss.

II.13 · Network layer

The network layer: overview

The two jobs of the network layer (forwarding and routing), the data plane and the control plane, the traditional and SDN ways of computing forwarding tables, and the best-effort service model.

Sources: Kurose §4.1 · Chapter_4_v8.2 slides 2–12

What the network layer does

The network layer moves datagrams from a sending host to a receiving host. At the sender it encapsulates transport-layer segments into datagrams; at the receiver it delivers the segments to the transport layer. Unlike the transport layer, a network-layer protocol runs in every host and every router: each router examines the header fields of every IP datagram passing through it.

The network layer has two important functions:

ForwardingRouting
Move a packet from a router's input link to the appropriate output linkDetermine the route or path packets take from source to destination
A router-local actionA network-wide process
Takes nanoseconds; usually done in hardwareTakes seconds; done in software
Implemented in the data planeImplemented in the control plane
Analogy: passing through a single highway interchangeAnalogy: planning the whole trip from source to destination

A key element in every router is its forwarding table. A router forwards a packet by examining the values of one or more header fields of the arriving packet and using them to index into its forwarding table. The value stored there indicates the outgoing link interface.

Two ways to build the forwarding tables (the control plane)

The traditional approach: per-router control

A routing algorithm runs in each and every router; both forwarding and routing functions live in the router. The routing-algorithm components in each router communicate with each other by exchanging routing messages (OSPF, BGP) to compute the forwarding tables.

The SDN approach: logically centralised control

A physically separate, remote controller computes and distributes the forwarding tables to be used by every router. Routers only forward. The controller communicates with the routers by exchanging messages that contain forwarding tables. Because the network is “software-defined” by the controller, this is software-defined networking (SDN) (Topic II.20).

The network service model

The network service model defines the characteristics of end-to-end delivery of packets. Services the network layer could provide include:

  • Guaranteed delivery: a packet will eventually arrive.
  • Guaranteed delivery with bounded delay: delivered within a specified delay bound (such as 100 ms).
  • In-order packet delivery.
  • Guaranteed minimal bandwidth: like a link of a given rate between sender and receiver.
  • Security: encryption of datagrams for confidentiality.

The Internet's network layer provides a single service, best-effort service. Packets are neither guaranteed to arrive, nor to arrive in order, nor within any delay, and there is no minimum bandwidth guarantee.

ArchitectureService modelBandwidth guaranteeNo-loss guaranteeOrderingTiming
InternetBest effortNoneNoneAny order possibleNot maintained
ATMConstant bit rate (CBR)Guaranteed constant rateYesIn orderMaintained
ATMAvailable bit rate (ABR)Guaranteed minimumNoneIn orderNot maintained
Internet IntServGuaranteed (RFC 1633)YesYesYesYes
Internet DiffServAssured forwarding (RFC 2475)PossiblePossiblePossibleNo

Why has best effort survived? Its simplicity let the Internet be widely deployed; over-provisioning of bandwidth gives good-enough performance for most applications (streaming, conferencing); replicated application-layer services (CDNs, data centres) bring content close to users; and congestion control of elastic services helps. IntServ and DiffServ were designed but are not widely deployed.

Exam focus
  • Forwarding vs routing, data plane vs control plane, per-router vs SDN control: two-mark definitions and comparisons.
  • The possible network services, and what the Internet's best-effort model does not guarantee.
Quick revision
  • Network layer runs in every host and router; datagrams.
  • Forwarding (data plane, local, ns, hardware) vs routing (control plane, network-wide, s, software).
  • Forwarding table: header field → output link. Built by per-router routing algorithms or a remote SDN controller.
  • Internet service model: best effort, with no delivery, order, delay or bandwidth guarantees.

II.14 · Network layer

Virtual-circuit and datagram networks

Two ways a packet-switched network layer can work: connectionless datagram forwarding by destination address, and connection-oriented virtual circuits with per-link VC numbers set up in advance.

Sources: Forouzan 8.2–8.3 Not in Kurose 8e §4.1–4.3

Source note

Virtual-circuit and datagram networks are on your syllabus but were removed from Kurose's 8th edition. This topic is taught from Forouzan Chapter 8 (Sections 8.2, Datagram networks, and 8.3, Virtual-circuit networks), in your CN_TEXTBOOK_2.pdf, with the comparison that older Kurose editions gave.

Connectionless and connection-oriented network layers

Just as the transport layer can offer a connectionless service (UDP) or a connection-oriented one (TCP), so can the network layer.

  • A datagram network provides only a connectionless service: no setup, every packet routed independently.
  • A virtual-circuit (VC) network provides a connection-oriented service: a path is set up before data flows, and every packet of the connection follows it.

Unlike in the transport layer, a network chooses one of the two for all its traffic. The Internet is a datagram network; ATM and frame relay are VC networks.

Datagram networks

In a datagram network, each time an end system wants to send a packet, it stamps the packet with the address of the destination and pops it into the network, with no circuit setup. Packet switches don't maintain any state about circuits (there are none).

  • Each packet is treated independently of all others, even packets from the same message; the packets are called datagrams.
  • Each switch forwards using a forwarding (routing) table indexed by destination address; the table may change over time as routing protocols update it.
  • Packets of one message may take different routes and can arrive out of order; some may be lost. Upper-layer protocols must reorder or ask for retransmission.
  • There is no setup or teardown phase, so efficiency is better than a circuit-switched network (resources are allocated only while packets are being transferred), although delay may vary from packet to packet.

Virtual-circuit networks

A virtual-circuit network is a cross between a circuit-switched network and a datagram network. A VC consists of:

  1. a path (a series of links and switches) between source and destination;
  2. VC numbers, one for each link along the path;
  3. entries in the forwarding table in each switch along the path.

A packet belonging to a VC carries a VC number (VCI, virtual-circuit identifier) in its header. Because a VC may have a different VC number on each link, each intervening switch replaces the VC number of each traversing packet with a new one, obtained from its forwarding table. (This is label swapping, and the idea reappears in MPLS, Topic III.8.)

Why not one VC number for the whole path? Because choosing a number locally per link avoids a lot of message exchange to agree on a common number across all switches, and a long path would make a globally unique number much longer.

The three phases

1. VC setupThe source contacts the network layer with the receiver's address; the network finds the path, assigns a VC number on each link and adds an entry to each switch's table. It may also reserve resources (bandwidth, buffers) along the path.
2. Data transferPackets flow along the circuit, each carrying the VC number of the link it is on; each switch swaps the number and forwards.
3. VC teardownThe sender (or receiver) asks to end the call; the network removes the table entries in every switch along the path.

Setup uses signalling messages (a setup request travels from source to destination, and an acknowledgement comes back), and the protocols that exchange them are signalling protocols. Because every switch along the path is involved in setup, it keeps connection state for every ongoing connection, unlike a datagram router.

A VC forwarding table

Incoming interfaceIncoming VC #Outgoing interfaceOutgoing VC #
112222
263118
37217
197387

A packet arriving on interface 1 with VC 12 leaves on interface 2 with its VC field rewritten to 22.

From a paperDatagram vs VC forwarding tables 19CCE304 End 2023 Q5

The paper's figure is a small network: hosts H1 and H2 attach to router A (interfaces 1 and 2); A connects to router B through interface 3 and to router C through interface 4; B and C each connect to router D, which connects to H3. (The interface numbers below follow the usual version of this textbook figure; use the numbers in your paper's figure.)

Given

Datagram case: traffic to H3 must leave A on interface 3. VC case: calls H1–H3 and H2–H3; H1's traffic must use interface 3 at A and H2's interface 4.

Required

(A) A's datagram forwarding table; (B) whether a datagram network can split by source; (C) A's VC table; (D) VC tables in B, C and D.

Formula

Datagram: destination → output interface. VC: (in interface, in VC#) → (out interface, out VC#).

Substitution

Choose VC numbers: H1–H3 uses VC 22 on every link; H2–H3 uses VC 17. (Any distinct numbers are fine.)

Calculation

(A) Destination H3 → interface 3. (B) No: a datagram router forwards on the destination address only, so all datagrams to H3 follow the same entry regardless of source. (C) A: in 1 / VC 22 → out 3 / VC 22; in 2 / VC 17 → out 4 / VC 17. (D) B: in 1 / 22 → out 2 / 22. C: in 1 / 17 → out 2 / 17. D: in 1 / 22 → out 3 / 22; in 2 / 17 → out 3 / 17.

Final answer

The datagram table has one row (H3 → 3) and cannot distinguish sources; a VC network can, because each connection has its own table entries.

Datagram versus virtual-circuit networks

FeatureDatagram networkVirtual-circuit network
Connection setupNoneRequired (signalling) before data
Addressing in each packetFull destination addressShort VC number, local to each link
State in switchesNone about connections; only a forwarding tablePer-connection state in every switch on the path
Forwarding table indexed byDestination addressIncoming interface + incoming VC number
Routing decisionMade for every packetMade once, at setup
Path of packetsEach may take a different routeAll follow the same path
Packet orderMay arrive out of orderArrive in order
Effect of a switch failurePackets reroute around itAll circuits through it are lost
Resource reservation and QoSHard (best effort)Easy (reserve at setup)
DelayNo setup delay; variable delaySetup delay; then more uniform delay
ExamplesThe Internet (IP)ATM, frame relay, X.25; ideas reused in MPLS

The Internet chose datagrams because it interconnects many different link technologies, and end systems are smart computers that can handle reordering and reliability themselves (TCP); keeping the network core simple made it easy to grow.

Exam focus
  • The comparison table is a standard 5-mark answer.
  • The three VC phases and the VC table with per-link number swapping.
  • The “trick question”: a datagram network cannot forward by source address.
Common mistakes
  • Saying a VC network reserves a physical circuit. It is packet-switched; the circuit is virtual (resources may or may not be reserved).
  • Giving the same VC number on every link as a rule. Numbers are local and usually change at each switch.
Quick revision
  • Datagram: no setup, destination address in every packet, per-packet forwarding, possible reordering, no switch state (the Internet).
  • VC: setup–transfer–teardown; path + per-link VC numbers + switch table entries; label swapping; in-order; state in switches (ATM, frame relay).

II.15 · Network layer

Inside a router

The four parts of a router, how input ports find the output link with longest-prefix matching, the three kinds of switching fabric, where queues form (including head-of-line blocking), how big buffers should be, and how packets are scheduled.

Sources: Kurose §4.2 · Chapter_4_v8.2 slides 13–40

The four components

  1. Input ports: terminate the incoming physical link (physical layer), interoperate with the link layer at the other end, and perform the crucial lookup function: consult the forwarding table to decide the output port.
  2. Switching fabric: connects the input ports to the output ports. It is a network inside the router.
  3. Output ports: store packets received from the fabric and transmit them on the outgoing link, performing the link-layer and physical-layer functions.
  4. Routing processor: performs control-plane functions. In a traditional router it runs the routing protocols, maintains routing tables and computes the forwarding table; in an SDN router it communicates with the remote controller to receive forwarding-table entries. It also performs network management.

The input ports, fabric and output ports form the data plane, almost always in hardware: a 100 Gbps link with 64-byte datagrams gives an input port only 5.12 ns to process each one. The control plane runs in software on the routing processor at millisecond or second timescales.

Input port processing and destination-based forwarding

The forwarding table is computed by the routing processor (or received from an SDN controller) and copied to each line card, so forwarding decisions are made locally at each input port without going through the central processor for every packet. The input port also checks the version number, checksum and time-to-live, and updates counters.

With 32-bit IP addresses, a brute-force table with one entry per address is impossible. Instead the table lists address prefixes, and the router uses longest prefix matching: when there are several matches, it uses the entry with the longest matching prefix.

Prefix (destination address range)Link interface
11001000 00010111 00010*** ********0
11001000 00010111 00011000 ********1
11001000 00010111 00011*** ********2
Otherwise3
  • Destination 11001000 00010111 00010110 10100001 matches only the first entry → interface 0.
  • Destination 11001000 00010111 00011000 10101010 matches the second and third entries; the second has the longer prefix (24 bits vs 21) → interface 1.

Lookups must be done at line speed, so routers use fast memory such as TCAM (ternary content addressable memory), which returns a lookup result in essentially one clock cycle regardless of table size. Input processing as a whole is “match plus action”: match the header, take the action (forward, drop, copy).

From a paperLongest prefix match at a CIDR router 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q6
Given

Destination 131.23.151.76. Entries: 131.16.0.0/12 → 3; 131.28.0.0/14 → 5; 131.19.0.0/16 → 2; 131.22.0.0/15 → 1.

Required

The output interface.

Formula

An address matches prefix p/n if its first n bits equal p's first n bits; choose the longest match.

Substitution

The first octet (131) matches everywhere; compare the second octet in binary: 23 = 0001 0111.

Calculation

/12 needs the first 4 bits of octet 2: 16 = 0001 0000 → 0001 ✓ (covers 131.16–131.31). /14 needs 6 bits: 28 = 000111 00 vs 000101 ✗. /16 needs all 8: 19 ≠ 23 ✗. /15 needs 7 bits: 22 = 0001011 0 vs 0001011 ✓ (covers 131.22–131.23).

Final answer

Two entries match (/12 and /15); the longest is /15, so the packet leaves on interface 1.

PracticeRanges behind each interface (Kurose-style, 8-bit addresses)
Given

A router with 8-bit host addresses and the table: 00 → 0; 010 → 1; 011 → 2; 10 → 2; 11 → 3 (longest prefix matching).

Required

The address range and number of addresses for each interface.

Formula

A k-bit prefix covers \(2^{8-k}\) addresses.

Substitution

00 = 0–63; 010 = 64–95; 011 = 96–127; 10 = 128–191; 11 = 192–255.

Calculation

Interface 0: 64 addresses. Interface 1: 32. Interface 2: 32 + 64 = 96. Interface 3: 64.

Final answer

0: 00000000–00111111 (64); 1: 01000000–01011111 (32); 2: 01100000–10111111 (96); 3: 11000000–11111111 (64).

Switching fabrics

The switching fabric is at the very heart of a router. There are three classic designs.

FabricHow it worksLimitationExample
Switching via memoryThe earliest routers were ordinary computers: an arriving packet is copied into the processor's memory (by an interrupt), the processor finds the output port, and copies the packet to the output port's bufferTwo memory accesses per packet (write then read), so throughput is at most B/2 for memory bandwidth B packets/s; only one packet at a timeFirst-generation routers; Cisco Catalyst 8500
Switching via a busThe input port transfers the packet directly to the output port over a shared bus, prefixing a label with the output port; all outputs see it but only the matching one keeps itOnly one packet at a time can cross the bus; the switching speed is limited by the bus bandwidthCisco 6500 (32 Gbps backplane bus)
Switching via an interconnection networkA crossbar of 2N buses connecting N inputs to N outputs; each crosspoint can be opened or closed, so packets to different outputs cross at the same time (non-blocking); multistage (Clos) networks and parallel switching planes scale furtherTwo packets for the same output must still waitCisco 12000 and CRS (multistage)
From a paper 20CYS303 End 2023 Q2 20CYS303 End 2024 Q3

“Explain the different types of switching techniques in a router” (5 marks): these three fabrics, a sketch of each, and their limitations.

Where queues form

Packet queues can form at both input ports and output ports. As queues grow, buffers fill and packet loss occurs.

Input queuing and head-of-line blocking

If the switching fabric is not fast enough to transfer all arriving packets through the fabric without delay (it is slower than the input ports combined), packets queue at the input ports. In a crossbar, if two packets at the fronts of two input queues are destined for the same output, one must wait. Worse, a packet behind the blocked one must wait too, even if its own output port is free. This is head-of-line (HOL) blocking: a queued packet in an input queue must wait for transfer through the fabric (even though its output port is free) because it is blocked by another packet at the head of the line. HOL blocking can cap a switch's throughput at about 58% of its capacity under certain assumptions.

Output queuing

Even with a fabric N times faster than one input port, if packets from all N inputs head for the same output, they queue at that output: the output link can send only one at a time. When the output buffer fills, the router must either drop an arriving packet (drop-tail) or remove an already-queued one. Active queue management (AQM) algorithms, such as RED (random early detection), drop or mark (ECN) packets before the buffer is full, to signal congestion to senders early.

How much buffering?

The long-standing rule of thumb (RFC 3439) is that the amount of buffering \(B\) should equal an average round-trip time (say 250 ms) times the link capacity \(C\): a 10 Gbps link would need \(0.25 \times 10^{10} = 2.5\) Gbits of buffer. More recent analysis says that with a large number \(N\) of TCP flows, \(B = \text{RTT} \cdot C / \sqrt{N}\) is enough. Too much buffering is harmful too: it causes long delays (bufferbloat) for interactive applications.

Packet scheduling

When several packets are queued at an output port, the packet scheduler decides which one to send next.

DisciplineHow it worksAnalogy
FIFO (first in, first out, or FCFS)Packets leave in the order they arrivedA single queue at a bank counter
Priority queuingArriving packets are classified into priority classes (by marking, port number, etc.); the scheduler sends from the highest-priority non-empty queue, FIFO within a class. Non-preemptive: a packet being sent isn't interruptedEmergency patients in a hospital queue
Round robin (RR)Packets are sorted into classes; the scheduler alternates among classes, sending one packet from each in turn. A work-conserving scheduler never leaves the link idle while packets are queuedDealing cards round the table
Weighted fair queuing (WFQ)Like round robin, but each class \(i\) gets a weight \(w_i\); in any interval with packets queued, class \(i\) receives at least a fraction \(w_i / \sum_j w_j\) of the link rateSharing time in proportion to agreed shares
WFQ guaranteed rate for class i on a link of rate R

\[ R_i \ge R \cdot \frac{w_i}{\sum_j w_j} \]

From a paper 20CYS303 End 2023 Q3 20CYS303 End 2024 Q4

“Which scheduling would you prefer for Amrita students?” A good answer: WFQ on the campus gateway, with classes such as academic/AUMS/online exams (weight 4), video lectures (weight 3), general browsing (weight 2) and downloads/updates (weight 1). Each class is guaranteed its share, important traffic gets more, and no class starves (unlike strict priority, where heavy high-priority traffic can starve lower classes). For a 1 Gbps link, academic traffic would be guaranteed at least \(1 \times 4/10 = 400\) Mbps.

Exam focus
  • Router architecture diagram with the four components and the data plane / control plane split.
  • Longest prefix matching numericals.
  • Three switching fabrics; input vs output queuing; HOL blocking (20CYS303 End 2023 Q1(e)).
  • FIFO, priority, RR and WFQ, with the WFQ share formula.
Common mistakes
  • Matching the first matching entry instead of the longest.
  • Saying a crossbar can't send any two packets at once. It can, as long as they go to different outputs.
  • Calling HOL blocking an output-queue problem. It happens at input queues.
Quick revision
  • Router = input ports + switching fabric + output ports (data plane, hardware) + routing processor (control plane).
  • Longest prefix matching, done in TCAM; forwarding table copied to each line card.
  • Fabrics: memory (≤ B/2, one at a time), bus (one at a time), interconnection network/crossbar (parallel to distinct outputs).
  • Input queuing → HOL blocking; output queuing → drop-tail or AQM (RED, ECN marking).
  • Buffer ≈ RTT × C (or RTT·C/√N).
  • Scheduling: FIFO, priority, round robin, WFQ (share \(w_i/\sum w_j\)).

II.16 · Network layer

The Internet Protocol: IPv4 datagrams, fragmentation and IPv6

Every field of the IPv4 header, how a large datagram is split to fit a smaller MTU (with the offset arithmetic examiners test), and what IPv6 changed and how the Internet is moving to it.

Sources: Kurose §4.3.1, §4.3.4 · Chapter_4_v8.2 slides 41–43, 74–80, 100–102

The IPv4 datagram format

FieldBitsPurpose
Version4IP version (4); tells the router how to interpret the rest of the header
Header length4Header length in 32-bit words (needed because of options); 5 for a typical 20-byte header
Type of service (TOS)8Distinguishes datagram types (e.g. real-time vs. bulk); 6 bits for differentiated services, 2 for ECN
Datagram length16Total length of the datagram (header + data) in bytes; maximum 65,535, but rarely more than 1,500
Identifier, flags, fragmentation offset16, 3, 13IP fragmentation (below)
Time-to-live (TTL)8Decremented by one at each router; the datagram is dropped when it reaches 0, so it can't circulate forever
Upper-layer protocol8Which transport protocol gets the data: 6 = TCP, 17 = UDP. The “glue” between the network and transport layers, as the port number is between transport and application
Header checksum16Detects bit errors in the header only. Recomputed and stored again at each router, because the TTL (and possibly options) change
Source and destination IP addresses32 eachThe sender's and receiver's addresses
OptionsVariableRarely used; dropped from IPv6
Data (payload)VariableUsually a TCP or UDP segment; can also be ICMP messages

Why does both TCP and IP have checksums? IP's covers only the header; TCP/UDP's covers the whole segment; and TCP/UDP need not run over IP at all.

IP fragmentation and reassembly

Not all link-layer protocols can carry packets of the same size. The maximum amount of data a link-layer frame can carry is the maximum transmission unit (MTU): Ethernet's is 1,500 bytes. A router may receive a datagram larger than the MTU of the outgoing link.

The solution is to fragment the datagram into two or more smaller IP datagrams, each encapsulated in its own frame. The fragments are reassembled only at the destination host, not at routers (to keep routers simple). The header fields that make this possible:

  • Identifier: all fragments of one original datagram carry the same identifier, so the destination knows which fragments belong together.
  • Flags: the more-fragments (MF) bit is 1 in every fragment except the last, which has MF = 0, so the destination knows when it has the last one. (A don't-fragment bit also exists.)
  • Fragmentation offset: where the fragment's data fits in the original datagram, in units of 8 bytes. So the data in every fragment except the last must be a multiple of 8 bytes.
Fragmentation rules
  • Data per fragment \( \le \text{MTU} - \text{header} \), rounded down to a multiple of 8.
  • Offset of a fragment \( = \dfrac{\text{byte position of its first data byte}}{8} \).
  • Every fragment carries its own header; total length = header + its data.
Level 1Kurose's 4,000-byte example (Additional Chapter 4 slides)
Given

A 4,000-byte datagram (20-byte header, 3,980 bytes of data) arrives at a router whose outgoing MTU is 1,500 bytes.

Required

The fragments: length, offset and flag.

Formula

Max data per fragment = 1,500 − 20 = 1,480 (a multiple of 8 ✓).

Substitution

3,980 = 1,480 + 1,480 + 1,020.

Calculation

Fragment 1: data bytes 0–1,479, length 1,500, offset 0, MF = 1. Fragment 2: bytes 1,480–2,959, length 1,500, offset 1,480/8 = 185, MF = 1. Fragment 3: bytes 2,960–3,979, length 1,040, offset 2,960/8 = 370, MF = 0.

Final answer

Three fragments: (1,500, 0, 1), (1,500, 185, 1), (1,040, 370, 0), all with the same identifier.

From a paper5,000 bytes over an MTU of 1,300 19CSE301 End 2024 Q4(a)
Given

Datagram size 5,000 bytes; MTU = 1,300 bytes; assume a 20-byte header (no options).

Required

The number of fragments, and each fragment's details.

Formula

Max data = 1,300 − 20 = 1,280 (multiple of 8 ✓). Total data = 5,000 − 20 = 4,980.

Substitution

\( 4{,}980 = 3 \times 1{,}280 + 1{,}140 \).

Calculation

Fragment 1: data 1,280, length 1,300, offset 0, MF 1. Fragment 2: data 1,280, length 1,300, offset 1,280/8 = 160, MF 1. Fragment 3: data 1,280, length 1,300, offset 320, MF 1. Fragment 4: data 1,140, length 1,160, offset 3,840/8 = 480, MF 0.

Final answer

4 fragments of 1,300, 1,300, 1,300 and 1,160 bytes, with offsets 0, 160, 320, 480.

From a paperA 1,600-byte packet with a 30-byte header 19CSE301 End 2023 Q4(ii)
Given

Packet size 1,600 bytes; header 30 bytes (as stated in the question); MTU 1,400 bytes.

Required

Each fragment's header and data size.

Formula

Max data = 1,400 − 30 = 1,370, rounded down to a multiple of 8 = 1,368. Total data = 1,600 − 30 = 1,570.

Substitution

\(1{,}570 = 1{,}368 + 202\).

Calculation

Fragment 1: header 30 + data 1,368 = 1,398 bytes, offset 0, MF 1. Fragment 2: header 30 + data 202 = 232 bytes, offset 1,368/8 = 171, MF 0.

Final answer

Two fragments: 1,398 bytes (30 + 1,368) and 232 bytes (30 + 202). (A real IPv4 header length must be a multiple of 4 bytes, so 30 is unusual; follow the question's value.)

IPv6

In the 1990s it was clear that the 32-bit IPv4 address space would soon run out, so IPv6 was developed. Its designers also took the chance to streamline the header.

What changed

  • Expanded addressing: addresses grow from 32 to 128 bits, so the world won't run out of addresses. IPv6 also adds anycast addresses: a datagram is delivered to any one of a group of hosts.
  • A streamlined 40-byte header: a fixed-length header lets routers process datagrams faster. Options are encoded through the next header field.
  • Flow labelling: a 20-bit flow label identifies packets belonging to a “flow” for which the sender requests special handling, such as audio or video.
IPv6 fieldSizeNotes
Version4 bits6
Traffic class8 bitsLike IPv4's TOS: priority among datagrams
Flow label20 bitsIdentifies a flow
Payload length16 bitsBytes after the 40-byte header
Next header8 bitsWhich protocol gets the contents (same values as IPv4's protocol field)
Hop limit8 bitsDecremented at each router; like TTL
Source and destination addresses128 bits each

What was removed

  • Fragmentation and reassembly at routers: IPv6 allows fragmentation only at the source and destination. A router that gets a too-big datagram drops it and sends an ICMP “Packet Too Big” error back to the sender, which resends smaller datagrams. This saves router time.
  • Header checksum: transport and link layers already check for errors, and recomputing a checksum at every router (as IPv4 must, because TTL changes) was costly.
  • Options: no longer part of the standard header; they can be carried as “next headers”.
FeatureIPv4IPv6
Address length32 bits (≈ 4.3 × 10⁹ addresses)128 bits (≈ 3.4 × 10³⁸ addresses)
Header length20–60 bytes (variable)40 bytes (fixed)
Header checksumYes, recomputed at each routerNo
FragmentationBy routers and sourcesOnly at the source; routers send ICMP Packet Too Big
OptionsIn the headerIn extension (“next”) headers
Flow labelNoYes (20 bits)
TTL / hop limitTTLHop limit
Protocol / next headerProtocolNext header
Address notationDotted decimal: 192.168.1.10Hexadecimal groups: 2001:db8::8a2e:370:7334
ConfigurationManual or DHCPDHCPv6 or stateless autoconfiguration
BroadcastYesNo broadcast; multicast and anycast instead

Fields in common (a frequent exam part, 19CCE304 End 2024 Q5(B)): version, the source and destination addresses (with different sizes), and fields with the same role under new names: TOS ≈ traffic class, protocol ≈ next header, TTL ≈ hop limit, datagram length ≈ payload length (which excludes the header in IPv6).

From a paper 19CCE304 Jan 2023 Q6(A)

How big are the address spaces? MAC: \(2^{48}\) ≈ 2.8 × 10¹⁴. IPv4: \(2^{32}\) ≈ 4.3 × 10⁹. IPv6: \(2^{128}\) ≈ 3.4 × 10³⁸.

The transition from IPv4 to IPv6

There can be no “flag day” on which every machine switches, because billions of devices are involved. Two approaches:

  • Dual stack: IPv6 nodes also run IPv4, and use IPv4 to talk to IPv4-only nodes.
  • Tunnelling: when two IPv6 nodes are connected through intervening IPv4 routers, the IPv6 node on the sending side of the tunnel puts the entire IPv6 datagram into the data field of an IPv4 datagram (with protocol number 41), addressed to the IPv6 node at the receiving end. The IPv4 routers forward it like any other IPv4 datagram. The receiving node sees that the IPv4 datagram contains an IPv6 datagram, extracts it and routes it onward as IPv6.

Google reported in 2023 that about 40% of the clients accessing its services did so over IPv6.

Exam focus
  • Draw the IPv4 datagram format and explain the key fields (20CYS303 End 2023 Q12, which also asks about the IPv4–IPv6 transition).
  • Fragmentation numericals appeared in two papers: offsets in units of 8 bytes, MF flags, every fragment with its own header.
  • IPv4 vs IPv6 header comparison and common fields.
Common mistakes
  • Writing the offset in bytes instead of 8-byte units.
  • Using data per fragment that isn't a multiple of 8.
  • Forgetting to subtract the header from the MTU (and from the original size).
  • Saying routers reassemble fragments. Only the destination does.
Quick revision
  • IPv4 header 20 bytes: version, header length, TOS, length, identifier, flags, offset, TTL, protocol (6/17), header checksum, addresses, options.
  • Fragmentation: same identifier, MF = 1 except last, offset in 8-byte units; reassembly only at the destination.
  • IPv6: 128-bit addresses, 40-byte fixed header, flow label; no router fragmentation, no checksum, options in next headers.
  • Transition: dual stack and tunnelling (IPv6 inside IPv4).

II.17 · Network layer

IPv4 addressing, subnetting, CIDR, DHCP and NAT

What an IP address identifies, how subnets are formed, classful addressing and why it wasted addresses, subnet masks and CIDR (with VLSM worked step by step), how hosts get addresses automatically with DHCP, and how NAT lets a whole home share one public address.

Sources: Kurose §4.3.2–4.3.3 · Forouzan Ch 19 · Chapter_4_v8.2 slides 44–73 · Ip_addressing.ppt

What an IP address identifies

A host typically has a single link into the network; a router has several. The boundary between a host or router and a physical link is an interface. Because every host and router can send and receive IP datagrams, IP requires each interface to have its own IP address. An IP address is technically associated with an interface, not with the host or router containing it.

Each IPv4 address is 32 bits long (4 bytes), so there are \(2^{32}\) (about 4.3 billion) possible addresses. They are written in dotted-decimal notation: each byte is written in decimal and separated by a period. For example, 193.32.216.9 is, in binary, 11000001 00100000 11011000 00001001.

Level 1Converting between binary and dotted decimal
Given

(a) 10000001 00001011 00001011 11101111; (b) 172.16.4.25.

Required

(a) dotted decimal; (b) binary.

Formula

Each byte: place values 128 64 32 16 8 4 2 1.

Substitution

(a) 10000001 = 128 + 1; 00001011 = 8 + 2 + 1; 11101111 = 128 + 64 + 32 + 8 + 4 + 2 + 1.

Calculation

(a) 129.11.11.239. (b) 172 = 10101100, 16 = 00010000, 4 = 00000100, 25 = 00011001.

Final answer

(a) 129.11.11.239. (b) 10101100 00010000 00000100 00011001.

Subnets

In IP terms, a group of interfaces connected so that they can reach each other without passing through a router forms a subnet (also called an IP network). In Kurose's example, three hosts and one router interface on one Ethernet all have addresses 223.1.1.x: their leftmost 24 bits are the same.

IP assigns this subnet the address 223.1.1.0/24, where the /24 is the subnet mask: the leftmost 24 bits of the 32-bit quantity define the subnet address. Any other host attached to this subnet must have an address of the form 223.1.1.xxx.

How to find the subnets in a figure (Kurose's recipe)

Detach each interface from its host or router, creating islands of isolated networks, with interfaces terminating the end points of the isolated networks. Each of these isolated networks is a subnet. Remember that a point-to-point link between two routers is a subnet too.

Classful addressing (Forouzan Ch 19)

Before 1993, IPv4 used classful addressing: the address space was divided into five classes, A to E, identified by the first bits (or the first byte).

ClassFirst bitsFirst byte rangeDefault maskNetwork bits / host bitsBlocksAddresses per blockUse
A00–127255.0.0.0 (/8)8 / 2412816,777,216Very large organisations
B10128–191255.255.0.0 (/16)16 / 1616,38465,536Mid-size organisations
C110192–223255.255.255.0 (/24)24 / 82,097,152256Small organisations
D1110224–239——1268,435,456Multicast
E1111240–255——1268,435,456Reserved

In classes A, B and C, an address is divided into a netid and a hostid. The network address (all host bits 0) identifies the block; the limited broadcast/directed broadcast address of the block has all host bits 1; neither can be assigned to a host, so a block with \(h\) host bits has \(2^h - 2\) usable host addresses.

The problem with classes: they waste addresses badly. A class C block (254 hosts) is too small for most organisations; a class B block (65,534 hosts) is too big for most. An organisation with 2,000 hosts got a class B and left about 63,000 addresses unused. Most class A addresses were wasted. This is what made the address space run out.

Subnet masks and subnetting

A mask is a 32-bit number with \(n\) leftmost 1s and \(32 - n\) 0s. ANDing an address with the mask gives the network (subnet) address.

Key subnet formulas (a block with prefix length n)

\[ \text{addresses in the block} = 2^{32-n} \qquad \text{usable hosts} = 2^{32-n} - 2 \]

\[ \text{network address} = \text{address AND mask} \qquad \text{broadcast address} = \text{network address OR (NOT mask)} \]

Borrowing \(s\) host bits to make subnets gives \(2^s\) subnets, each with \(2^{32-n-s}\) addresses.

PrefixMaskAddressesUsable hosts
/24255.255.255.0256254
/25255.255.255.128128126
/26255.255.255.1926462
/27255.255.255.2243230
/28255.255.255.2401614
/29255.255.255.24886
/30255.255.255.25242
/23255.255.254.0512510
/22255.255.252.01,0241,022
/20255.255.240.04,0964,094
/16255.255.0.065,53665,534
Level 2First, last and broadcast address
Given

The address 205.16.37.39/28.

Required

The block's network (first) address, broadcast (last) address, the usable range and the number of addresses.

Formula

Network = address AND mask; block size = \(2^{32-28} = 16\).

Substitution

Last byte 39 = 0010 0111; mask's last byte /28 = 1111 0000.

Calculation

0010 0111 AND 1111 0000 = 0010 0000 = 32. Broadcast = 32 + 16 − 1 = 47.

Final answer

Network 205.16.37.32, broadcast 205.16.37.47, usable hosts .33 to .46 (14 hosts), 16 addresses in total.

CIDR: classless interdomain routing

The Internet's address-assignment strategy since 1993 is CIDR. It generalises subnet addressing: the 32-bit address is divided into two parts in dotted-decimal form a.b.c.d/x, where x is the number of bits in the first part (the prefix), and any \(x\) is allowed, not just 8, 16 or 24.

  • An organisation is typically assigned a block of contiguous addresses with a common prefix. Routers outside the organisation consider only the leading \(x\) prefix bits. This greatly reduces the size of forwarding tables: one entry of the form a.b.c.d/x suffices to reach every address in the organisation.
  • The remaining \(32 - x\) bits identify devices inside the organisation, which may be further subnetted.

Route aggregation (address summarisation)

Kurose's example: ISP Fly-By-Night has the block 200.23.16.0/20 and divides it into eight /23 blocks for eight organisations (200.23.16.0/23, 200.23.18.0/23, …, 200.23.30.0/23). To the rest of the Internet it advertises one route: “send me anything whose first 20 bits match 200.23.16.0/20”. Using a single prefix to advertise multiple networks is route aggregation.

What if Organisation 1 moves to another ISP (ISPs-R-Us, 199.31.0.0/16) but keeps its addresses 200.23.18.0/23? ISPs-R-Us advertises both 199.31.0.0/16 and the more specific 200.23.18.0/23. Routers use longest prefix matching, so traffic for Organisation 1 goes to ISPs-R-Us while the rest of 200.23.16.0/20 still goes to Fly-By-Night.

VLSM: subnets of different sizes

With variable-length subnet masks, each subnet gets a block just large enough for it. The method:

1. SortList the subnets from the largest requirement to the smallest.
2. Round upRound each requirement up to a power of 2 (add 2 for network and broadcast if the requirement is for hosts).
3. AllocateGive the largest block first, starting at the beginning of the address space; each block must start at a multiple of its own size.
4. WriteFor each subnet: prefix, mask, network address, first and last usable address, broadcast address.
Try it

The subnet and VLSM calculator in the Important numericals section shows the binary AND for any address/prefix and builds a complete VLSM allocation table, step by step.

Worked subnetting questions from your papers

From a paperClass-based vs classless allocation 23CSE302 End 2025 Q2

An ISP holds ten class C blocks, 193.10.1.0/24 to 193.10.10.0/24. A client needs 5 subnets with 120, 56, 28, 12 and 12 addresses.

Given

Requirements 120, 56, 28, 12, 12 addresses; available blocks 193.10.1.0/24 … 193.10.10.0/24.

Required

(a) the blocks assigned under classful and classless addressing; (b) the addresses saved, and the disadvantages of classless addressing.

Formula

Classful: one whole class C block (/24, 256 addresses) per subnet. Classless: block size = next power of 2 ≥ requirement; prefix = 32 − log₂(size).

Substitution

120 → 128 (/25); 56 → 64 (/26); 28 → 32 (/27); 12 → 16 (/28); 12 → 16 (/28). Total = 128 + 64 + 32 + 16 + 16 = 256, exactly one /24.

Calculation

(a) Classful: 193.10.1.0/24, 193.10.2.0/24, 193.10.3.0/24, 193.10.4.0/24, 193.10.5.0/24 (5 × 256 = 1,280 addresses). Classless (VLSM) inside 193.10.1.0/24: S1 = 193.10.1.0/25 (.0–.127); S2 = 193.10.1.128/26 (.128–.191); S3 = 193.10.1.192/27 (.192–.223); S4 = 193.10.1.224/28 (.224–.239); S5 = 193.10.1.240/28 (.240–.255). (b) Saved \(= 1{,}280 - 256 = 1{,}024\) addresses (four whole class C blocks remain free for other clients).

Final answer

Classful uses five /24 blocks; classless fits everything in 193.10.1.0/24 and saves 1,024 addresses. Disadvantages of classless allocation: the mask can no longer be inferred from the address, so every host and router must be configured with the prefix length (more chance of misconfiguration); address planning and record-keeping are more complex; routers must do longest-prefix matching rather than a simple class lookup; small blocks scattered across organisations make renumbering harder when a customer changes ISP (and break aggregation, adding more-specific routes).

From a paperThree sub-blocks of 148.56.0.0/24 19CSE301 End 2023 Q4(iv)
Given

Block 148.56.0.0/24; sub-blocks of 29, 16 and 12 addresses.

Required

(a) class; (b) default mask; (c) addresses for the largest subnet; (d) masks of the three blocks; (e) ranges of blocks 1 and 2.

Formula

Block size = next power of 2 ≥ requirement.

Substitution

29 → 32; 16 → 16; 12 → 16.

Calculation

(a) First byte 148 is in 128–191: class B. (b) 255.255.0.0. (c) 32 addresses. (d) Block 1: /27 = 255.255.255.224; Block 2: /28 = 255.255.255.240; Block 3: /28 = 255.255.255.240. (e) Block 1: 148.56.0.0 – 148.56.0.31; Block 2: 148.56.0.32 – 148.56.0.47 (Block 3: 148.56.0.48 – 148.56.0.63).

Final answer

Class B; 255.255.0.0; 32; /27, /28, /28; 148.56.0.0–.31 and 148.56.0.32–.47.

From a paperThree subnets of 45 hosts 19CSE301 End 2024 Q4(b)
Given

Network 200.175.14.0; 3 subnets needed; 45 usable hosts each.

Required

Class, default mask, new mask, first and last address of each subnet, hosts per subnet.

Formula

Host bits h with \(2^h - 2 \ge 45\); subnet bits s with \(2^s \ge 3\).

Substitution

\(h = 6\) gives 62 usable; then \(s = 8 - 6 = 2\) gives 4 subnets ≥ 3 ✓.

Calculation

Class C (first byte 200); default mask 255.255.255.0; new mask /26 = 255.255.255.192. Subnet 1: 200.175.14.0 (network) … .63 (broadcast), usable .1–.62. Subnet 2: .64 … .127, usable .65–.126. Subnet 3: .128 … .191, usable .129–.190. (Subnet 4, .192/26, is spare.)

Final answer

Class C; 255.255.255.0 → 255.255.255.192; 62 usable hosts per subnet; first/last usable addresses as listed (network and broadcast addresses also shown).

From a paperQuarters and eighths of a class B network 20CYS303 End 2023 Q15
Given

Address 150.1.2.5 with its class's default mask; divide into 5 subnets: the first three get one quarter of the addresses each, the last two get half of the remainder each.

Required

For each subnet: gateway IP, total addresses, usable hosts, broadcast address.

Formula

Class B → network 150.1.0.0/16 = 65,536 addresses. A quarter = 16,384 (/18); the remaining quarter split in half = 8,192 each (/19).

Substitution

Allocate in order from 150.1.0.0.

Calculation

S1: 150.1.0.0/18 (150.1.0.0 – 150.1.63.255). S2: 150.1.64.0/18 (– 150.1.127.255). S3: 150.1.128.0/18 (– 150.1.191.255). S4: 150.1.192.0/19 (– 150.1.223.255). S5: 150.1.224.0/19 (– 150.1.255.255).

Final answer

See the table (gateway taken as the first usable address, a common convention).

SubnetBlockGatewayTotal addressesUsable hostsBroadcast
1150.1.0.0/18150.1.0.116,38416,382150.1.63.255
