FDDI and IEEE 802.5 token ring used it. With a very large perimeter, the token takes a long time to travel around, so nodes wait longer and efficiency drops (19CCE304 Jan 2023 Q7(A)).

Comparing the three classes

Channel partitioningRandom accessTaking turns
CollisionsNoneYesNone
Rate when one node is activeOnly R/N (poor)Full R (good)Nearly R
Behaviour under heavy loadEfficient and fairCollisions waste capacityEfficient and fair
Central controlNoNoPolling: yes (master); token: no
OverheadsIdle slots or bandsCollisions, backoffPolling delay, token passing
ExamplesTDMA, FDMA, CDMA (cellular)ALOHA, CSMA/CD (Ethernet), CSMA/CA (Wi-Fi)Polling (Bluetooth), token ring, FDDI

DOCSIS cable access uses all three: FDM for downstream and upstream channels, TDM with mini-slots upstream, random access for requests in contention slots, and the head end (CMTS) assigns turns.

Interactive visualizationMultiple access simulatorAnimate slotted ALOHA, pure ALOHA and CSMA/CD on a shared channel: watch frames collide, jam signals, and nodes draw random backoffs. Change N and p and compare the measured throughput with the formulas.
Open simulator →
Exam focus
  • ALOHA efficiency formulas and their maxima (0.37, 0.18); the derivation idea (exactly one node transmits; vulnerable period 2 for pure ALOHA). Asked in 5 papers.
  • CSMA/CD from the adapter's viewpoint (the five steps), and binary exponential backoff calculations.
  • Three classes of MAC protocol with examples; pure vs slotted ALOHA (20CYS303 End 2023 Q4); CSMA/CD (20CYS303 End 2023 Q5); three random access protocols (20CYS303 End 2024 Q13).
Common mistakes
  • Using \((1-p)^{N}\) instead of \((1-p)^{N-1}\) in slotted ALOHA.
  • Forgetting the factor 2 in pure ALOHA's exponent.
  • Choosing K from {0, …, 2ⁿ} instead of {0, …, 2ⁿ − 1}.
  • Saying CSMA prevents all collisions. Propagation delay still allows them.
Quick revision
  • Ideal MAC: R alone, R/M shared, decentralised, simple.
  • Partitioning: TDMA, FDMA, CDMA (no collisions, R/N max).
  • Slotted ALOHA \(Np(1-p)^{N-1}\), max 1/e ≈ 0.37; pure ALOHA \(Np(1-p)^{2(N-1)}\), max 1/(2e) ≈ 0.18.
  • CSMA: listen before talk; collisions from propagation delay. CSMA/CD: abort + jam + backoff K ∈ {0 … 2ⁿ − 1} × 512 bit times; \(L_{min} = 2 d_{prop} R\).
  • CSMA/CA: no detection; DIFS, random backoff, ACK, RTS/CTS.
  • Taking turns: polling (master, polling delay) and token passing (token loss, large ring delay).

III.5 · Link layer

Link-layer addressing and ARP

MAC addresses and why hosts need them as well as IP addresses, how ARP finds the MAC address for an IP address on the same subnet, and exactly which addresses a frame carries when a datagram is sent to another subnet.

Sources: Kurose §6.4.1 · Chapter_6_v8.2 slides 41–56

MAC addresses

Hosts and routers have link-layer addresses as well as IP addresses. More precisely, it is not hosts and routers but their adapters (network interfaces) that have link-layer addresses. A host or router with several interfaces has several link-layer addresses, just as it has several IP addresses. (Link-layer switches do not have link-layer addresses on the interfaces that connect to hosts and routers: a switch carries datagrams transparently.)

  • A link-layer address is called a LAN address, physical address or MAC address.
  • It is 6 bytes (48 bits) long, giving \(2^{48}\) possible addresses, written in hexadecimal, one pair of digits per byte: 1A-2F-BB-76-09-AD.
  • MAC addresses were designed to be permanent (burned into the adapter's ROM), although software can now change them.
  • No two adapters have the same address: the IEEE manages the address space. A company buys a block of \(2^{24}\) addresses; the IEEE fixes the first 24 bits (the organisationally unique identifier) and the company makes the last 24 bits unique.
  • A MAC address has a flat structure (no hierarchy) and doesn't change wherever the adapter goes. An IP address has a hierarchical structure (network part and host part) and changes when the host moves.
Aadhaar number and postal address

A MAC address is like your Aadhaar (or social security) number: flat, and it stays with you wherever you live. An IP address is like your postal address: hierarchical, and it changes when you move. The post office routes by postal address; once the letter is at the right building, the name on it picks you out.

When an adapter sends a frame, it puts the destination adapter's MAC address in the frame. On a broadcast LAN, every adapter may receive the frame, but each checks whether the destination address matches its own; if not, it discards the frame (without interrupting the host). To reach all adapters on the LAN, the sender uses the MAC broadcast address FF-FF-FF-FF-FF-FF (48 ones).

Why have both MAC and IP addresses?

  1. LANs are designed for arbitrary network-layer protocols, not just IP. If adapters used IP addresses instead of neutral MAC addresses, they couldn't easily support other network-layer protocols.
  2. If adapters used network-layer addresses stored in RAM and reconfigured each time the adapter moved, the link layer and network layer would no longer be independent.
  3. Without MAC addresses, every adapter would have to pass every frame up to the network layer to check the destination, interrupting the host for every frame on the LAN.

ARP: the Address Resolution Protocol

A host knows the IP address of the destination (from DNS, for instance) but needs its MAC address to build a frame. ARP translates between them: given an IP address on the same subnet, it returns the corresponding MAC address. (DNS resolves host names anywhere on the Internet; ARP resolves IP addresses only for hosts and router interfaces on the same subnet.)

Each host and router has an ARP table in memory:

IP addressMAC addressTTL
222.222.222.22188-B2-2F-54-1A-0F13:45:00
222.222.222.2235C-66-AB-90-75-B113:52:00

The TTL says when each entry will be deleted; a typical lifetime is 20 minutes. The table doesn't necessarily contain an entry for every host on the subnet.

How ARP works

Host A (222.222.222.220) wants to send a datagram to host B (222.222.222.222) on the same subnet, but B is not in A's ARP table.

  1. A builds an ARP packet with its own IP and MAC addresses and the IP address to be resolved, and asks its adapter to send it in a frame with the broadcast address FF-FF-FF-FF-FF-FF.
  2. Every adapter on the subnet receives the frame and passes the ARP packet up to its ARP module, which checks whether its own IP address matches the one in the query.
  3. The one with a match (B) sends back an ARP response containing the desired mapping, in a standard (unicast) frame to A.
  4. A updates its ARP table and can now send the datagram in a frame addressed to B's MAC address.

Notes: the query is broadcast, the response is unicast; ARP is plug-and-play: the table builds itself and no administrator is needed. ARP sits on the boundary between the link and network layers (its packets are carried in link-layer frames, but it contains both kinds of address).

Interactive visualizationARP and frames across subnetsWatch an ARP broadcast and reply, then follow a datagram from one subnet to another through a router, with the source and destination MAC and IP addresses shown at every hop.
Open simulator →

Sending a datagram to another subnet

Host A (111.111.111.111, MAC 74-29-9C-E8-FF-55) on subnet 1 wants to send a datagram to host B (222.222.222.222, MAC 49-BD-D2-C7-56-2A) on subnet 2. Router R connects the subnets: its interface on subnet 1 is 111.111.111.110 (E6-E9-00-17-BB-4B), and on subnet 2 is 222.222.222.220 (1A-23-F9-CD-06-9B).

  1. A uses its forwarding rule: B is not on its subnet, so the datagram must go to its first-hop (default gateway) router, 111.111.111.110. A uses ARP to get the router interface's MAC address, E6-E9-00-17-BB-4B.
  2. A creates a frame: destination MAC = the router's interface (E6-E9-…), source MAC = A's (74-29-…). The datagram inside keeps source IP = A (111.111.111.111), destination IP = B (222.222.222.222).
  3. The router's adapter on subnet 1 accepts the frame (it is addressed to it) and passes the datagram up. The router consults its forwarding table and finds that the datagram goes out of interface 222.222.222.220.
  4. The router uses ARP on subnet 2 to find B's MAC address, then builds a new frame: destination MAC = B (49-BD-…), source MAC = the router's subnet-2 interface (1A-23-…), with the same IP datagram inside.
  5. B's adapter accepts the frame and passes the datagram up.
HopSource MACDestination MACSource IPDestination IP
A → RA (74-29-9C-E8-FF-55)R, subnet 1 (E6-E9-00-17-BB-4B)111.111.111.111222.222.222.222
R → BR, subnet 2 (1A-23-F9-CD-06-9B)B (49-BD-D2-C7-56-2A)111.111.111.111222.222.222.222

The IP addresses stay the same end to end; the MAC addresses change at every hop. (The exception is NAT, which rewrites IP addresses.)

From a paperSwitch S1 and router R1 19CCE304 End 2023 Q7 20CYS303 End 2023 Q10 19CCE304 End 2024 Q6(B)

These papers use Kurose's three-subnet figure: hosts on subnets 1, 2 and 3; the router between subnets 1 and 2 is replaced by a switch S1, and the router between subnets 2 and 3 is R1. (So subnets 1 and 2 become one IP subnet; R1 is the only router.)

Given

E and F are on subnet 3; B is on the other side of R1; E's ARP cache doesn't contain B's MAC.

Required

(A) E → F: does E ask R1 for help? Which addresses are in the frame? (B) E → B: does E ARP for B's MAC? Which addresses are in the frame delivered to R1?

Formula

Same subnet → deliver directly (ARP for the destination). Different subnet → deliver to the gateway (ARP for the router).

Substitution

E and F share subnet 3; B does not.

Calculation

(A) No: E and F are on the same subnet, so E finds F's MAC with ARP (if needed) and sends directly. (B) No ARP for B: B is not on E's subnet, so E needs only R1's MAC on subnet 3 (from its ARP cache, or by ARPing for R1's interface address).

Final answer

(A) Frame: source IP = E's IP, destination IP = F's IP, source MAC = E's MAC, destination MAC = F's MAC. (B) E does not ARP for B. Frame delivered to R1: source IP = E's IP, destination IP = B's IP, source MAC = E's MAC, destination MAC = R1's interface on subnet 3. (When R1 forwards it on, the new frame has source MAC = R1's other interface and destination MAC = B's MAC, found through S1; the switch itself never appears in any address.)

From a paperarp -a and the lost first ping 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q9
Given

PC-A (192.168.1.1/24) and PC-B (192.168.1.2/24) are connected by a crossover cable and have never communicated. On PC-A: arp -a, then ping 192.168.1.2; replies come back but one packet is lost.

Required

The output of arp -a, and why the first packet is lost.

Formula

ARP tables fill only when traffic needs them.

Substitution

No communication has happened yet.

Calculation

arp -a shows no entry for 192.168.1.2 (only static or multicast entries, or “No ARP entries found”). When ping starts, A must first resolve 192.168.1.2 with an ARP broadcast. The first ICMP echo request waits in a queue (or is dropped) while ARP completes, and its timer expires before the reply; once B's MAC is in the cache, the remaining echo requests go straight through.

Final answer

Initially the ARP table has no entry for PC-B. The first ping is lost because of ARP resolution delay: the first echo request can't be framed until PC-B's MAC address is learned, and it times out. Afterwards arp -a shows 192.168.1.2 with PC-B's MAC address (dynamic).

Exam focus
  • Source and destination MAC and IP addresses at each hop appeared in 6 papers. Make the hop table every time.
  • ARP: broadcast query, unicast response, ARP table with TTL, plug-and-play.
  • Why both MAC and IP addresses exist; flat vs hierarchical; 48 bits in hex; FF-FF-FF-FF-FF-FF.
  • 20CYS303 End 2024 Q2 and 19CCE304 Jan 2023 Q7(B): explain ARP with a flow chart and a case study.
Common mistakes
  • Putting the final destination's MAC address in the first-hop frame. It's the router's MAC.
  • Changing the IP addresses at the router.
  • Saying ARP works across subnets. It resolves addresses only on the local subnet.
  • Saying the ARP reply is broadcast. Only the query is.
Quick revision
  • MAC: 48 bits, hex, flat, permanent, IEEE-managed; broadcast FF-FF-FF-FF-FF-FF.
  • ARP: IP → MAC on the same subnet; ARP table (IP, MAC, TTL ≈ 20 min); query broadcast, reply unicast.
  • Off-subnet: frame to the gateway router's MAC; IP addresses unchanged end to end; MAC addresses change each hop.

III.6 · Link layer

Ethernet

How Ethernet evolved from a shared bus to switched stars, the fields of the Ethernet frame, its connectionless unreliable service, and the family of Ethernet standards (including the line coding each uses).

Sources: Kurose §6.4.2 · Chapter_6_v8.2 slides 57–62

Why Ethernet won

Ethernet has pretty much taken over the wired LAN market. It was the first widely deployed high-speed LAN; token ring, FDDI and ATM were more complex and expensive; and Ethernet kept producing versions with equal or higher rates (10 Mbps → 100 Mbps → 1 Gbps → 10, 40, 100 Gbps) while hardware became very cheap.

The evolution of Ethernet's topology

PeriodTopologyBehaviour
Original (Metcalfe and Boggs, mid-1970s)Bus: coaxial cableA broadcast LAN: all frames reach all adapters; collisions handled by CSMA/CD
Late 1990sHub-based star: hosts and routers connect to a hub with twisted-pair copperA hub is a physical-layer device acting on individual bits: when a bit arrives on one interface, the hub regenerates it and sends it out on all other interfaces. Still a broadcast LAN with collisions
Early 2000s onwardSwitch-based star: the hub is replaced by a switchA switch is collision-less and a bona fide store-and-forward packet switch, operating only up to layer 2

The Ethernet frame

FieldSizePurpose
Preamble8 bytesThe first 7 bytes are 10101010; the last is 10101011. They wake up the receiving adapters and synchronise their clocks to the sender's (the sender's rate drifts slightly from nominal). The two final 1s tell the receiver that the important content is coming
Destination address6 bytesThe destination adapter's MAC address. An adapter passes a frame up only if this matches its own address or is the broadcast address; otherwise it discards it
Source address6 bytesThe sending adapter's MAC address
Type2 bytesWhich network-layer protocol the data belongs to, so the receiver can demultiplex: 0x0800 for IPv4, 0x0806 for ARP, 0x86DD for IPv6 (like the protocol field in IP and port numbers in TCP/UDP)
Data46–1,500 bytesThe IP datagram. The maximum (the MTU) is 1,500 bytes; larger datagrams must be fragmented. If the datagram is smaller than 46 bytes, it is padded to 46 bytes, and the network layer uses the IP length field to remove the padding
CRC4 bytesCyclic redundancy check (CRC-32) so the receiver can detect bit errors in the frame

Ethernet's service: connectionless and unreliable

  • Connectionless: no handshaking with the receiving adapter before sending a frame (like IP datagrams and UDP).
  • Unreliable: when an adapter receives a frame, it runs the CRC check but sends no acknowledgement if the frame passes and no negative acknowledgement if it fails; a failed frame is simply discarded. Gaps in the stream of datagrams are left for higher layers: if the application uses TCP, the TCP receiver won't acknowledge the missing data and the sender will retransmit it; with UDP the gap remains.

This lack of reliable transport keeps Ethernet simple and cheap.

Ethernet technologies

There are many standards (IEEE 802.3), named like 10BASE-T, 10BASE-2, 100BASE-T, 1000BASE-LX, 10GBASE-T and 40GBASE-T:

  • the first part is the speed: 10 Mbps, 100 Mbps, 1,000 Mbps (gigabit), 10 Gbps, 40 Gbps;
  • BASE means baseband Ethernet: the physical medium carries only Ethernet traffic;
  • the final part is the medium: T = twisted-pair copper; 2 = thin coax (about 200 m); F, FX, LX, SX = optical fibre.

All share the same frame format; they differ in rate and physical layer. Early 10BASE-2 and 10BASE-5 used coax buses limited to 500 m segments, extended with repeaters (physical-layer devices that regenerate the signal). Today, 100 Mbps runs over twisted pair to about 100 m and fibre to several km; gigabit Ethernet keeps the standard frame format, allows both point-to-point links through switches and shared broadcast channels through hubs (using CSMA/CD), and allows full-duplex operation at 1 Gbps in both directions on point-to-point links.

StandardRateLine coding (Forouzan 4.1)
10BASE-T (classic Ethernet)10 MbpsManchester
100BASE-TX (Fast Ethernet, copper)100 Mbps4B/5B block coding + MLT-3
100BASE-FX (Fast Ethernet, fibre)100 Mbps4B/5B + NRZ-I
1000BASE-T (Gigabit, copper)1 Gbps4D-PAM5 over four pairs
10GBASE-T10 GbpsPAM-16 (beyond the syllabus)
From a paper 23CSE302 End 2025 Q1

“Suggest a suitable encoding technique for the Fast Ethernet standard.” Fast Ethernet over copper (100BASE-TX) uses 4B/5B block coding with MLT-3 line coding: 4B/5B guarantees enough transitions for synchronisation, and MLT-3's three levels reduce the bandwidth needed so 100 Mbps fits on Category 5 twisted pair. (Fibre, 100BASE-FX, uses 4B/5B with NRZ-I.) Manchester would double the baud rate to 200 Mbaud, which is too much for the cable. Line coding itself is taught in Topic III.10.

CSMA/CD in a switched world

In a switch-based star with full-duplex links, a switch never sends two frames onto the same interface at once, and each link carries frames in both directions independently: there are no collisions, so no MAC protocol is needed. CSMA/CD remains part of the standard for shared (hub or bus) segments.

Exam focus
  • Draw and explain the Ethernet frame fields and their sizes (preamble, addresses, type, data 46–1,500, CRC).
  • Ethernet is connectionless and unreliable; who recovers lost frames.
  • Decode a standard's name (100BASE-TX); line coding for Fast Ethernet.
Quick revision
  • Bus → hub star (layer 1, broadcast) → switch star (layer 2, store-and-forward, no collisions).
  • Frame: preamble 8 (7 × 10101010 + 10101011), dest 6, source 6, type 2 (0x0800 IPv4, 0x0806 ARP), data 46–1,500 (padding), CRC 4.
  • Connectionless, unreliable: bad frames dropped, no ACK/NAK.
  • Names: speed + BASE (baseband) + medium. 10BASE-T Manchester; 100BASE-TX 4B/5B + MLT-3.

III.7 · Link layer

Link-layer switches and VLANs

How a switch forwards and filters frames, how its table builds itself (self-learning), why switches beat hubs, how switches compare with routers, and how VLANs split one switched network into isolated broadcast domains with 802.1Q tagging.

Sources: Kurose §6.4.3–6.4.4 · Chapter_6_v8.2 slides 63–82

What a switch does

A switch receives incoming link-layer frames and forwards them onto outgoing links. The switch itself is transparent to hosts and routers: a host addresses a frame to another host (not to the switch) and sends it into the LAN, unaware that a switch will receive and forward it. Because frames can arrive faster than an output interface can send them, each switch output interface has buffers.

Forwarding and filtering

  • Filtering decides whether a frame should be forwarded to some interface or dropped.
  • Forwarding decides the interface(s) to which a frame should be directed, and moves it there.

Both use a switch table with entries of the form (MAC address, interface, time):

MAC addressInterfaceTime
62-FE-F7-11-89-A319:32
7C-BA-B2-B4-91-1039:36

When a frame with destination address DD-DD-DD-DD-DD-DD arrives on interface x, the switch looks up DD-DD-…:

  1. No entry for DD: the switch forwards copies to all interfaces except x (it broadcasts, or “floods”, the frame).
  2. An entry associates DD with interface x (the same one the frame came from): the frame came from a LAN segment that already contains the destination, so the switch filters it (discards it).
  3. An entry associates DD with interface y ≠ x: the switch forwards the frame to interface y (puts it in y's output buffer).

Self-learning

The table is built automatically, dynamically and autonomously, with no administrator and no configuration protocol:

  1. The switch table is initially empty.
  2. For each incoming frame received on an interface, the switch stores in its table (a) the MAC address in the frame's source address field, (b) the interface from which the frame arrived, and (c) the current time. If every host eventually sends a frame, every host will be recorded.
  3. The switch deletes an address if no frame is received with that address as source after some period (the aging time, for example 60 minutes), so the table tracks hosts that move or are removed.

Switches are therefore plug-and-play devices.

From a paperA learning switch with six hosts 19CSE301 Jan 2023 Q10

Hosts A to F are star-connected to one Ethernet switch (host X on interface X). The switch table starts empty.

Given

Events in order: (i) B sends to E; (ii) E replies to B; (iii) A sends to B; (iv) B replies to A.

Required

The switch table after each event and the links each frame is forwarded on.

Formula

Learn the source; look up the destination: unknown → flood (all but incoming); known on another port → forward there.

Substitution

Table after each event below.

Calculation

(i) Learn B → port B. E unknown → flood to A, C, D, E, F. (ii) Learn E → port E. B known → forward only to B. (iii) Learn A → port A. B known → forward only to B. (iv) B already in the table (time refreshed). A known → forward only to A.

Final answer

Table: after (i) {B}; after (ii) {B, E}; after (iii) {A, B, E}; after (iv) {A, B, E}. Only the first frame is flooded, because only then was the destination unknown.

After eventMAC addressInterfaceFrame forwarded on
(i) B → EBBA, C, D, E, F (flooded)
(ii) E → BB; EB; EB
(iii) A → BA; B; EA; B; EB
(iv) B → AA; B; EA; B; EA
Interactive visualizationSelf-learning switchSend frames between hosts on one switch or several interconnected switches, and watch each table learn, flood, filter and forward.
Open simulator →

Self-learning across several switches

Switches can be interconnected (for example, a department switch per building connected to a backbone switch). Self-learning works unchanged: each switch learns which of its own interfaces leads to each source address. When C sends to I through switches S1 and S4, S1 learns C on its host interface and floods; S4 learns C on its interface towards S1, and so on. 19CSE301 End 2024 Q6(b) gave a network of self-learning bridges (bridges behave the same way): for each transmission, apply the learn-source / lookup-destination rule at each bridge the frame reaches.

Properties of link-layer switching

  • Elimination of collisions: in a LAN built from switches (and no hubs), no bandwidth is wasted on collisions. Switches buffer frames and never send more than one frame on a segment at any time. The maximum aggregate throughput is the sum of all the interface rates.
  • Heterogeneous links: a switch isolates links from one another, so different links in the LAN can run at different speeds and over different media.
  • Management: a switch can detect an adapter that malfunctions and sends continuous frames (“jabbering”) and disconnect it internally; it can gather statistics on bandwidth use, collision rates and traffic types; a cable cut disconnects only one host.
  • Security note: switch poisoning (MAC flooding) sends huge numbers of frames with bogus source addresses to fill the switch table, so that legitimate frames are flooded and a sniffer can capture them.

Hubs, switches and routers

FeatureHubSwitchRouter
Layer1 (physical)2 (link)3 (network)
Works onBitsFrames, MAC addressesDatagrams, IP addresses
ForwardingRepeats every bit out of every other portForwards to the one port where the destination is (floods if unknown)Forwards by longest prefix match on the destination IP
Collision domainOne shared collision domainEach port is its own collision domain (none with full duplex)Each interface separate
Broadcast domainOneOne (unless VLANs are used)Separates broadcast domains
TableNoneSelf-learning switch tableForwarding table from routing protocols or a controller
ConfigurationNonePlug-and-playNeeds IP addresses and routing configured
Store-and-forwardNoYesYes
Handles loops/topologyNoNeeds a spanning tree (no loops allowed)Any topology; best paths via routing

Switches versus routers in more detail

SwitchesRouters
ProsPlug-and-play; relatively high filtering and forwarding rates (process only up to layer 2)Hierarchical addressing, so packets don't cycle even with redundant paths; any topology with best paths; firewall protection against layer-2 broadcast storms
ConsThe active topology is restricted to a spanning tree; a large switched network needs large ARP tables and generates much ARP traffic; vulnerable to broadcast storms (one malfunctioning host can collapse the network)Not plug-and-play (IP addresses must be configured); often slower per packet because they process up to layer 3
Best forSmall networks of a few hundred hostsLarger networks of thousands of hosts (usually with switches inside each subnet)

Virtual LANs (VLANs)

Modern institutional LANs are often configured hierarchically, with each workgroup (department) on its own switched LAN connected to the others through a hierarchy of switches. Three drawbacks:

  1. Lack of traffic isolation: broadcast traffic (ARP, DHCP, frames to unknown destinations) still crosses the whole institutional network, hurting performance; for security and privacy, it is also desirable to limit traffic to a group.
  2. Inefficient use of switches: with 10 groups, you would need 10 first-level switches even if each group had only a few people.
  3. Managing users: when someone moves between groups, the physical cabling must change to connect them to a different switch.

A switch that supports VLANs lets multiple virtual LANs be defined over a single physical LAN infrastructure. Hosts in a VLAN communicate as if they (and only they) were connected to the switch.

  • In a port-based VLAN, the network manager divides the switch's ports into groups. Each group is a VLAN, and the ports in each VLAN form a broadcast domain: broadcast traffic from one port reaches only the other ports in the same group.
  • Moving a person to another group just means reconfiguring which VLAN their port belongs to, in software.
  • Traffic between VLANs must go through a router (often built into the switch as a layer-3 switch), exactly as if the VLANs were separate switches.

VLAN trunking and 802.1Q

When VLANs span several switches (for example, some EE staff are in another building), connecting each VLAN with its own cable between switches doesn't scale. Instead, a special port on each switch is configured as a trunk port, and the two switches are joined by a trunk link that belongs to all VLANs.

How does a switch know which VLAN a frame arriving on a trunk port belongs to? The IEEE defined an extended Ethernet frame, 802.1Q: a 4-byte VLAN tag is added into the header between the source address and type fields, by the sending side of the trunk, and removed by the receiving side.

  • Tag Protocol Identifier (TPID): 2 bytes, fixed hexadecimal value 0x8100, marking the frame as tagged.
  • Tag Control Information (TCI): 2 bytes: a 12-bit VLAN identifier (up to 4,094 VLANs), a 3-bit priority field (like IP's TOS) and a 1-bit CFI flag.
  • Because the frame changed, the CRC is recomputed.

VLANs can also be defined by MAC address (the manager lists which MAC addresses belong to each VLAN, and the switch looks up the VLAN when a device attaches) or by network-layer protocol (IPv4, IPv6, AppleTalk).

From a paperSegregating bank departments without a router 19CSE301 End 2023 Q5(a)
Given

All the bank's departments share one LAN with no routers; the manager wants to stop communication between Retail Banking and Trading.

Required

A method without a router, and three advantages.

Formula

Port-based VLANs on the existing switches.

Substitution

Retail ports → VLAN 10; Trading ports → VLAN 20 (and other departments in their own VLANs); trunk ports with 802.1Q tags between switches.

Calculation

Frames, including broadcasts, from a VLAN-10 port are delivered only to other VLAN-10 ports. With no router between VLANs, the two departments cannot exchange traffic at all; any permitted traffic could later be allowed through a firewall or layer-3 switch with access control.

Final answer

Configure VLANs. Advantages: (1) traffic isolation and security: Retail and Trading cannot see each other's frames, and broadcast domains are smaller; (2) flexibility: moving an employee to another department is a software change to a port's VLAN, no recabling; (3) efficient use of hardware: several logical LANs share the same switches (no separate switch per department), and smaller broadcast domains improve performance.

Exam focus
  • Switch table exercises appeared in 3 papers: learn the source, flood unknown destinations, forward to known ones, filter same-port ones.
  • Switch vs router (pros and cons), and hub vs switch vs router.
  • VLAN motivation, port-based VLANs, trunking and the 802.1Q tag (TPID 0x8100, 12-bit VLAN ID, 3-bit priority) (20CYS303 End 2023 Q6, 19CCE304 End 2023 Q8).
Common mistakes
  • Learning the destination address. A switch learns only source addresses.
  • Forwarding a frame back out of the port it came in on.
  • Saying VLANs need no router to communicate. Inter-VLAN traffic must be routed.
Quick revision
  • Filtering and forwarding with a switch table (MAC, interface, time); unknown → flood; same port → filter; other port → forward.
  • Self-learning from source addresses; aging; plug-and-play.
  • Switch benefits: no collisions, heterogeneous links, management. Weakness: spanning-tree topology, broadcast storms, ARP load.
  • VLAN: port-based broadcast domains on shared switches; trunking with the 4-byte 802.1Q tag; routing between VLANs.

III.8 · Link layer

Link virtualisation: a network as a link layer (MPLS)

How Multiprotocol Label Switching forwards IP datagrams by short labels, where its header sits, and why ISPs use it for traffic engineering, fast reroute and VPNs. Listed on your official syllabus image.

Sources: Kurose §6.5 · Chapter_6_v8.2 slides 83–89

Source note

The official syllabus image includes “Link Virtualization: A Network as a Link Layer, Multiprotocol Label Switching (MPLS)”. This is the prescribed Kurose Section 6.5.

A network that IP sees as a single link

So far a “link” has been a physical wire or radio channel. But from IP's point of view, an entire network can serve as one link: the dial-up telephone network connecting two modems, or an ISP's MPLS network connecting two IP routers. IP simply sees “a link to the next IP router”, just as it does for an Ethernet. Such a network is a virtualised link.

MPLS: label switching

Multiprotocol Label Switching (MPLS) grew out of industry efforts in the late 1990s to improve the forwarding speed of IP routers by borrowing a key idea from virtual-circuit networks: a fixed-length label. The aim is not to abandon IP's destination-based datagram forwarding, but to augment it, by selectively labelling datagrams and letting routers forward them by label (where possible) instead of by destination IP address.

The MPLS header

An MPLS-capable router adds a small MPLS header between the layer-2 (e.g. PPP or Ethernet) header and the layer-3 (IP) header:

FieldBitsPurpose
Label20The label used for forwarding
Exp3Reserved for experimental use (now used for class of service)
S1Marks the end of a stack of MPLS headers (labels can be stacked)
TTL8Time to live

An MPLS-enhanced frame can be sent only between routers that are both MPLS-capable; such a router is a label-switched router (LSR). An LSR forwards a frame by looking up its MPLS label in its forwarding table and immediately passing the frame to the appropriate output interface, without extracting the destination IP address and doing a longest-prefix match.

R3's MPLS table
In labelOut labelDestinationOut interface
106A1
129D0

How does an LSR know labels and routes? MPLS routers advertise reachability with modified link-state routing (extending OSPF), and labels are distributed with a signalling protocol (RSVP-TE) along chosen paths.

Why use MPLS?

The real benefit is not speed (IP longest-prefix lookups are fast today) but traffic management:

  • Traffic engineering: MPLS can forward packets along routes that standard IP routing cannot. IP routing sends all packets to a destination along the single least-cost path; with MPLS, an operator can send traffic from different sources to the same destination along different paths (for example, R4's traffic to A via one route and R6's via another), balancing load.
  • Fast reroute: precomputed backup paths let an MPLS network switch traffic quickly when a link fails.
  • Virtual private networks (VPNs): an ISP can use MPLS to connect a customer's sites, isolating the customer's resources and addressing from other users who cross the same network.
IP destination-based routingMPLS
Path determined by the destination address onlyPath can depend on the source, flow or class
Longest-prefix match at every routerExact match on a short label; labels swapped at each hop
One least-cost path per destinationMultiple engineered paths, backup paths
ConnectionlessLabel-switched paths set up in advance (VC-like)
Quick revision
  • A whole network (telephone network, MPLS network) can be a virtual link for IP.
  • MPLS header (32 bits: label 20, Exp 3, S 1, TTL 8) between the layer-2 and IP headers; LSRs forward by label, swapping it at each hop.
  • Uses: traffic engineering (source-dependent paths), fast reroute, VPNs. Borrowed from virtual circuits.

III.9 · Physical layer

Analog and digital signals

What a signal is, the three properties of a sine wave, the time and frequency domains, composite signals and bandwidth, and digital signals: levels, bit rate, bit length, and baseband versus broadband transmission.

Sources: Forouzan §3.1–3.3 (3.3 prescribed) · PhysicalLayer ppt

Data and signals

To be transmitted, data must be transformed into electromagnetic signals. Both data and signals can be analog or digital.

  • Analog data takes continuous values (a human voice: as you speak, an analog wave is created in the air). Digital data takes discrete values (data stored in memory as 0s and 1s).
  • An analog signal has infinitely many levels of intensity over a period of time: as it moves from value A to value B, it passes through an infinite number of values. A digital signal can have only a limited number of defined values (often just two, 0 and 1).
Analog signalDigital signal
ValuesInfinitely many, continuousA limited set of discrete levels
ChangeSmoothJumps abruptly between levels
ExampleVoice over an old telephone line, AM/FM radioData on an Ethernet cable, a USB link
Effect of noiseNoise becomes part of the signal and can't be removedCan be regenerated exactly as long as levels are distinguishable
Typical channelBand-pass (a range of frequencies)Low-pass (from 0 up to some frequency)

Signals can also be periodic (a pattern repeats every period) or nonperiodic (no repeating pattern). In data communications, we commonly use periodic analog signals and nonperiodic digital signals.

Periodic analog signals: the sine wave

The sine wave is the most fundamental periodic analog signal. It is described by three characteristics:

CharacteristicMeaningUnit
Peak amplitudeThe absolute value of its highest intensity (proportional to the energy it carries); for electric signals, in voltsV
Frequency \(f\)The number of periods in 1 secondHertz (Hz)
Period \(T\)The time to complete one cycleSeconds
PhaseThe position of the waveform relative to time 0Degrees or radians (360° = 2π rad)
Frequency, period and wavelength

\[ f = \frac{1}{T} \qquad T = \frac{1}{f} \qquad \lambda = \frac{c}{f} = c \times T \]

\(\lambda\) is the wavelength: the distance a simple signal travels in one period, where \(c\) is the propagation speed (\(3 \times 10^8\) m/s in a vacuum).

  • Frequency is the rate of change with respect to time: a change in a short span means high frequency; a change over a long span means low frequency.
  • If a signal doesn't change at all, its frequency is zero. If it changes instantaneously, its frequency is infinite.

(Only the positive half is shown on this axis; a full sine wave swings between +A and −A.)

Time domain and frequency domain

The time-domain plot shows amplitude changes over time. A frequency-domain plot shows the relationship between amplitude and frequency: a single sine wave appears as one spike at its frequency, with height equal to its peak amplitude. The frequency domain is more compact, especially for signals made of many sine waves.

Composite signals and bandwidth

A single-frequency sine wave is not useful for data communications; we need composite signals, made of many simple sine waves. Fourier analysis shows that any composite signal is a combination of simple sine waves with different frequencies, amplitudes and phases. A periodic composite signal decomposes into discrete frequencies; a nonperiodic one into a continuous range of frequencies.

Bandwidth of a composite signal

\[ B = f_{highest} - f_{lowest} \]

Level 1Sine wave and bandwidth basics (Forouzan examples)
Given

(a) Indian mains power has frequency 50 Hz. (b) A period of 100 ms. (c) A periodic signal is made of five sine waves of 100, 300, 500, 700 and 900 Hz. (d) A signal has bandwidth 20 Hz and highest frequency 60 Hz.

Required

(a) the period; (b) the frequency in kHz; (c) the bandwidth; (d) the lowest frequency.

Formula

\(T = 1/f\); \(f = 1/T\); \(B = f_h - f_l\).

Substitution

(a) \(1/50\). (b) \(1/0.1\). (c) \(900 - 100\). (d) \(20 = 60 - f_l\).

Calculation

(a) 0.02 s = 20 ms. (b) 10 Hz = 0.01 kHz. (c) 800 Hz. (d) \(f_l = 40\) Hz.

Final answer

20 ms; 0.01 kHz; 800 Hz; 40 Hz.

Digital signals (Forouzan 3.3)

Information can also be represented by a digital signal: a 1 can be encoded as a positive voltage and a 0 as zero voltage. A digital signal can have more than two levels, and then each level can send more than 1 bit.

Bits per level

\[ \text{bits per level} = \log_2 L \]

where \(L\) is the number of levels. 2 levels → 1 bit; 4 levels → 2 bits; 8 levels → 3 bits.

Bit rate and bit length

Most digital signals are nonperiodic, so period and frequency are not appropriate. Instead:

  • Bit rate: the number of bits sent in 1 second, in bits per second (bps).
  • Bit length: the distance one bit occupies on the transmission medium: \( \text{bit length} = \text{propagation speed} \times \text{bit duration} \).
Level 2Bit rates and bit lengths (Forouzan examples)
Given

(a) A digital signal with 8 levels. (b) Download 100 pages of text per minute; each page has 24 lines of 80 characters, 8 bits per character. (c) Digitised voice: 4 kHz analog voice sampled at twice the highest frequency, 8 bits per sample. (d) HDTV: 1920 × 1080 pixels, 30 frames/s, 24 bits per pixel. (e) A 1 Mbps signal on copper with \(s = 2 \times 10^8\) m/s.

Required

(a) bits per level; (b)–(d) required bit rates; (e) bit length.

Formula

\(\log_2 L\); bit rate = items per second × bits per item; bit length = \(s \times (1/R)\).

Substitution

(a) \(\log_2 8\). (b) \(100 \times 24 \times 80 \times 8 / 60\). (c) \(2 \times 4{,}000 \times 8\). (d) \(1920 \times 1080 \times 30 \times 24\). (e) \(2 \times 10^8 \times 10^{-6}\).

Calculation

(a) 3 bits. (b) \(100 \times 24 \times 80 \times 8 = 1{,}536{,}000\) bits per minute = 25,600 bps. (c) 64,000 bps = 64 kbps. (d) 1,492,992,000 bps ≈ 1.5 Gbps (TV stations compress this to 20–40 Mbps). (e) 200 m.

Final answer

3 bits; 25.6 kbps; 64 kbps; ≈ 1.5 Gbps; each bit occupies 200 m of cable.

A slip in the textbook

Forouzan's printed solution to the pages example (Example 3.18) gives “100 × 24 × 80 × 8 = 1,636,000 bps = 1.636 Mbps”. The product is actually 1,536,000, and because the rate is 100 pages per minute, the required bit rate is 1,536,000 / 60 = 25.6 kbps. (1.536 Mbps would be the rate for 100 pages per second.) If this appears in an exam, show your working and state the time unit.

A digital signal is a composite analog signal

A digital signal, with its sudden vertical jumps, is a composite analog signal with an infinite bandwidth (a perfect square wave needs infinitely many harmonics). So how can it be sent over a real channel? Two approaches:

Baseband transmissionBroadband transmission (modulation)
Send the digital signal as is, without converting it to analogModulate the digital data onto a carrier: convert it to an analog signal
Needs a low-pass channel (bandwidth starting from 0)Uses a band-pass channel (a range of frequencies not starting at 0)
A dedicated medium for the whole channel, e.g. a LAN cable between two computersLets one medium carry many channels, e.g. DSL, cable modems, radio
A rough approximation: to send \(N\) bps, the channel needs a bandwidth of at least about \(N/2\) Hz (first harmonic); better quality needs \(3N/2\), \(5N/2\), …Bandwidth needed depends on the modulation

Example: to send 1 Mbps by baseband using only the first harmonic, the channel needs at least 500 kHz; using the first and third harmonics, 1.5 MHz.

Exam focus
  • Define amplitude, frequency, period, phase and wavelength; time vs frequency domain; bandwidth.
  • Digital signal levels and \(\log_2 L\); bit rate and bit length numericals.
  • Baseband vs broadband; why a digital signal has infinite bandwidth.
Quick revision
  • Analog = continuous; digital = discrete levels. Periodic vs nonperiodic.
  • Sine wave: peak amplitude, frequency (\(f = 1/T\)), phase; \(\lambda = c/f\).
  • Composite signals = sums of sines; bandwidth = \(f_h - f_l\).
  • Digital: bits per level \(\log_2 L\); bit rate (bps); bit length = speed × bit duration.
  • Baseband (low-pass, needs ≈ N/2 Hz) vs broadband (modulated, band-pass).

III.10 · Physical layer

Data communication and line coding

The components and data-flow modes of data communication, then Forouzan's digital-to-digital conversion: line-coding characteristics (signal rate, baseline wandering, DC, self-synchronisation), every line-coding scheme with drawings, block coding (4B/5B) and scrambling (B8ZS, HDB3).

Sources: Forouzan §1.1 and §4.1 (prescribed) · PhysicalLayer-DigitalTransmission ppt

Data communication

Data communication is the exchange of data between two devices via some form of transmission medium. Its effectiveness depends on four characteristics: delivery (to the correct destination), accuracy (data unaltered), timeliness (delivered in time; for video and audio, as produced) and jitter (the variation in packet arrival time, which should be low).

The five components

ComponentMeaningExample: a WhatsApp “Hi”
MessageThe information to be communicated: text, numbers, images, audio, videoThe bits for “Hi”
SenderThe device that sends the messageYour phone
ReceiverThe device that receives itYour friend's phone
Transmission mediumThe physical path the message travelsWi-Fi radio, fibre, 4G radio
ProtocolThe set of rules that govern the communicationTCP/IP and WhatsApp's protocol

Data flow: simplex, half-duplex, full-duplex

SimplexHalf-duplexFull-duplex
DirectionsOne onlyBoth, one at a timeBoth simultaneously
Channel capacityAll in one directionAll to whichever device is sendingShared: two physical paths, or capacity divided between directions
ExamplesKeyboard → computer, TV broadcastWalkie-talkie, hub-based Ethernet, Wi-FiTelephone call, switched full-duplex Ethernet
From a paperHalf-duplex vs full-duplex 19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q1(a)
Given

A 100 Mbps link; A must send 50 MB to B and B must send 50 MB to A (1 MB = 10⁶ bytes).

Required

The total time in half-duplex and in full-duplex mode, to show the difference with numbers.

Formula

Time = L/R per direction; half-duplex adds the directions; full-duplex overlaps them.

Substitution

\(L = 50 \times 10^6 \times 8 = 4 \times 10^8\) bits; \(L/R = 4 \times 10^8 / 10^8\).

Calculation

One direction = 4 s. Half-duplex = 4 + 4 = 8 s. Full-duplex = 4 s.

Final answer

In half-duplex both stations can send but not at the same time, so the transfers take 8 s; in full-duplex both send simultaneously, finishing in 4 s.

Line coding: digital-to-digital conversion

Line coding is the process of converting digital data to digital signals. Data (text, numbers, images, audio, video) stored in a computer as sequences of bits is converted at the sender into a digital signal; at the receiver the digital data is recreated by decoding the signal.

Data elements, signal elements and rates

  • A data element is the smallest entity that can represent information: a bit. A signal element is the shortest unit (timewise) of a digital signal. Data elements are what we need to send; signal elements are what we can send.
  • The ratio \(r\) is the number of data elements carried by each signal element.
  • The data rate \(N\) (bit rate) is the number of data elements sent in 1 s (bps). The signal rate \(S\) (pulse rate, modulation rate or baud rate) is the number of signal elements sent in 1 s (baud). We want to increase the data rate while decreasing the signal rate (which decreases the bandwidth needed).
Baud rate and bandwidth (Forouzan)

\[ S_{avg} = c \times N \times \frac{1}{r} \ \text{baud} \qquad B_{min} = c \times N \times \frac{1}{r} \qquad N_{max} = \frac{1}{c} \times B \times r \]

\(c\) is the case factor (worst, best or average case; the average is usually taken as \(c = 1/2\)). The bandwidth (range of frequencies) is proportional to the signal rate.

Level 1Baud rate (Forouzan Example 4.1)
Given

A signal carries data in which one data element is encoded as one signal element (r = 1); bit rate N = 100 kbps; c = 1/2.

Required

The average baud rate.

Formula

\(S = c \times N \times 1/r\).

Substitution

\(S = \tfrac{1}{2} \times 100{,}000 \times 1\).

Calculation

\(S = 50{,}000\).

Final answer

50 kbaud. (With r = 2, i.e. 2 bits per signal element, it would be 25 kbaud.)

What a good line code needs

ConcernProblemWhat a good scheme does
Baseline wanderingThe receiver decodes by comparing against a running average of the received power (the baseline). A long run of 0s or 1s shifts the baseline and makes decoding hardKeeps the average steady
DC componentsWhen the voltage stays constant for a while, very low frequencies (near zero) appear; channels that can't pass them (transformers, phone lines) distort the signal, and they waste energyHas no DC component (equal positive and negative energy)
Self-synchronisationThe receiver's bit intervals must match the sender's. Without transitions, the receiver's clock drifts and it misreads bitsIncludes timing information (transitions) in the signal itself
Built-in error detection—Some codes detect errors (e.g. AMI violations)
Immunity to noise—Levels well separated
ComplexityMore levels or rules cost moreSimple where possible

Line-coding schemes

From a paperNRZ-L, NRZ-I and Manchester for 0010111101000010 23CSE302 End 2025 Q1

The figure below draws the paper's 16-bit stream with Forouzan's conventions: NRZ-L: 0 = positive level, 1 = negative level. NRZ-I: a 1 means invert the level at the start of the bit; a 0 means no change (starting level assumed positive). Manchester (IEEE 802.3, as in Forouzan): 0 = high-to-low in the middle of the bit, 1 = low-to-high.

Some textbooks draw NRZ-L with 1 = high, or Manchester with the opposite convention (G. E. Thomas). Examiners accept either if you state your convention in the answer. The paper also asked for Fast Ethernet's encoding: 4B/5B with MLT-3 (100BASE-TX), see below.

Interactive visualizationLine-coding studioType any bit stream and see unipolar, NRZ-L, NRZ-I, RZ, Manchester, differential Manchester, AMI, pseudoternary and MLT-3 drawn bit by bit, with the conventions switchable. Also Nyquist, Shannon and decibel calculators.
Open simulator →

Unipolar scheme

All signal levels are on one side of the time axis. Unipolar NRZ (non-return-to-zero): 1 = positive voltage, 0 = zero voltage; the signal doesn't return to zero in the middle of the bit. It is costly: its normalised power (power needed per unit line resistance) is double that of polar NRZ. Normally not used in data communications today.

Polar schemes

The voltages are on both sides of the time axis.

  • NRZ-L (NRZ-Level): the level of the voltage determines the bit value (Forouzan: 0 positive, 1 negative).
  • NRZ-I (NRZ-Invert): the change or lack of change in level determines the bit: if there is no change, the bit is 0; if there is a change (inversion), the bit is 1.

Both NRZ schemes have average signal rate N/2 baud. Both have a DC component problem. NRZ-L suffers baseline wandering and loss of synchronisation for long runs of 0s or 1s; NRZ-I only for long runs of 0s (a run of 1s produces transitions). A sudden change of polarity (e.g. twisted-pair wires swapped) inverts all NRZ-L bits, but NRZ-I is immune to it.

  • RZ (return-to-zero): uses three values (positive, negative, zero). The signal changes in the middle of the bit to zero and stays there until the next bit: 1 = positive then zero, 0 = negative then zero. It provides synchronisation, but needs two signal changes per bit, so more bandwidth, and three levels are more complex. Not used today; replaced by Manchester.

Biphase: Manchester and differential Manchester

  • Manchester combines RZ (transition in the middle of the bit) and NRZ-L: the bit duration is divided into two halves; the voltage stays at one level in the first half and moves to the other in the second. The transition in the middle of every bit provides synchronisation.
  • Differential Manchester combines RZ and NRZ-I: there is always a transition in the middle of the bit, but the bit value is determined at the beginning of the bit: if the next bit is 0, there is a transition at the start; if it is 1, there is none.

Both overcome NRZ-L's and NRZ-I's problems: no baseline wandering, no DC component, and self-synchronising. The cost: the minimum bandwidth is twice that of NRZ (signal rate N baud). Manchester is used in 10 Mbps Ethernet (10BASE-T); differential Manchester in token ring LANs.

Bipolar (multilevel binary) schemes

Three voltage levels: positive, negative and zero.

  • AMI (alternate mark inversion): binary 0 = zero voltage; binary 1 = alternating positive and negative voltages.
  • Pseudoternary: the reverse: 1 = zero voltage, 0 = alternating positive and negative.

Bipolar coding has the same signal rate as NRZ (N/2) but no DC component (positive and negative 1s cancel). A long run of 0s in AMI still loses synchronisation, which scrambling fixes. A violation of the alternation rule indicates an error, giving simple error detection.

Multilevel schemes

These increase the number of bits per baud by encoding a pattern of \(m\) data elements into a pattern of \(n\) signal elements with \(L\) levels (mBnL coding; B = binary, T = ternary, Q = quaternary).

  • 2B1Q (two binary, one quaternary): 2 bits per signal element, four levels; signal rate N/4. Used in DSL technology.
  • 8B6T (eight binary, six ternary): 8 bits encoded as 6 ternary signal elements; used in 100BASE-4T.
  • 4D-PAM5 (four-dimensional five-level pulse amplitude modulation): used in Gigabit Ethernet over four twisted pairs.

Multitransition: MLT-3

MLT-3 (multiline transmission, three level) uses three levels (+V, 0, −V) and three rules: if the next bit is 0, there is no transition; if the next bit is 1 and the current level is not 0, the next level is 0; if the next bit is 1 and the current level is 0, the next level is the opposite of the last nonzero level. In effect, each 1 moves the signal one step around the cycle 0 → +V → 0 → −V → 0. Its signal rate is about N/3, so it suits 100 Mbps on copper together with 4B/5B (100BASE-TX).

Summary of line-coding schemes (Forouzan Table 4.1)

CategorySchemeBandwidth (average)Characteristics
UnipolarNRZB = N/2Costly, no self-synchronisation for long 0s or 1s, DC
PolarNRZ-LB = N/2No self-synchronisation for long 0s or 1s, DC
PolarNRZ-IB = N/2No self-synchronisation for long 0s, DC
PolarBiphase (Manchester, differential Manchester)B = NSelf-synchronisation, no DC, high bandwidth
BipolarAMIB = N/2No self-synchronisation for long 0s, DC-free
Multilevel2B1QB = N/4No self-synchronisation for long runs of the same double bits
Multilevel8B6TB = 3N/4Self-synchronisation, no DC
Multilevel4D-PAM5B = N/8Self-synchronisation, no DC
MultilineMLT-3B = N/3No self-synchronisation for long 0s

Block coding: 4B/5B

We need redundancy to ensure synchronisation and to detect errors. Block coding changes a block of \(m\) bits into a block of \(n\) bits (\(n > m\)): mB/nB. It is done in three steps: divide the stream into m-bit groups, substitute each m-bit group with an n-bit group from a table, and line-code the result.

4B/5B is designed to be used with NRZ-I. NRZ-I has good signal rate but loses synchronisation on long runs of 0s. 4B/5B replaces each 4-bit group with a 5-bit code chosen so that no code has more than one leading 0 and no more than two trailing 0s; so in any sequence of codes there are never more than three consecutive 0s, and NRZ-I stays synchronised. The cost: 5 bits are sent for every 4, so the signal rate rises by 25%: 100 Mbps of data needs 125 Mbps (125 Mbaud with NRZ-I). Unused 5-bit codes serve as control codes and error detection.

Data (4B)Code (5B)DataCode
000011110100010010
000101001100110011
001010100101010110
001110101101110111
010001010110011010
010101011110111011
011001110111011100
011101111111111101

Scrambling: B8ZS and HDB3

Biphase schemes are unsuitable for long-distance links because of their wide bandwidth, and AMI loses synchronisation on long runs of 0s. Scrambling modifies AMI without increasing the bit count, substituting long zero-level pulses with a combination of other levels during encoding:

  • B8ZS (bipolar with 8-zero substitution), used in North America: eight consecutive zero-level voltages are replaced by 000VB0VB, where V is a violation (a nonzero voltage that breaks the AMI rule: the same polarity as the previous nonzero pulse) and B is a normal bipolar pulse (following the AMI rule). The receiver recognises the violation pattern and restores the 8 zeros.
  • HDB3 (high-density bipolar 3-zero), used outside North America: four consecutive zeros are replaced by 000V or B00V: if the number of nonzero pulses since the last substitution is odd, use 000V; if even, use B00V. This keeps the number of positive and negative pulses balanced (no DC).
Exam focus
  • 23CSE302 End 2025 Q1 (your own course): draw NRZ, NRZ-I and Manchester for a given bit stream and name the encoding for Fast Ethernet. State your conventions, mark bit boundaries, and label voltage levels.
  • Know which schemes are self-synchronising and DC-free, and each scheme's bandwidth (Table 4.1).
  • Signal rate vs data rate and the formula \(S = c \times N / r\).
  • The five components and the three data-flow modes (2-mark definitions; 19CSE301 Mid 2023 Q1(a)).
Common mistakes
  • In NRZ-I, encoding a 1 as “high”. A 1 means change; a 0 means no change.
  • In Manchester, forgetting the transition in the middle of every bit.
  • In differential Manchester, putting the extra transition at the start for a 1 instead of a 0.
  • Not stating the assumed starting level for NRZ-I, differential Manchester and AMI.
Quick revision
  • Data communication: 5 components; delivery, accuracy, timeliness, jitter; simplex, half-duplex, full-duplex.
  • Data element vs signal element; \(S = c \cdot N / r\) baud; bandwidth ∝ signal rate.
  • Concerns: baseline wandering, DC, self-synchronisation, error detection, noise immunity, complexity.
  • NRZ-L (level), NRZ-I (1 = invert), RZ, Manchester (mid-bit transition; Ethernet), differential Manchester (0 = transition at start; token ring), AMI (1s alternate), pseudoternary, 2B1Q, 8B6T, 4D-PAM5, MLT-3.
  • 4B/5B (≤ 3 consecutive 0s, +25% rate); B8ZS (000VB0VB), HDB3 (000V / B00V).

III.11 · Physical layer

Transmission media and their characteristics

Guided media (twisted pair, coaxial cable, optical fibre) and unguided media (radio, microwave, infrared, satellite): how each works, its characteristics, and how they compare.

Sources: Kurose §1.2.2 (prescribed) · Chapter_1_v8.2 slides 24–25 · background from Forouzan Ch 7

Guided and unguided media

A bit travelling from one end system to another passes through a series of transmitter–receiver pairs. For each pair, the bit is sent as electromagnetic waves or optical pulses across a physical medium.

  • Guided media: the waves are guided along a solid medium: twisted-pair copper, coaxial cable, optical fibre.
  • Unguided media: the waves propagate in the atmosphere and in outer space: wireless LANs, cellular, satellite channels.

The actual cost of a physical link (copper wire, fibre) is often small compared with other networking costs; the labour cost of installation can be orders of magnitude higher, so builders often install twisted pair, fibre and coax in every room of a new building.

Twisted-pair copper wire

The least expensive and most commonly used guided medium, used for over a hundred years by telephone networks (more than 99% of the wired connections from a telephone handset to the local switch).

  • Two insulated copper wires, each about 1 mm thick, arranged in a regular spiral. The wires are twisted together to reduce electrical interference from similar pairs close by (crosstalk) and external noise: the twisting makes both wires pick up roughly the same noise, which the receiver cancels.
  • A number of pairs are bundled in a cable, wrapped in a protective shield; each pair is a single communication link.
  • Unshielded twisted pair (UTP) is common for LANs. Data rates range from 10 Mbps to 10 Gbps, depending on the wire thickness and the distance. Category 6a cable reaches 10 Gbps at distances up to a hundred metres.
  • Twisted pair is also used for residential Internet access (DSL) over the existing telephone wiring.

Coaxial cable

  • Two copper conductors that are concentric rather than parallel: an inner conductor, an insulating layer, an outer conductor (braided shield) and a plastic jacket.
  • With this construction and special insulation, coax can achieve high data rates, and it is common in cable television systems.
  • Coax can be used as a guided shared medium: many end systems can be connected directly to the cable, each receiving whatever is sent by the others (the cable modem system).

Optical fibre

  • A thin, flexible medium that conducts pulses of light, each pulse representing a bit. A single fibre can support enormous bit rates, tens or even hundreds of Gbps.
  • Light stays inside the glass core because of total internal reflection at the boundary with the cladding, which has a lower refractive index.
  • Immune to electromagnetic interference, has very low signal attenuation up to 100 km, and is very hard to tap. These characteristics make fibre the preferred long-haul guided medium, particularly for overseas links and the backbone of the Internet.
  • The high cost of optical devices (transmitters, receivers, switches) has hindered deployment for short-haul transport such as LANs or residential access.
  • Types (Forouzan): multimode fibre (many light beams bounce along different paths; step-index or graded-index; cheaper, shorter distances) and single-mode fibre (a very thin core, light travels almost straight; highest bandwidth over the longest distances).

Terrestrial radio channels

Radio channels carry signals in the electromagnetic spectrum. They need no physical wire, can penetrate walls, provide connectivity to a mobile user, and can carry signals over long distances. Their characteristics depend on the propagation environment and the distance:

  • Path loss and shadow fading (signal strength decreases with distance and around or through obstructing objects);
  • Multipath fading (the signal reflects off interfering objects and arrives by several paths);
  • Interference from other transmissions and electromagnetic signals.

Three groups: very short distance (a metre or two: wireless headsets, keyboards), local area (ten to a few hundred metres: Wi-Fi), and wide area (tens of kilometres: cellular access).

Forouzan's frequency-based classification:

KindFrequenciesPropertiesUses
Radio waves3 kHz – 1 GHzMostly omnidirectional; penetrate walls; long range (low frequencies)AM/FM radio, TV, cordless phones
Microwaves1 – 300 GHzUnidirectional; need line of sight; higher frequencies can't penetrate wallsCellular, satellite, wireless LANs, point-to-point links
Infrared300 GHz – 400 THzShort range; can't penetrate walls (no interference between rooms)Remote controls, short-range device links

Satellite radio channels

A communication satellite links two or more ground stations (microwave transmitter/receivers). It receives on one frequency band, regenerates the signal with a repeater, and transmits on another.

  • Geostationary (GEO) satellites stay permanently above the same spot on Earth, at 36,000 km. That distance introduces a substantial signal propagation delay of about 280 ms. They can still operate at hundreds of Mbps, and are used where there's no wired access.
  • Low-earth orbiting (LEO) satellites are much closer to Earth and don't stay above one spot; they rotate around Earth and communicate with each other and with ground stations. Providing continuous coverage needs many of them (satellite constellations).

Comparing the media

CharacteristicTwisted pairCoaxial cableOptical fibreTerrestrial radioSatellite (GEO)
SignalElectricalElectricalLightElectromagnetic wavesMicrowaves
Bandwidth / data rateLow to medium (up to 10 Gbps short)Medium to highVery high (100s Gbps)MediumMedium to high
AttenuationHigh; rises with frequencyModerateVery low (repeaters ~50 km+)Path loss with distanceVery long path
Interference / noiseSusceptible (EMI, crosstalk)Shield reduces itImmune to EMIInterference, fadingWeather affects some bands
Security (tapping)Easy to tapModerateVery hard to tapEasy to interceptEasy to intercept
Distance~100 m (LAN)Hundreds of m to kmUp to 100 km without amplificationm to tens of kmGlobal
CostLowestMediumCable cheap, optical devices expensiveNo cableVery high

Exam focus
  • Guided vs unguided media with examples, and why twisted pair is twisted.
  • The advantages and disadvantages of optical fibre.
  • Satellite delay (GEO ≈ 280 ms) and LEO.
  • 19CSE301 Mid 2024 Q1(e): the medium connecting an Ethernet switch to end systems is twisted-pair copper (UTP).
Quick revision
  • Guided: twisted pair (cheap, twisted against interference, up to 10 Gbps over 100 m), coax (concentric, shared, cable TV), fibre (light, huge rates, low attenuation, EMI-immune, hard to tap).
  • Unguided: terrestrial radio (path loss, fading, interference; short/local/wide area), satellite (GEO 36,000 km ≈ 280 ms; LEO).
  • Installation labour usually costs more than the cable.

III.12 · Physical layer

Signal and noise: transmission impairment

Why the signal at the end of a medium is not the signal that was sent: the three impairments (attenuation, distortion, noise), the kinds of noise, and how impairments limit performance.

Sources: Forouzan §3.4 (prescribed)

Why impairment matters

Signals travel through transmission media, which are not perfect. The imperfection causes signal impairment: the signal at the beginning of the medium is not the same as the signal at the end. What is sent is not what is received. There are three causes: attenuation, distortion and noise.

Attenuation

Attenuation means a loss of energy. When a signal travels through a medium, it loses some energy overcoming the resistance of the medium: that is why a wire carrying an electric signal gets warm, if not hot, after a while. Some of the electrical energy is converted to heat. To compensate, amplifiers are used to boost the signal. Attenuation is measured in decibels (Topic III.13).

Distortion

Distortion means the signal changes its form or shape. It can occur in a composite signal made of different frequencies. Each component has its own propagation speed through a medium and therefore its own delay in arriving at the final destination. Differences in delay may create a difference in phase if the delay is not exactly the same as the period. The signal components at the receiver therefore have phases different from what they had at the sender, and the shape of the composite signal is no longer the same.

Noise

Noise is another cause of impairment: unwanted energy added to the signal. Several types of noise may corrupt a signal:

TypeCause
Thermal noiseThe random motion of electrons in a wire, which creates an extra signal not originally sent by the transmitter
Induced noiseComes from sources such as motors and appliances, which act as sending antennas while the medium acts as the receiving antenna
CrosstalkThe effect of one wire on another: one wire acts as a sending antenna and the other as the receiving antenna
Impulse noiseA spike (a signal with high energy in a very short time) from power lines, lightning and so on

The effect of noise is measured by the signal-to-noise ratio (Topic III.14). Noise is what makes the receiver occasionally decide a 1 was a 0, which is why the link layer adds error detection (III.2).

Digital signals tolerate noise better

An analog signal that has picked up noise can't be cleaned: the noise is now part of the waveform, and an amplifier boosts it along with the signal. A digital signal can be regenerated by a repeater: as long as the receiver can still tell which level each bit was meant to be, it re-creates a perfect new signal. This is a key reason for digital transmission.

Exam focus
  • Name and explain attenuation, distortion and noise, with the four kinds of noise. A common 5-mark question.
  • Why amplifiers fix attenuation but not noise; why digital regeneration helps.
Quick revision
  • Impairments: attenuation (energy loss, amplifiers, dB), distortion (frequency components arrive with different delays/phases), noise (thermal, induced, crosstalk, impulse).
  • Noise is measured by SNR; digital signals can be regenerated.

III.13 · Physical layer

Attenuation and decibels

Measuring signal loss and gain with decibels, adding losses along a path, dBm, and voltage ratios, with step-by-step numericals.

Sources: Forouzan §3.4 (prescribed)

The decibel

To show that a signal has lost or gained strength, engineers use the decibel (dB). The decibel measures the relative strengths of two signals, or of one signal at two different points.

Decibels

\[ \text{dB} = 10 \log_{10} \frac{P_2}{P_1} \]

\(P_1\) and \(P_2\) are the powers of a signal at points 1 and 2. A negative value means the signal is attenuated (loss); a positive value means it is amplified (gain). In terms of voltage (since power ∝ V²): \[ \text{dB} = 20 \log_{10} \frac{V_2}{V_1} \]

Why decibels? Because they add: when a signal passes through several stages, the overall gain or loss is just the sum of the decibel values of the stages, instead of a product of ratios.

Power ratio \(P_2/P_1\)dB
1/2 (half the power)−3 dB
1/10−10 dB
2 (double)+3 dB
10+10 dB
100+20 dB
1,000+30 dB

dBm: absolute power

Sometimes decibels measure signal power relative to 1 milliwatt; the unit is dBm:

\[ \text{dB}_m = 10 \log_{10} P_m \]

where \(P_m\) is the power in milliwatts. 1 mW = 0 dBm; 10 mW = 10 dBm; 1 µW = −30 dBm.

Worked numericals

Level 1Half the power (Forouzan Example 3.26)
Given

A signal travels through a medium and its power is reduced to one half: \(P_2 = 0.5 P_1\).

Required

The attenuation in dB.

Formula

\( \text{dB} = 10 \log_{10}(P_2/P_1) \).

Substitution

\( 10 \log_{10}(0.5\,P_1 / P_1) = 10 \log_{10} 0.5 \).

Calculation

\( \log_{10} 0.5 = -0.301 \); \( 10 \times (-0.301) = -3.01 \).

Final answer

About −3 dB: losing half the power is a 3 dB loss.

Level 2An amplifier, then a chain of stages (Forouzan Examples 3.27–3.28)
Given

(a) An amplifier increases the power 10 times: \(P_2 = 10 P_1\). (b) A signal passes from point 1 to point 4 through: a cable (−3 dB), an amplifier (+7 dB), another cable (−3 dB).

Required

(a) the gain in dB; (b) the overall change.

Formula

dB = 10 log₁₀(P₂/P₁); decibels add along a chain.

Substitution

(a) \(10 \log_{10} 10\). (b) \(-3 + 7 - 3\).

Calculation

(a) \(10 \times 1 = 10\) dB. (b) \(+1\) dB.

Final answer

(a) +10 dB. (b) +1 dB: the signal ends up slightly stronger than it started.

Level 3Loss per kilometre and output power (Forouzan Example 3.30)
Given

A cable loses 0.3 dB/km. A 2 mW signal enters a 5 km length.

Required

The output power, in mW and in dBm.

Formula

Total dB = loss/km × length; \(P_2 = P_1 \times 10^{\text{dB}/10}\); dBm = \(10 \log_{10} P_{mW}\).

Substitution

dB = \(-0.3 \times 5 = -1.5\). \(P_2 = 2 \times 10^{-0.15}\).

Calculation

\(10^{-0.15} = 0.708\); \(P_2 = 1.416\) mW. dBm \(= 10 \log_{10} 1.416 = 1.51\) dBm. (Check: input 2 mW = 3.01 dBm; 3.01 − 1.5 = 1.51 dBm ✓.)

Final answer

About 1.42 mW, i.e. 1.51 dBm.

Level 3Using dBm and voltage ratios (Forouzan Example 3.29 and practice)
Given

(a) The power of a signal is −30 dBm. (b) A voltage drops from 10 V to 5 V.

Required

(a) the power in watts; (b) the change in dB.

Formula

\(P_m = 10^{\text{dB}_m/10}\) mW; voltage dB \(= 20 \log_{10}(V_2/V_1)\).

Substitution

(a) \(10^{-30/10} = 10^{-3}\) mW. (b) \(20 \log_{10}(5/10)\).

Calculation

(a) \(10^{-3}\) mW = \(10^{-6}\) W. (b) \(20 \times (-0.301) = -6.02\) dB.

Final answer

(a) 1 µW. (b) About −6 dB (halving the voltage quarters the power).

Exam focus
  • Use dB = 10 log₁₀(P₂/P₁) for power, 20 log₁₀(V₂/V₁) for voltage; negative = loss.
  • Add decibels along a path.
  • Convert between mW and dBm.
Common mistakes
  • Putting the larger power on top regardless of direction: always \(P_2\) (later point) over \(P_1\) (earlier point), so loss comes out negative.
  • Using 20 log for power ratios (that is for voltage).
  • Multiplying dB values along a chain instead of adding them.
Quick revision
  • Attenuation = energy loss; amplifiers compensate.
  • dB = 10 log₁₀(P₂/P₁); −3 dB = half; +10 dB = ×10; voltages: 20 log₁₀.
  • dB values add along a chain; dBm is relative to 1 mW.

III.14 · Physical layer

Signal-to-noise ratio and data-rate limits

Measuring noise with SNR (as a ratio and in dB), then the two limits on how fast data can go: Nyquist's bit rate for a noiseless channel and Shannon's capacity for a noisy one, and how to use them together.

Sources: Forouzan §3.4–3.5 (prescribed)

Signal-to-noise ratio (SNR)

To find the theoretical bit-rate limit we need the ratio of the signal power to the noise power. The signal-to-noise ratio is

SNR

\[ \text{SNR} = \frac{\text{average signal power}}{\text{average noise power}} \qquad \text{SNR}_{dB} = 10 \log_{10} \text{SNR} \]

SNR is the ratio of what is wanted (signal) to what is not wanted (noise). A high SNR means the signal is less corrupted by noise; a low SNR means it is more corrupted. Because SNR is a ratio of two powers, it is often given in decibels.

Level 1SNR from powers (Forouzan Examples 3.31–3.32)
Given

(a) Signal power 10 mW, noise power 1 µW. (b) A noiseless channel.

Required

SNR and SNR_dB.

Formula

SNR = P_signal / P_noise; SNR_dB = 10 log₁₀ SNR.

Substitution

(a) \(10{,}000\ \mu\text{W} / 1\ \mu\text{W}\). (b) Noise power = 0.

Calculation

(a) SNR = 10,000; SNR_dB = \(10 \log_{10} 10^4 = 40\) dB. (b) SNR = signal/0 = ∞.

Final answer

(a) 10,000, i.e. 40 dB. (b) Infinite (we can never achieve this in practice).

Data-rate limits

A very important consideration is how fast we can send data, in bits per second, over a channel. The data rate depends on three factors:

  1. the bandwidth available,
  2. the number of signal levels we use, and
  3. the quality of the channel (the level of noise).

Two theoretical formulas: Nyquist for a noiseless channel and Shannon for a noisy channel.

Noiseless channel: the Nyquist bit rate

Nyquist bit rate

\[ \text{BitRate} = 2 \times B \times \log_2 L \]

\(B\) = bandwidth of the channel (Hz), \(L\) = number of signal levels used to represent data, bit rate in bps.

Given a specific bandwidth, we can have any bit rate we want by increasing the number of levels? In theory, yes; in practice, no: more levels make it harder for the receiver to distinguish them, which increases the error probability, especially when the channel is not perfect.

Noisy channel: the Shannon capacity

In reality we can't have a noiseless channel. In 1944, Claude Shannon introduced a formula, the Shannon capacity, for the highest data rate of a noisy channel:

Shannon capacity

\[ C = B \times \log_2(1 + \text{SNR}) \]

\(B\) = bandwidth (Hz), SNR = signal-to-noise ratio (as a ratio, not dB), \(C\) = capacity (bps). For high SNR, \(\text{SNR} \gg 1\), a useful approximation is \( C \approx B \times \dfrac{\text{SNR}_{dB}}{3} \).

The Shannon formula has no number of levels in it: no matter how many levels we use, we cannot achieve a data rate higher than the capacity of the channel. The formula defines a characteristic of the channel, not of the transmission method.

Using both limits

In practice we need both: Shannon gives the upper limit; Nyquist tells us how many signal levels we need.

Worked numericals

Level 1Nyquist (Forouzan Examples 3.34–3.35)
Given

(a) A noiseless channel of bandwidth 3,000 Hz with two signal levels. (b) The same channel with four levels.

Required

The maximum bit rates.

Formula

BitRate = 2 × B × log₂ L.

Substitution

(a) \(2 \times 3{,}000 \times \log_2 2\). (b) \(2 \times 3{,}000 \times \log_2 4\).

Calculation

(a) \(6{,}000 \times 1 = 6{,}000\) bps. (b) \(6{,}000 \times 2 = 12{,}000\) bps.

Final answer

(a) 6 kbps. (b) 12 kbps: doubling the levels' bits doubles the rate.

Level 2How many levels? (Forouzan Example 3.36)
Given

We need to send 265 kbps over a noiseless channel with a bandwidth of 20 kHz.

Required

The number of signal levels.

Formula

\(265{,}000 = 2 \times 20{,}000 \times \log_2 L\).

Substitution

\(\log_2 L = 265{,}000 / 40{,}000\).

Calculation

\(\log_2 L = 6.625\); \(L = 2^{6.625} = 98.7\) levels.

Final answer

98.7 is not a power of 2, so we either use 128 levels (giving 280 kbps) or 64 levels (giving 240 kbps).

Level 2Shannon (Forouzan Examples 3.37–3.39)
Given

(a) An extremely noisy channel with SNR ≈ 0. (b) A regular telephone line: bandwidth 3,000 Hz (300 to 3,300 Hz), SNR = 3,162. (c) A channel with SNR_dB = 36 and bandwidth 2 MHz.

Required

The capacities.

Formula

\(C = B \log_2(1 + \text{SNR})\); convert dB first: SNR = \(10^{\text{SNR}_{dB}/10}\).

Substitution

(a) \(B \log_2 1\). (b) \(3{,}000 \log_2 3{,}163\). (c) SNR = \(10^{3.6} = 3{,}981\); \(2 \times 10^6 \log_2 3{,}982\).

Calculation

(a) \(\log_2 1 = 0\) → C = 0. (b) \(\log_2 3{,}163 = 11.62\); \(C = 34{,}860\) bps. (c) \(\log_2 3{,}982 = 11.96\); \(C = 23.92\) Mbps ≈ 24 Mbps (the approximation \(2 \times 36/3 = 24\) Mbps agrees).

Final answer

(a) 0: we can't receive any data through such a channel, whatever the bandwidth. (b) ≈ 34.86 kbps: to send faster over a phone line we must either increase the bandwidth or improve the SNR. (c) ≈ 24 Mbps.

Level 3Shannon then Nyquist (Forouzan Example 3.41)
Given

A channel with a 1 MHz bandwidth and SNR = 63.

Required

An appropriate bit rate and the number of signal levels.

Formula

First Shannon for the upper limit, then Nyquist for the levels.

Substitution

\(C = 10^6 \log_2(1 + 63) = 10^6 \log_2 64\).

Calculation

\(C = 6\) Mbps (upper limit). For better performance choose something lower, say 4 Mbps. Nyquist: \(4 \times 10^6 = 2 \times 10^6 \times \log_2 L\) → \(\log_2 L = 2\) → \(L = 4\).

Final answer

Use 4 Mbps with 4 signal levels (Shannon's 6 Mbps is the theoretical ceiling).

PracticeCombining dB, Shannon and Nyquist
Given

A 4 kHz channel; signal power 1 W; noise power 1 mW.

Required

SNR (ratio and dB), the Shannon capacity, and the levels needed to reach about half that capacity with Nyquist.

Formula

SNR = P_s/P_n; dB = 10 log₁₀ SNR; \(C = B \log_2(1 + \text{SNR})\); \(L = 2^{R/2B}\).

Substitution

SNR = 1/0.001 = 1,000. C = \(4{,}000 \log_2 1{,}001\).

Calculation

SNR_dB = 30 dB. \(\log_2 1{,}001 = 9.97\); C ≈ 39.9 kbps. Half ≈ 20 kbps: \(\log_2 L = 20{,}000 / 8{,}000 = 2.5\) → L = 5.66 → use 8 levels (24 kbps) or 4 levels (16 kbps).

Final answer

SNR 1,000 (30 dB); C ≈ 39.9 kbps; 8 levels give 24 kbps (safely below capacity).

NyquistShannon
Noiseless channelNoisy channel
BitRate = 2B log₂ LC = B log₂(1 + SNR)
Depends on the number of levelsIndependent of levels; a property of the channel
More levels → higher rate (in theory)No method can exceed C
Tells you how many levels to useTells you the maximum rate possible
Exam focus
  • These formulas are on your syllabus even though no uploaded paper has asked them yet; expect a straightforward numerical.
  • Always convert SNR from dB to a ratio before using Shannon.
  • Use Shannon for the ceiling, then Nyquist for the levels.
Common mistakes
  • Plugging SNR in dB straight into \(\log_2(1 + \text{SNR})\).
  • Writing \(B \log_2 L\) for Nyquist (the factor 2 is essential).
  • Choosing a non-power-of-2 number of levels without rounding to a practical value.
Quick revision
  • SNR = signal power / noise power; SNR_dB = 10 log₁₀ SNR.
  • Nyquist (noiseless): BitRate = 2B log₂ L.
  • Shannon (noisy): C = B log₂(1 + SNR); approximately B × SNR_dB/3 for high SNR.
  • Shannon gives the ceiling; Nyquist gives the levels.

Important numericals

Every numerical type, with a step-by-step solver

Each solver below lays its answer out exactly as you should in the exam: Given → Required → Formula → Substitution → Calculation → Final answer. The default values are taken from your past papers or the textbook, so pressing “Solve step by step” first reproduces a known answer.

How to attack any numerical

1. List the givensWrite every value with its unit. Convert at once: bytes → bits (× 8), km → m, ms → s, Mbps → bps.
2. Name the targetWrite what is required, in which unit.
3. Pick the formulaWrite it symbolically before substituting. This alone earns marks.
4. SubstitutePut the numbers in, keeping units visible.
5. CalculateShow intermediate values (each link's delay, each partial sum, each table row).
6. Box the answerWith the unit, and one sentence of interpretation (“propagation dominates”, “packet switching is faster”).

Working offline
